## I / 一

===Original===

Faced with Reality

On that particular November evening, Viner, a young gentleman of means and leisure, who lived in a comfortable old house in Markendale Square, Bayswater, in company with his maiden aunt Miss Bethia Penkridge, had spent his after-dinner hours in a fashion which had become a habit. Miss Penkridge, a model housekeeper and an essentially worthy woman, whose whole day was given to supervising somebody or something, had an insatiable appetite for fiction, and loved nothing so much as that her nephew should read a novel to her after the two glasses of port which she allowed herself every night had been thoughtfully consumed and he and she had adjourned from the dining-room to the hearthrug in the library. Her tastes, however, in Viner’s opinion were somewhat, if not decidedly, limited. Brought up in her youth on Miss Braddon, Wilkie Collins and Mrs. Henry Wood, Miss Penkridge had become a confirmed slave to the sensational. She had no taste for the psychological, and nothing but scorn for the erotic. What she loved was a story which began with crime and ended with a detection﻿—a story which kept you wondering who did it, how it was done, and when the doing was going to be laid bare to the light of day. Nothing pleased her better than to go to bed with a brain titivated with the mysteries of the last three chapters; nothing gave her such infinite delight as to find, when the final pages were turned, that all her own theories were wrong, and that the real criminal was somebody quite other than the person she had fancied. For a novelist who was so little master of his trade as to let you see when and how things were going, Miss Penkridge had little but good-natured pity; for one who led you by all sorts of devious tracks to a startling and surprising sensation she cherished a whole-souled love; but for the creator of a plot who could keep his secret alive and burning to his last few sentences she felt the deepest thing that she could give to any human being﻿—respect. Such a master was entered permanently on her mental library list.

At precisely ten o’clock that evening Viner read the last page of a novel which had proved to be exactly suited to his aunt’s tastes. A dead silence fell on the room, broken only by the crackling of the logs in the grate. Miss Penkridge dropped her knitting on her silk-gowned knees and stared at the leaping flames; her nephew, with an odd glance at her, rose from his easy-chair, picked up a pipe and began to fill it from a tobacco-jar on the mantelpiece. The clock had ticked several times before Miss Penkridge spoke.

“Well!” she said, with the accompanying sigh which denotes complete content. “So he did it! Now, I should never have thought it! The last person of the whole lot! Clever﻿—very clever! Richard, you’ll get all the books that that man has written!”

===Chinese===

直面现实

十一月里的那个晚上，维纳（Viner）——一位有钱有闲的年轻绅士，住在贝斯沃特（Bayswater）马肯代尔广场（Markendale Square）一幢舒适的老宅里，同未婚的姨妈贝西娅·彭克里奇小姐（Miss Bethia Penkridge）作伴——又照着已成惯例的方式，消磨起晚餐后的时光。彭克里奇小姐是治家的模范、地道的善良女人，一整天工夫都花在监督这个、照管那个上，偏偏对小说怀有一副永不餍足的胃口；她最爱不过的，是每晚自许的两杯波特酒被细细体贴地享用完毕、甥舅二人从饭厅移步到书房的炉前地毯上之后，由外甥念一部小说给她听。不过照维纳看来，她的趣味即便说不上十分褊狭，也多少有些局限。彭克里奇小姐年轻时候是读着布雷登小姐（Miss Braddon）、威尔基·柯林斯（Wilkie Collins）和亨利·伍德夫人（Mrs. Henry Wood）长大的，早已成了惊险小说无可救药的奴隶。心理剖析她全无兴味，情爱笔墨她只有鄙夷。她所钟爱的，是那种以罪案开篇、以侦破收尾的故事——那种叫你一路悬着心猜想的故事：是谁干的？怎么干的？这桩勾当几时才会被揭到光天化日之下？再没有什么比揣着一脑袋末三章的疑团上床更叫她受用的了；也再没有什么比翻到最后一页时发现自己的一套推想全盘落空、真凶压根儿不是她相中的那个人，更叫她乐不可支的了。对于本领欠火候、早早让你看穿情节来龙去脉的小说家，彭克里奇小姐除去好心肠的怜悯，给不了什么；对于领着你七弯八绕、末了猛地给你一记惊雷的作家，她怀着一片赤诚的爱；至于那位能把秘密一直烧到全书最后几句才揭盅的布局圣手，她便献上她所能给人的最深的情意——敬意。这样的高手，是要被永久登录进她心头那册藏书单的。

那天晚上十点整，维纳念完了那部小说的最后一页——这部书恰好投合他姨妈的口味。房间里顿时一片死寂，只有炉栅里柴薪噼啪作响。彭克里奇小姐把针线活搁在绸裙覆着的双膝上，怔怔地望着跳动的火苗；她外甥异样地瞥了她一眼，从安乐椅里起身，拿起一支烟斗，凑着壁炉架上的烟草罐装起烟丝来。时钟嘀嗒响了好几下，彭克里奇小姐才开腔。

“好啊！”她说，还带着一声标示心满意足的叹息。“果真是他干的！啧，我怎么也想不到啊！满盘子人里偏偏最不像他！高明——实在高明！理查德（Richard），此人写的书，你全去给我弄来！”


===Original===

Viner lighted his pipe, thrust his hands in the pockets of his trousers and leaned back against the mantelpiece.

“My dear aunt!” he said half-teasingly, half-seriously. “You’re worse than a drug-taker. Whatever makes a highly-respectable, shrewd old lady like you cherish such an insensate fancy for this sort of stuff?”

“Stuff?” demanded Miss Penkridge, who had resumed her knitting. “Pooh! It’s not stuff﻿—it’s life! Real life﻿—in the form of fiction!”

Viner shook his head, pityingly. He never read fiction for his own amusement; his tastes in reading lay elsewhere, in solid directions. Moreover, in those directions he was a good deal of a student, and he knew more of his own library than of the world outside it. So he shook his head again.

===Chinese===

维纳点着烟斗，两手插进裤袋，身子往后靠在壁炉架上。

“亲爱的姨妈！”他半戏谑、半正经地说道。“你这瘾头比服麻醉药的还大。像你这么一位极体面、极精明的老太太，究竟是什么缘故，叫你对这种货色抱着这般没有节制的痴好？”

“货色？”彭克里奇小姐重新拿起针线，诘问道。“呸！这不是货色——这是生活！真实的生活——不过穿的是小说的衣裳！”

维纳怜悯地摇了摇头。他自己消遣是从不读小说的；他读书的趣味在别处，在庄重踏实的方向上。况且，在那几个方向上他还相当用功，他对自己那座书房的了解，比书房外头的世界还要多。于是他又摇了摇头。


===Original===

“Life!” he said. “You don’t mean to say that you think those things”﻿—he pointed a half-scornful finger to a pile of novels which had come in from Mudie’s that day﻿—“really represent life?”

“What else?” demanded Miss Penkridge.

“Oh﻿—I don’t know,” replied Viner vaguely. “Fancy, I suppose, and imagination, and all that sort of thing﻿—invention, you know, and so on. But﻿—life! Do you really think such things happen in real life, as those we’ve been reading about?”

“I don’t think anything about it,” retorted Miss Penkridge sturdily. “I’m sure of it. I never had a novel yet, nor heard one read to me, that was half as strong as it might have been!”

===Chinese===

“生活！”他说。“你总不会是说，你当真以为这些东西”——他带着几分轻蔑，指了指当天从穆迪租书店（Mudie’s）送来的一摞小说——“能够代表生活吧？”

“不叫它们代表生活，又叫它们代表什么？”彭克里奇小姐反诘。

“哦——我说不上来，”维纳含糊地答道。“大概是奇想吧，我猜，还有想象力，以及诸如此类的东西——就是说，虚构，你懂么，等等。可是——生活！你当真以为，像我们方才读到的这类事情，会在真实生活里发生么？”

“我倒不用‘以为’，”彭克里奇小姐理直气壮地回敬，“我是拿得稳的。到如今，不论我自己读的小说，还是听人念的小说，还没有一部写到了它本该有的那份力道的一半！”


===Original===

“Queer thing, one never hears or sees of these things, then!” exclaimed Viner. “I never have!﻿—and I’ve been on this planet thirty years.”

“That sort of thing hasn’t come your way, Richard,” remarked Miss Penkridge sententiously. “And you don’t read the popular Sunday newspapers. I do! They’re full of crime of all sorts. So’s the world. And as to mysteries﻿—well, I’ve known of two or three in my time that were much more extraordinary than any I’ve ever read of in novels. I should think so!”

Viner dropped into his easy-chair and stretched his legs.

“Such as﻿—what?” he asked.

===Chinese===

“怪就怪在，”维纳嚷道，“这种事情居然从来无人听说、无人眼见！我就从来没有！——我在这颗行星上可是住了三十年啦。”

“那一类事儿只是还没有轮到你头上，理查德，”彭克里奇小姐说得像格言似的。“再说，那些风行的星期日报纸你又不看。我看！那种报纸上满纸都是形形色色的罪案。这世界也一样。至于离奇事儿——嘿，我这一辈子里亲身知道的就有那么两三桩，比我历来在小说里读到的任何一桩都离奇得多。可不是么！”

维纳一屁股跌进安乐椅，伸直了两腿。

“比如说——什么呢？”他问。


===Original===

“Well,” answered Miss Penkridge, regarding her knitting with appraising eyes, “there was a case that excited great interest when your poor mother and I were mere girls. It was in our town﻿—young Quainton, the banker. He was about your age, married to a very pretty girl, and they’d a fine baby. He was immensely rich, a strong healthy young fellow, fond of life, popular, without a care in the world, so far as anyone knew. One morning, after breakfasting with his wife, he walked away from his house, on the outskirts of the town﻿—only a very small town, mind you﻿—to go to the bank, as usual. He never reached the bank﻿—in fact, he was never seen again, never heard of again. He’d only half a mile to walk, along a fairly frequented road, but﻿—complete, absolute, final disappearance! And﻿—never cleared up!”

“Odd!” agreed Viner. “Very odd, indeed. Well﻿—any more?”

“Plenty!” said Miss Penkridge, with a click of her needles. “There was the case of poor young Lady Marshflower﻿—as sweet a young thing as man could wish to see! Your mother and I saw her married﻿—she was a Ravenstone, and only nineteen. She married Sir Thomas Marshflower, a man of forty. They’d only just come home from the honeymoon when it﻿—happened. One morning Sir Thomas rode into the market-town to preside at the petty sessions﻿—he hadn’t been long gone when a fine, distinguished-looking man called, and asked to see Lady Marshflower. He was shown into the morning-room﻿—she went to him. Five minutes later a shot was heard. The servants rushed in﻿—to find their young mistress shot through the heart, dead. But the murderer? Disappeared as completely as last year’s snow! That was never solved, never!”

===Chinese===

“好比说，”彭克里奇小姐答道，一面用品评的眼光端详着手里的针线，“有一桩案子，在你可怜的母亲和我还是黄毛丫头的时候，大大轰动过一阵。就出在我们住的那座镇上——银行家小昆顿（Quainton）。他年纪同你相仿，娶了个非常俊俏的太太，家里还有个壮实的娃娃。他极其富有，是个筋骨强健的年轻人，热爱生活，人缘又好，据任何人所知，尘世上没有一件叫他操心的事。一天早晨，他同妻子用罢早餐，从镇子边上——记着，那不过是很小的一座镇子——的家里动身，照常步行去银行。他再没有走到银行——其实，他从此再没有人见过，再没有一点音信。统共不过半英里的路，走的又是一条行人不算稀少的大路，可是——完完全全、干干净净、彻头彻尾地失踪了！而且——到今天没有弄明白！”

“古怪！”维纳附和道。“确实非常古怪。那么——还有么？”

“多着呢！”彭克里奇小姐说着，织针咔嗒一响。“还有可怜的年轻马什弗劳尔夫人（Lady Marshflower）那一桩——多标致的一位小佳人，男人见了没有不中意的！你母亲和我都亲眼瞧着她出嫁——她是拉文斯通家（Ravenstone）的小姐，才十九岁。嫁的是托马斯·马什弗劳尔爵士（Sir Thomas Marshflower），一位四十岁的男人。两口子蜜月旅行方归，那件事就——出了。一天早晨，托马斯爵士骑马进城去主持治安法庭——他走了没多久，便来了一位气宇轩昂、仪表堂堂的男子，求见马什弗劳尔夫人。仆人把他领进晨室——她去了。五分钟之后，一声枪响。仆人们抢进房去——只见他们年轻的主妇被一枪打穿了心脏，死了。可那凶手呢？消失得如同去年的积雪，一丝影踪不留！那桩案子始终没破，始终没有！”


===Original===

“Do you mean to tell me the man was never caught?” exclaimed Viner.

“I tell you that not only was the man never caught, but that although Sir Thomas spent a fortune and nearly lost his senses in trying to find out who he was, what he wanted and what he had to do with Lady Marshflower, he never discovered one single fact!” affirmed Miss Penkridge. “There!”

“That’s queerer than the other,” observed Viner. “A veritable mystery!”

“Veritable mysteries!” said Miss Penkridge, with a sniff. “The world’s full of ’em! How many murders go undetected﻿—how many burglaries are never traced﻿—how many forgeries are done and never found out? Piles of ’em﻿—as the police could tell you. And talking about forgeries, what about old Barrett, who was *the* great man at Pumpney, when your mother and I were girls there? That was a fine case of crime going on for years and years and years, undetected﻿—aye, and not even suspected!”

===Chinese===

“你的意思是说，那人始终没有落网？”维纳失声叫道。

“我告诉你，岂止那人始终没有落网，”彭克里奇小姐一口咬定，“托马斯爵士为了查明那人是谁、来意何在、同马什弗劳尔夫人又有何干，家私花去了一大笔，人也差点儿疯掉，到头来却连一星半点儿实情也没查出来！你说说看！”

“这比那一桩还要古怪，”维纳品评道。“真正的一桩疑案！”

“真正的疑案！”彭克里奇小姐嗤了一声说。“这世上到处都是！多少命案沉冤未破——多少盗窃案无从追查——多少伪造文书做了又做、始终无人察觉？成堆成堆——警察可以讲给你听。说到伪造文书，还有老巴雷特呢——你母亲和我做姑娘儿的时候，他在普姆尼（Pumpney）可算得独一无二的头号人物。那才是一桩好案子：罪案一年一年又一年地干下去，无人侦破——是啊，连疑心都没有人疑心过！”


===Original===

“What was it?” asked Viner, who had begun by being amused and was now becoming interested. “Who was Barrett?”

“If you’d known Pumpney when we lived there,” replied Miss Penkridge, “you wouldn’t have had to ask twice who Mr. Samuel Barrett was. He was everybody. He was everything﻿—except honest. But nobody knew that﻿—until it was too late. He was a solicitor by profession, but that was a mere nothing﻿—in comparison. He was chief spirit in the place. I don’t know how many times he wasn’t mayor of Pumpney. He held all sorts of offices. He was a big man at the parish church﻿—vicar’s warden, and all that. And he was trustee for half the moneyed people in the town﻿—everybody wanted Samuel Barrett, for trustee or executor; he was such a solid, respectable, square-toed man, the personification of integrity. And he died, suddenly, and then it was found that he’d led a double life, and had an establishment here in London, and was a gambler and a speculator, and Heaven knows what, and all the money that had been entrusted to him was nowhere, and he’d systematically forged, and cooked accounts, and embezzled corporation money﻿—and he’d no doubt have gone on doing it for many a year longer if he hadn’t had a stroke of apoplexy. And that wasn’t in a novel!” concluded Miss Penkridge triumphantly. “Novels﻿—Improbability﻿—pooh! Judged by what some people can tell of life, the novel that’s improbable hasn’t yet been written!”

“Well!” remarked Viner after a pause, “I dare say you’re right, Aunt Bethia. Only, you see, I haven’t come across the things in life that you read about in novels.”

===Chinese===

“那是怎么一回事？”维纳问道，他起初只当趣谈，此刻却来了兴致。“巴雷特是谁？”

“你要是认得我们住在普姆尼那阵子的老巴雷特，就不会把塞缪尔·巴雷特先生（Mr. Samuel Barrett）是谁这件事问上两遍了，”彭克里奇小姐答道。“他无处不在，无所不当——独独不诚实。可这一点谁也不知道——直到知道已经太迟。他正业是个律师，可那比起别的来不过区区小节。他是那地方的头号灵魂人物。他当过多少届普姆尼市长，我都记不清。他一身兼职不计其数。他在教区教堂里也是个大人物——教堂执事什么的。他还是镇上半数有钱人家的财产受托人——谁家都想请塞缪尔·巴雷特做受托人、做遗嘱执行人；他是这么一位敦实、体面、一板一眼的人物，正直的化身。后来他猝然死了，这才发现他一直过着双重生活，在伦敦（London）这边另有一处公馆，又是赌徒又是投机家，天知道还有些什么名堂，凡托付给他的钱财一概不知去向，他有步骤地伪造文书、篡改账目、挪用市镇公款——若不是突然中了风，毫无疑问他还会照这样再干上好些年。这一桩可不在小说里！”彭克里奇小姐得意扬扬地下了断语。“小说——荒诞不经——呸！照某些人讲得出的真实人生来判断，真正荒诞不经的小说还没有人写出来呢！”

“唉！”维纳沉吟了半晌说道，“我敢说你是对的，贝西娅姨妈。只不过，你知道，小说里读到的那些事，我在生活里还不曾碰到过。”


===Original===

“You may yet,” replied Miss Penkridge. “But when anybody says to me of a novel that it’s impossible and far-fetched and so on, I’m always inclined to remind him of the old adage. For you can take it from me, Richard, that truth is stranger than fiction, and that life’s full of queer things. Only, as you say, we don’t all come across the strange things.”

The silvery chime of the clock on the mantelpiece caused Miss Penkridge, at this point, to bring her work and her words to a summary conclusion. Hurrying her knitting into the handbag which she carried at her belt, she rose, kissed her nephew and departed bedward; while Viner, after refilling his pipe, proceeded to carry out another nightly proceeding which had become a habit. Every night, throughout the year, he always went for a walk before going to bed. And now, getting into an overcoat and pulling a soft cap over his head, he let himself out of the house, and crossing the square, turned down a side-street and marched slowly in the direction of the Bayswater Road.

November though it was the night was fine and clear, and there was a half-moon in the heavens; also there was rather more than a suspicion of frost in the air, and the stars, accordingly, wore a more brilliant appearance. To one who loved night strolling, as Viner did, this was indeed an ideal night for the time of year; and on this occasion, therefore, he went further than usual going along Bayswater Road as far as Notting Hill Gate, and thence returning through the various streets and terraces which lay between Pembridge Gardens and Markendale Square. And while he strolled along, smoking his pipe, watching the twinkling lights of passing vehicles and enjoying the touch of frost, he was thinking, in a half-cynical, half-amused way, of his Aunt Bethia’s taste for the sensational fiction and of her evidently sincere conviction that there were much stranger things in real life than could be found between the covers of any novel.

===Chinese===

“往后也许碰得到，”彭克里奇小姐答道。“不过每逢有人对我说某部小说不可能、太牵强等等，我总忍不住要请他记起那句老话。因为你可以信我的话，理查德：事实比小说更离奇，生活里满是古怪事儿。只不过，正如你说的，古怪事儿不是人人都撞得上的。”

正说到这里，壁炉架上那座钟敲出一串银铃般的乐音，彭克里奇小姐便就此草草结束了手里的活计和嘴里的话头。她慌忙把针线塞进挂在腰际的手提包，站起身，吻了吻外甥，径自安寝去了；维纳呢，重新装满烟斗，接着去履行另一项已成惯例的每晚功课。一年到头，夜夜如此，他上床之前总要出去走一遭。此刻他穿上大衣，扣好一顶软帽，自己开门出了宅子，穿过广场，拐进一条小街，朝贝斯沃特路（Bayswater Road）的方向信步而去。

时令虽是十一月，夜色却晴朗澄净，天心悬着一弯半月；空气里的霜意也不止一丝半点，星子因此显得格外晶亮。对一个像维纳这样酷爱夜行的人，这在这个季节里确是一个再理想不过的夜晚；于是这一晚他走得比往常更远，沿贝斯沃特路一直走到诺丁山门（Notting Hill Gate），再从那儿折回来，穿行在彭布里奇花园（Pembridge Gardens）与马肯代尔广场之间那一片纵横的街巷排屋之间。他一路缓缓踱着，抽着烟斗，望着过往车辆闪烁的灯火，受用着拂面的轻霜，心里半带讥诮、半觉好笑地想着贝西娅姨妈对惊险小说的那份嗜好，想着她那显然出于至诚的信念：现实生活里的事情，远比任何一部小说的书皮之间夹着的更离奇。


===Original===

“Those were certainly two very odd instances which she gave me,” he mused, “those of the prosperous banker and the pretty bride. In the first, how on earth did the man contrive to get away unobserved from a town in which, presumably, every soul knew him? Why did he go? Did he go? Is his body lying at the bottom of some hole by some roadside? Was he murdered in broad daylight on a public road? Did he lose his reason or his memory, and wander away and away? I think, as my aunt sagely remarked, that nobody is ever going to find anything about that affair! Then my Lady Marshflower﻿—there’s a fine mystery! Who was the man? What did she know about him? Where had they met? Had they ever met? Why did he shoot her? How on earth did he contrive to disappear without leaving some trace? How﻿—”

At this point Viner’s musings and questionings were suddenly and rudely interrupted. Unconsciously he had walked back close to his own Square, but on the opposite side to that by which he had left it, approaching it by one of the numerous long terraces which run out of the main road in the Westbourne Grove district﻿—when his musings were rudely interrupted. Between this terrace and Markendale Square was a narrow passage, little frequented save by residents, or by such folk familiar enough with the neighbourhood to know that it afforded a shortcut. Viner was about to turn into this passage, a dark affair set between high walls, when a young man darted hurriedly out of it, half collided with him, uttered a hasty word of apology, ran across the road and disappeared round the nearest corner. But just there stood a streetlamp, and in its glare Viner caught sight of the hurrying young man’s face. And when the retreating footsteps had grown faint, Viner still stood staring in the direction in which they had gone.

“That’s strange!” he muttered. “I’ve seen that chap somewhere﻿—I know him. Now, who is he? And what made him in such a deuce of a hurry?”

===Chinese===

“她方才举的这两个例子，的确都古怪得很，”他暗自琢磨，“一个是那位兴旺发达的银行家，一个是那位如花似玉的新娘。头一个，那人是怎么做到的，居然能从一座想来人人识得他的镇子不露形迹地走脱？他为什么要走？他真走了么？他的尸身是不是正躺在哪条路边一个洞穴的底上？他是不是光天化日之下在一条公用大道上遭了毒手？他是不是丧失了神志或记忆，一路漂泊，越走越远？我看，正如我姨妈明断的那样，那桩案子是谁也休想查出个究竟了！再说我们的那位马什弗劳尔夫人——好一桩疑案！那男人是谁？她知道他些什么？他们在哪里碰过面？他们到底碰过面没有？他为什么开枪打她？他又怎么做到消逝得连一丝痕迹不留？怎么——”

正想到这里，维纳的沉思和疑问被突然而粗暴地打断了。不知不觉间他已走回自家广场左近，只是走的不是出门的那一面，而是从韦斯特本格罗夫（Westbourne Grove）街区由大路岔出去的众多长排屋中的一条走近它的——恰在此时，他的沉思被粗暴地打断了。这条排屋街与马肯代尔广场之间有一条窄巷，除了住户，只有那些对左近足够熟稔、知道它是一条近路的人才走。维纳正要拐进这条夹在高墙之间、黑黢黢的巷子，一个年轻人急匆匆地从里头窜出来，同他撞了个半怀，慌忙丢下一句道歉的话，奔过马路，在最近的一个街角后头一闪便没了影。恰好那里立着一盏路灯，灯影煌煌之下，维纳看清了那年轻人仓皇的一张脸。远去的脚步声渐渐微弱下去之后，维纳仍旧站在那里，朝脚步声消逝的方向呆呆望着。

“怪事！”他喃喃道。“这家伙我在哪儿见过——我认得他。可是，他是谁呢？又是什么事，逼得他这样没命地赶路？”


===Original===

It was very quiet at that point. There seemed to be nobody about. Behind him, far down the long, wide terrace, he heard slow, measured steps﻿—that, of course, was a policeman on his beat. But beyond the subdued murmur of the traffic in the Bayswater Road in one direction and in Bishop’s Road, Viner heard nothing but those measured steps. And after listening to them for a minute, he turned into the passage out of which the young man had just rushed so unceremoniously.

There was just one lamp in that passage﻿—an old-fashioned affair, fixed against the wall, halfway down. It threw but little light on its surroundings. Those surroundings were ordinary enough. The passage itself was about thirty yards in length. It was enclosed on each-side by old brick walls, so old that the brick had grown black with age and smoke. These walls were some fifteen feet in height; here and there they were pierced by doors﻿—the doors of the yards at the rear of the big houses on either side. The doors were set flush with the walls﻿—Viner, who often walked through that passage at night, and who had something of a whimsical fancy, had thought more than once that after nightfall the doors looked as if they had never been opened, never shut. There was an air of queer, cloistral or prisonlike security in their very look. They were all shut now, as he paced down the passage, as lonely a place at that hour as you could find in all London. It was queer, he reflected, that he scarcely ever remembered meeting anybody in that passage.

And then he suddenly paused, pulling himself up with a strange consciousness that at last he was to meet something. Beneath the feeble light of the one lamp Viner saw a man. Not a man walking, or standing still, or leaning against the wall, but lying full length across the flagged pavement, motionless﻿—so motionless that at the end of the first moment of surprise, Viner felt sure that he was in the presence of death. And then he stole nearer, listening, and looked down, and drawing his matchbox from his pocket added the flash of a match to the poor rays from above. Then he saw white linen, and a bloodstain slowly spreading over its glossy surface.

===Chinese===

那个地界非常清静。四下里似乎一个人影也没有。在他背后，那条又长又宽的排屋街远远的尽头，传来缓慢而匀整的脚步声——那自然是巡警在巡他的路段。除了这一边的贝斯沃特路和那一边的主教路（Bishop’s Road）上隐约低回的车马声，维纳耳朵里便只剩那匀整的脚步声。他听了约莫一分钟，便转身走进那条年轻人方才如此冒失地冲出来的窄巷。

那条巷子里统共只有一盏灯——一盏老式的灯，钉在巷子半截深处的墙上。它照不亮周围多少地方。周围的一切也平平无奇。巷子本身约莫三十码长，两侧围着古老的砖墙，古得砖色都被岁月和烟火熏成了黑。墙高约莫十五英尺，隔三差五开着一道门——是两旁大宅后院的门。那些门都与墙面齐平——维纳夜里常打这条巷子走过，天性又有些古怪的遐思，不止一次想到：入夜之后，这些门看上去就像从来不曾开过、也从来不曾关过似的。它们那副神气里自有一种古怪的、修道院一般、监狱一般的森严。此刻他顺着巷子往里走，门扇全都关着，这条巷子在这个时辰，是偌大伦敦里你能寻见的最冷清的处所。他想，真是古怪，他简直不记得在这条巷子里撞见过什么人。

就在这时，他猛地立定，怀着一种异样的预感收住脚步：这一回，他终于要撞上点什么了。那盏孤灯微弱的灯光底下，维纳看见一个人。不是一个走着的人，也不是一个站定的人，更不是一个倚墙的人，而是直挺挺横躺在石板铺的巷路上，一动不动——一动不动到这般地步，以致维纳在最初一惊之后便确信，自己面对的是死亡。随后他蹑手蹑脚地挨近，侧耳听了听，低头看去，又从口袋里摸出火柴盒，擦亮一根火柴，给头顶那一线可怜的光添上一星火光。于是他看见了白色的亚麻布，和一摊血渍，正在那光洁的布面上缓缓洇开。

## II / 二

===Original===

Number Seven in the Square

Before the sputter of the match had died out, Viner had recognized the man who lay dead at his feet. He was a man about whom he had recently felt some curiosity, a man who, a few weeks before, had come to live in a house close to his own, in company with an elderly lady and a pretty girl; Viner and Miss Penkridge had often seen all three in and about Markendale Square, and had wondered who they were. The man looked as if he had seen things in life﻿—a big, burly, bearded man of apparently sixty years of age, hard, bronzed; something about him suggested sun and wind as they are met with in the far-off places. Usually he was seen in loose, comfortable, semi-nautical suits of blue serge; there was a roll in his walk that suggested the sea. But here, as he lay before Viner, he was in evening dress, with a light overcoat thrown over it; the overcoat was unbuttoned and the shirtfront exposed. And on it that sickening crimson stain widened and widened as Viner watched.

Here, without doubt, was murder, and Viner’s thoughts immediately turned to two things﻿—one the hurrying young man whose face he thought he had remembered in some vague fashion; the other the fact that a policeman was slowly pacing up the terrace close by. He turned and ran swiftly up the still deserted passage. And there was the policeman, twenty yards away, coming along with the leisureliness of one who knows that he has a certain area to patrol. He pulled himself to an attitude of watchful attention as Viner ran up to him; then suddenly recognizing Viner as a well-known inhabitant of the Square, touched the rim of his helmet.

===Chinese===

广场上的七号

火柴的噼啪声还未熄灭，维纳（Viner）就认出了倒在自己脚下的这个死人。此人近来曾引起他几分好奇——几个星期之前，他携一位上了年纪的太太和一个俊俏的姑娘，搬到离他家不远的一所宅子里住下；维纳和彭克里奇小姐（Miss Penkridge）常在马肯代尔广场（Markendale Square）内外望见他们三人，也常猜测他们究竟是什么人。这人看上去像是阅历世事——身材高大魁伟，蓄着胡须，年纪约莫六十岁，筋骨结实，面色红黑；他周身上下有种气息，叫人想起天涯海角那般地方的风吹日晒。平日见他，总穿着宽松舒适、带几分水手风格的蓝哗叽便服；走起路来微微摇晃，透出常年海上生活的味道。可此刻，他直挺挺躺在维纳面前，身上却穿着晚礼服，外面罩了件薄大衣；大衣敞着，衬衫前襟露在外面。维纳眼睁睁看着，那片令人作呕的猩红血渍在前襟上越漫越大。

这无疑是谋杀。维纳的念头立刻转到两件事上：一是那个匆匆离去的年轻人，他觉得自己仿佛依稀记得那张面孔；二是附近平台上正缓缓巡逻的一名警察。他转过身，沿着那条依然空无一人的过道疾步跑了回去。那警察就在二十码开外，正不慌不忙地走来，一副深知自己巡逻范围的从容样子。见维纳朝自己奔来，他立刻挺身摆出警戒的姿态；随即认出维纳是广场一带的知名住户，便伸手碰了碰头盔檐。

===Original===

“I say!” said Viner in the hushed voice of one who imparts strange and confidential tidings. “There’s a man lying dead in the passage round here. And without doubt murdered! There’s blood all over his shirtfront.”

The policeman stood stock still for the fraction of a second. Then he pulled out his whistle and blew loudly and insistently. Before the shrill call had died away, he was striding towards the passage, with Viner at his side.

“Did you find him, Mr. Viner?” he asked.

“I found him,” asserted Viner. “Just now﻿—halfway down the passage!”

“Sure he’s dead, sir?”

===Chinese===

“喂！”维纳压低了嗓音说，像是在转告什么离奇而机密的消息。“这附近的过道里躺着个死人。而且八成是被人谋害的！他衬衫前襟上全是血。”

警察怔了怔，站在原地一动不动。随即他掏出警笛，大声而不停地吹了起来。尖利的笛声尚未消散，他已大步朝过道走去，维纳紧随在旁。

“是您发现他的吗，维纳先生？”他问。

“是我发现的，”维纳口气肯定，“就在刚才——在过道中段！”

“您确定他死了，先生？”

===Original===

“Dead﻿—yes! And murdered, too! And﻿—”

He was about to mention the hurrying young man, but they had just then arrived at the mouth of the passage, and the policeman once more drew out his whistle and blew more insistently than before.

“There’s my sergeant and inspector not far off,” he remarked. “Some of ’em’ll be on the spot in a minute or two. Now then, sir.”

He marched down the passage to the dead man, glanced at the lamp, and turning on his own lantern, directed its light on the body.

“God bless me!” he muttered. “Mr. Ashton!”

===Chinese===

“死了——没错！还是被人谋害的！还有——”

他正要提起那个匆匆跑开的年轻人，两人却已到了过道口，警察便再次掏出警笛，吹得比方才更急。

“我们巡佐和督察就在不远处，”他说道，“一两分钟内就会有人赶到现场。走吧，先生。”

他大步走到过道里的死人跟前，朝那盏灯瞥了一眼，拧亮自己的手提灯，把光束照在尸体上。

“老天爷！”他低声惊呼，“阿什顿（Ashton）先生！”

===Original===

“You know him?” said Viner.

“Gent that came to live at number seven in your square a while back, Mr. Viner,” answered the policeman. “Australian or New Zealander, I fancy. He’s gone right enough, sir! And﻿—knifed! You didn’t see anybody about, sir?”

“Yes,” replied Viner, “that’s just it. As I turned into the passage, I met a young fellow running out of it in a great hurry﻿—he ran into me, and then, shot off across the road, Westbourne Grove way. Then I came along and found﻿—this!”

The policeman bent lower and suddenly put a knowing finger on certain of the dead man’s pockets.

“Robbed!” he said. “No watch there, anyway, and nothing where you’d expect to find his purse. Robbery and murder﻿—murder for the sake of robbery﻿—that’s what it is, Mr. Viner! Westbourne Grove way, you say this fellow went? And five minutes’ start!”

===Chinese===

“你认识他？”维纳问。

“就是前一阵子搬到您广场七号宅的那位先生呀，维纳先生，”警察答道，“我猜是澳大利亚人，也可能是新西兰人。他这回是真死了，先生！而且是——挨了刀子！您没瞧见附近有什么人吗，先生？”

“瞧见了，”维纳答道，“问题正在这儿。我刚拐进过道，迎面撞见一个小伙子慌慌张张地跑出来——他一头撞在我身上，随后蹿过马路，朝韦斯特本格罗夫（Westbourne Grove）方向跑了。接着我走进去，就发现了——这个！”

警察俯下身去，忽然伸出手指，内行地在死人身上的几处口袋上按了按。

“遭劫了！”他说，“起码表没了，往常该放钱夹的地方也空空如也。劫财带谋杀——为劫财而谋命——就是这么回事，维纳先生！您说那家伙朝韦斯特本格罗夫方向跑了？足足跑出去五分钟了！”

===Original===

“Is it any good getting a doctor?” asked Viner.

“A thousand doctors’ll do him no good,” replied the policeman grimly. “But﻿—there’s Dr. Cortelyon somewhere about here﻿—number seven in the terrace. One of these back doors is his. We might call him.”

He turned the light of his lantern on the line of doors in the right-hand wall, and finding the number he wanted, pulled the bell. As its tinkle sounded somewhere up the yard behind, he thrust his whistle into Viner’s hand.

“Mr. Viner,” he said, “go up to the end of the passage and blow on that as loud as you can, three times. I’ll stand by here till you come back. If you don’t hear or see any of our people coming from either direction, blow again.”

Viner heard steps coming down the yard behind the door as he walked away. And he heard more steps, hurrying steps, as he reached the end of the passage. He turned it to find an inspector and a sergeant approaching from one part of the terrace, a constable from another.

“You’re wanted down here,” said Viner as they all converged on him. “There’s been murder! One of your men’s there﻿—he gave me this whistle to summon further help. This way!”

===Chinese===

“要不要请个医生来？”维纳问。

“请一千个医生也救不活他了，”警察冷冷地答道，“不过——科特利恩医生（Dr. Cortelyon）就住在这一带——平台上七号。这一排后门里有一扇就是他家的。咱们可以去叫叫他。”

他把手提灯的光转向右手墙那排门，找到了要找的门牌号，便去拉门铃。铃声在后院深处叮当作响，这时他把警笛塞进维纳手里。

“维纳先生，”他说，“您到过道那头去，使足力气吹三声。我在这儿守着，等您回来。要是两个方向都没听见、没看见我们自己人来，就再吹。”

维纳转身走开时，听见门后院子里传来脚步声。等他走到过道尽头，又听见一阵脚步，还是急促的脚步。他拐过弯，只见一名督察和一名巡佐正从平台一头走来，另一头还来了个警察。

“下面有用得着各位的地方，”众人朝他聚拢过来时，维纳说道，“出了人命案！你们的一位同事正在那儿——他把这警笛给我，让我召更多的人来。这边走！”

===Original===

The police followed him in silence down the passage. Another figure had come on the scene. Bending over the body and closely scrutinizing it in the light of the policeman’s lantern was a man whom Viner knew well enough by sight﻿—a tall, handsome man, whose olive-tinted complexion, large lustrous eyes and Vandyke beard gave him the appearance of a foreigner. Yet though he had often seen him, Viner did not know his name; the police-inspector, however, evidently knew it well enough.

“What is it, Dr. Cortelyon?” he asked as he pushed himself to the front. “Is the man dead?”

Dr. Cortelyon drew himself up from his stooping position to his full height﻿—a striking figure in his dress jacket and immaculate linen. He glanced round at the expectant faces.

“The man’s been murdered!” he said in calm, professional accents. “He’s been stabbed clean through the heart. Dead? Yes, for several minutes.”

“Who found him here?” demanded the inspector.

“I found him,” answered Viner. He gave a hurried account of the whole circumstances as he knew them, the police watching him keenly. “I should know the man again if I saw him,” he concluded. “I saw his face clearly enough as he passed me.”

===Chinese===

警察们默默跟着他走下过道。现场又多了一个人影。一个男子正俯在尸体上，就着警察的手提灯光仔细察看——此人维纳相当面熟——高挑的身材，仪表堂堂，橄榄色的皮肤、乌亮的大眼睛和范戴克式尖须，让他透出几分外国人的模样。虽然常见此人，维纳却叫不上他的名字；不过那位督察显然熟得很。

“怎么回事，科特利恩医生？”他挤到前面问道，“这人死了吗？”

科特利恩医生直起身来，站得笔挺——礼服上装加一尘不染的雪白衬衫，风度夺目。他环视四周一张张屏息期待的面孔。

“这人是被谋害的！”他以平静而内行的口吻说道，“被人一刀正扎穿了心脏。死了吗？是的，已经死了好几分钟了。”

“是谁在这儿发现他的？”督察厉声问。

“我发现的，”维纳答道。他把所知的全部经过匆匆讲了一遍，警察们目光锐利地盯着他。“再见到那人，我认得出，”他末了说道，“他擦身而过时，我看清了他的脸。”

===Original===

The inspector bent down and hastily felt the dead man’s pockets.

“Nothing at all here,” he said as he straightened himself. “No watch or chain or purse or anything. Looks like robbery as well as murder. Does anybody know him?”

“I know who this gentlemen is, sir,” answered the policeman to whom Viner had first gone. “He’s a Mr. Ashton, who came to live not so long since at number seven in Markendale Square, close by Mr. Viner there. I’ve heard that he came from the Colonies.”

“Do you know him,” asked the inspector, turning to Viner.

“Only by sight,” answered Viner. “I’ve seen him often, but I didn’t know his name. I believe he has a wife and daughter﻿—”

“No sir,” interrupted the policeman. “He was a single gentleman. The young lady at number seven is his ward, and the older lady looked after her﻿—sort of a companion.”

===Chinese===

督察俯下身，匆匆摸了摸死人的口袋。

“什么都没有，”他直起身来说道，“表、表链、钱夹，一样都没有。看来是谋财害命。有人认识他吗？”

“我知道这位先生是谁，长官，”维纳最先去找的那名警察答道，“是阿什顿先生，不久前搬到马肯代尔广场七号住的，就在这位维纳先生家附近。我听说他是从殖民地来的。”

“您认识他吗？”督察转向维纳问道。

“只是面熟，”维纳答道，“常见到他，但不知道他的姓名。我以为他有妻室和女儿——”

“不是，先生，”警察插话说，“他是位单身绅士。七号宅的那位小姐是他的被监护人，那位年长的太太是照应她的——算是陪伴之人。”

===Original===

The Inspector looked round. Other policemen, attracted by the whistle, were coming into the passage at each end, and he turned to his sergeant.

“Put a man at the top and another at the bottom of this passage,” he said. “Keep everybody out. Send for the divisional surgeon. Dr. Cortelyon, will you see him when he comes along? I want him to see the body before its removal. Now, then, about these ladies﻿—they’ll have to be told.” He turned to Viner. “I understand you live close by them?” he asked. “Perhaps you’ll go there with me?”

Viner nodded; and the inspector, after giving a few more words of instruction to the sergeant, motioned him to follow; together they went down the passage into Markendale Square.

“Been resident here long, Mr. Viner?” asked the Inspector as they emerged. “I noticed that some of my men knew you. I’ve only recently come into this part myself.”

“Fifteen years,” answered Viner.

“Do you know anything of this dead man?”

===Chinese===

督察环顾四周。闻笛声而来的其他警察正从过道两头进来，他便转向巡佐。

“过道两头各派一人把守，”他说，“任何人不得入内。去请分区警医。科特利恩医生，他来了您在这儿见一见好吗？我要他在尸体搬走之前先验看一遍。那么，关于这两位女士——总得有人去报信。”他转向维纳。“我听说您就住在她们附近？”他问，“可否劳驾陪我走一趟？”

维纳点点头。督察又对巡佐交代了几句，便示意他跟上；两人一同走出过道，进了马肯代尔广场。

“您在这儿住得久吗，维纳先生？”走出过道时督察问道，“我注意到我手下有人认得您。我自己调到这一区才不久。”

“十五年了，”维纳答道。

“您了解这个死人的情况吗？”

===Original===

“Nothing﻿—not so much as your constable knows.”

“Policemen pick things up. These ladies, now? It’s a most unpleasant thing to have to go and break news like this. You know nothing about them, sir?”

“Not even as much as your man knew. I’ve seen them often﻿—with him, the dead man. There’s an elderly lady and a younger one, a mere girl. I took them for his wife and daughter. But you heard what your man said.”

“Well, whatever they are, they’ve got to be told. I’d be obliged if you’d come with me. And then﻿—that fellow you saw running away! You’ll have to give us as near a description of him as you can. What number did my man say it was﻿—seven?”

Viner suddenly laid a hand on his companion’s sleeve. A smart car, of the sort let out on hire from the more pretentious automobile establishments, had just come round the corner and was being pulled up at the door of a house in whose porticoed front hung a brilliant lamp.

===Chinese===

“一无所知——还不及您那位警员知道得多。”

“警察嘛，东听西听总能捡着些消息。那这两位女士呢？登门去报这样的凶信，实在是桩顶不痛快的差事。您对她们一无所知吗，先生？”

“连您那位警员知道的都不如。我常常见到她们——同他，也就是这位死者在一起。一位是上了年纪的太太，一位年轻些，还是个姑娘。我一直当她们是他的妻子和女儿。可您那位警员的话，您也听见了。”

“唉，不管她们是什么人，这信总得报。您肯陪我去一趟，我感激不尽。还有——您看见跑掉的那个家伙！您得尽量把他的模样给我们描述一番。我那位警员说的是几号来着——七号？”

维纳忽然伸手按住同伴的衣袖。一辆气派的汽车——就是上等汽车行出租的那种——刚拐过街角，正停在一所宅子门前，那带门廊的宅子正面悬着一盏亮晃晃的灯。

===Original===

“That’s number seven,” said Viner. “And﻿—those are the two ladies.”

The Inspector stopped and watched. The door of the house opened, letting a further flood of light on the broad step beneath the portico and on the pavement beyond; the door of the car opened too, and a girl stepped out, and for a second or two stood in the full glare of the lamps. She was a slender, lissome young creature, gowned in white, and muffled to the throat in an opera cloak out of which a fresh, girlish face, bright in colour, sparkling of eye, crowned by a mass of hair of the tint of dead gold, showed clearly ere she rapidly crossed to the open door. After her came an elderly, well-preserved woman in an elaborate evening toilette, the personification of the precise and conventional chaperon. The door closed; the car drove away; the Inspector turned to Viner with a shake of his head.

“Just home from the theatre!” he said. “And﻿—to hear this! Well, it’s got to be done, Mr. Viner, anyhow.”

Viner, who had often observed the girl whom they had just seen with an interest for which he had never troubled to account, found himself wishing that Miss Penkridge was there in his place. He did not know what part he was to play, what he was to do or say; worse than that, he did not know if the girl in whose presence he would certainly find himself within a minute or two was very fond of the man whom he had just found done to death. In that case﻿—but here his musings were cut short by the fact that the Inspector had touched the bell in the portico of number seven, and that the door had opened, to reveal a smart and wondering parlourmaid, who glanced with surprise at the inspector’s uniform.

“Hush! This is Mr. Ashton’s?” said the Inspector. “Yes﻿—well, now, what is the name of the lady﻿—the elderly lady﻿—I saw come in just now? Keep quiet, there’s a good girl﻿—the fact is, Mr. Ashton’s had an accident, and I want to see that lady.”

===Chinese===

“那就是七号，”维纳说，“还有——那两位女士回来了。”

督察停住脚望着。宅门开了，又一片灯光泼洒在门廊下宽阔的台阶上和外面的人行道上；车门也开了，一个姑娘跨了下来，在车灯的强光里站了一两秒钟。她是个纤细柔韧的年轻姑娘，一袭白裙，观剧斗篷裹到颈边；那张清新的少女脸庞，色泽明艳，双目流光，头顶浓密的头发泛着暗金色——她快步走向敞开的宅门之前，这一切都看得清清楚楚。她身后下来一位保养得宜的年长妇人，考究的晚礼服盛装华服，活脱是那种一丝不苟、恪守常规的伴护太太的化身。宅门关上了；汽车驶走了；督察摇着头转向维纳。

“刚从戏院回来！”他说，“却要——听这个消息！唉，这事总得办，维纳先生，横竖躲不掉。”

维纳素常怀着一种自己也懒得深究的兴趣，留意着他们方才望见的那位姑娘；此刻他倒盼着彭克里奇小姐能替他站在这个地方。他不清楚自己该演个什么角色，该做什么、说什么；更糟的是，一两分钟内他必将站到那姑娘面前，却不知道她对这个刚被残害致死的人，感情究竟有多深。倘若很深的话——可他的胡思乱想到此被打断了：督察已按响七号门廊的门铃，门开了，露出一名伶俐而满脸诧异的客厅侍女，正惊异地打量督察那身制服。

“嘘，轻声！这是阿什顿先生的府上？”督察说，“好——那么请问，我方才看见进去的那位太太——年长的那位——尊姓大名？安静点，好姑娘——是这样，阿什顿先生出了点意外，我要见那位太太。”

===Original===

“Mrs. Killenhall,” answered the parlourmaid.

“And the young lady﻿—her name?” asked the Inspector.

“Miss Wickham.”

The Inspector walked inside the house.

“Just ask Mrs. Killenhall and Miss Wickham if they’ll be good enough to see Inspector Drillford for a few minutes,” he said. Then, as the girl closed the door and turned away up the inner hall, he whispered to Viner. “Better see both and be done with it. It’s no use keeping bad news too long; they may as well know﻿—both.”

===Chinese===

“基伦霍尔夫人（Mrs. Killenhall），”客厅侍女答道。

“那位小姐——她叫什么？”督察问。

“威克姆小姐（Miss Wickham）。”

督察走进屋内。

“请去问一声基伦霍尔夫人和威克姆小姐，可否劳驾见一见德里尔福德督察（Inspector Drillford）几分钟，”他说。待侍女关上门、转身沿内厅走开后，他低声对维纳说：“干脆两个人一起见，一次了事。坏消息捂久了没用；反正都得知道——两个人都得。”

===Original===

The parlourmaid reappeared at the door of a room along the hall; and the two men, advancing in answer to her summons, entered what was evidently the dining-room of the house. The two ladies had thrown off their wraps; the younger one sat near a big, cheery fire, holding her slender fingers to the blaze; the elder stood facing the door in evident expectancy. The room itself was luxuriously furnished in a somewhat old-fashioned, heavy style; everything about it betokened wealth and comfort. And that its owner was expected home every minute was made evident to the two men by the fact that a spirit-case was set on the centre table, with glasses and mineral waters and cigars; Viner remembered, as his eyes encountered these things, that a half-burned cigar lay close to the dead man’s hand in that dark passage so close by.

“Mrs. Killenhall? Miss Wickham?” began Drillford, looking sharply from one to the other. “Sorry to break in on you like this, ladies, but the fact is, there has been an accident to Mr. Ashton, and I’m obliged to come and tell you about it.”

Viner, who had remained a little in the background, was watching the faces of the two to whom this initial breaking of news was made. And he saw at once that there was going to be no scene. The girl by the fire looked for an instant at the inspector with an expression of surprise, but it was not the surprise of great personal concern. As for the elder woman, after one quick glance from Drillford to Viner, whom she evidently recognized, she showed absolute self-possession.

“A bad accident?” she asked.

Drillford again looked from the elder to the younger lady.

===Chinese===

客厅侍女在门厅旁一间屋子的门口重新露面；两人应声上前，走进一间显然是餐室的屋子。两位女士已卸下外出斗篷；年轻的那位坐在一大炉欢腾的炉火边，纤细的手指伸向火苗；年长的那位面朝门口站着，显然在等着什么。屋子本身陈设奢华，风格略显老派厚重；处处透出富裕与安逸。而屋子的主人随时会到家这一点，两人也从一眼东西上看得分明：餐桌中央摆开了便携酒柜，配着玻璃酒杯、矿泉水和雪茄；维纳的目光扫过这些东西时，忽然想起，就在近旁那条黑暗的过道里，死者的手边还躺着半支烧剩的雪茄。

“基伦霍尔夫人？威克姆小姐？”德里尔福德开了口，目光锐利地在两人之间来回扫视，“两位女士，如此唐突打扰，实在抱歉。实情是，阿什顿先生出了意外，我不得不登门奉告。”

维纳稍稍退在后头，观察着这开场的凶信在两人脸上引起的反应。他一眼就看出，这里不会有哭天抢地的场面。炉边的姑娘向督察投来一瞬间的惊讶神色，但那惊讶里并没有多少切身的关切。至于那位年长的妇人，从德里尔福德到维纳身上飞快扫了一眼——显然认出了后者——便显出全然的镇定自若。

“严重的意外？”她问。

德里尔福德的目光又从年长的女士移到年轻的女士身上。

===Original===

“You’ll excuse me if I ask what relation you ladies are to Mr. Ashton?” he said with a significant glance at Mrs. Killenhall.

“None!” replied Mrs. Killenhall. “Miss Wickham is Mr. Ashton’s ward. I am Miss Wickham’s chaperon﻿—and companion.”

“Well, ma’am,” said Drillford, “then I may tell you that my news is﻿—just about as serious as it possibly could be, you understand.”

In the silence that followed, the girl turned toward the visitors, and Viner saw her colour change a little. And it was she who first spoke.

“Don’t be afraid to tell us,” she said. “Is Mr. Ashton dead?”

===Chinese===

“恕我冒昧，两位与阿什顿先生是什么关系？”他说着，意味深长地看了基伦霍尔夫人一眼。

“谈不上什么关系！”基伦霍尔夫人答道，“威克姆小姐是阿什顿先生的被监护人。我是威克姆小姐的伴护——兼作陪伴。”

“那么，夫人，”德里尔福德说，“我可以告诉您，我带来的消息——实在是再严重不过了，您明白吗。”

随之而来的一片寂静中，姑娘转向两位来客，维纳看见她的脸色微微变了。而先开口的正是她。

“别怕告诉我们，”她说，“阿什顿先生死了吗？”

===Original===

Drillford inclined his head, and spoke as he was bidden.

“I’m sorry to say he is,” he replied. “And still more to be obliged to tell you that he came to his death by violence. The truth is﻿—”

He paused, looking from one to the other, as if to gauge the effect of his words. And again it was the girl who spoke.

“What is the truth?” she asked.

“Murder!” said Drillford. “Just that!”

===Chinese===

德里尔福德低下头，如实照答。

“很遗憾，他死了，”他答道，“更不得不奉告的是，他是死于暴力。实情是——”

他顿了顿，目光在两人之间来回移动，仿佛在掂量这番话的分量。又是那姑娘先开了口。

“实情是什么？”她问。

“谋杀！”德里尔福德说，“正是这样！”

===Original===

Mrs. Killenhall, who had remained standing until then, suddenly sat down, with a murmur of horror. But the girl was watching the inspector steadily.

“When was this? and how, and where?” she inquired.

“A little time ago, near here,” answered Drillford. “This gentleman, Mr. Viner, a neighbour of yours, found him﻿—dead. There’s no doubt, from what we can see, that he was murdered for the sake of robbery. And I want some information about him, about his habits and﻿—”

Miss Wickham got up from her chair and looked meaningly at Mrs. Killenhall.

“The fact is,” she said, turning to Drillford; “strange as it may seem, neither Mrs. Killenhall nor myself know very much about Mr. Ashton.”

===Chinese===

基伦霍尔夫人先前一直站着，这时忽然坐了下去，发出一声恐怖的低呼。可那姑娘只是目不转睛地盯着督察。

“什么时候的事？怎么发生的？在哪儿？”她问道。

“就在不久前，离这儿不远，”德里尔福德答道，“这位维纳先生，你们的邻居，发现了他——已经死了。就我们目前所见，毫无疑义，他是为劫财而遭谋害的。我想了解一些他的情况，他的日常起居，还有——”

威克姆小姐从椅子上站起身，意味深长地看了看基伦霍尔夫人。

“实情是，”她转向德里尔福德说道，“说来尽管奇怪，基伦霍尔夫人也好，我也好，对阿什顿先生都知之甚少。”

## III / 三

===Original===

Who Was Mr. Ashton?

For the first time since they had entered the room, Drillford turned and glanced at Viner; his look indicated the idea which Miss Wickham’s last words had set up in his mind. Here was a mystery! The police instinct was aroused by it.

“You don’t know very much about Mr. Ashton?” he said, turning back to the two ladies. “Yet﻿—you’re under his roof? This is his house, isn’t it?”

“Just so,” assented Miss Wickham. “But when I say we don’t know much, I mean what I say. Mrs. Killenhall has only known Mr. Ashton a few weeks, and until two months ago I had not seen Mr. Ashton for twelve years. Therefore, neither of us can know much about him.”

“Would you mind telling me what you do know?” asked Drillford. “We’ve got to know something﻿—who he is, and so on.”

“All that I know is this,” replied Miss Wickham. “My father died in Australia, when I was about six years old. My mother was already dead, and my father left me in charge of Mr. Ashton. He sent me, very soon after my father’s death, to school in England, and there I remained for twelve years. About two months ago Mr. Ashton came to England, took this house, fetched me from school and got Mrs. Killenhall to look after me. Here we’ve all been ever since﻿—and beyond that I know scarcely anything.”

===Chinese===

阿什顿先生究竟是谁？

自从走进这间屋子以来，德里尔福德（Drillford）第一次转过身，瞥了维纳（Viner）一眼；他的神情表明，威克姆（Wickham）小姐最后那句话在他心里勾起了一个念头。这事儿透着蹊跷！警察的直觉被唤醒了。

“你们对阿什顿（Ashton）先生不太了解？”他转回身来，对两位女士说道，“可是——你们住在他的屋檐下？这是他的宅子，对吧？”

“正是这样，”威克姆小姐应道，“不过我说我们不大了解，就是字面上的意思。基伦霍尔（Killenhall）夫人认识阿什顿先生才几个星期；而直到两个月前，我已经整整十二年没有见过阿什顿先生了。所以说，我们两个谁也不可能对他有多少了解。”

“能不能请你们讲讲，你们究竟知道些什么？”德里尔福德问道，“我们总得掌握一些情况——他是什么人，诸如此类。”

“我知道的只有这些，”威克姆小姐答道，“我父亲在澳大利亚去世的时候，我大约六岁。我母亲那时已经不在人世，父亲便把我托付给阿什顿先生照管。父亲去世后不久，他就把我送到英国上学，我在那里一待就是十二年。大约两个月前，阿什顿先生来到英国，住进这幢房子，把我从学校接了出来，又请了基伦霍尔夫人来照应我。打那以后，我们一直住在这里——除此之外，我几乎一无所知。”

===Original===

Drillford looked at the elder lady.

“I know, practically, no more than Miss Wickham has told you,” said Mrs. Killenhall. “Mr. Ashton and I got in touch with each other through his advertisement in the *Morning Post*. We exchanged references, and I came here.”

“Ah!” said Drillford. “And﻿—what might his references be, now?”

“To his bankers, the London and Orient, in Threadneedle Street,” answered Mrs. Killenhall promptly. “And to his solicitors, Crawle, Pawle and Rattenbury, of Bedford Bow.”

“Very satisfactory they were, no doubt, ma’am?” suggested Drillford.

Mrs. Killenhall let her eye run round the appointments of the room.

===Chinese===

德里尔福德望向年长的那位女士。

“实际上，我知道的并不比威克姆小姐告诉您的多，”基伦霍尔夫人说，“阿什顿先生和我是通过他在《晨邮报》（Morning Post）上登的广告结识的。我们互相交换了担保人的信息，随后我就来到了这里。”

“哦！”德里尔福德说，“那么——他的担保人都是哪些呢？”

“他的开户银行，针线街（Threadneedle Street）的伦敦与东方银行（London and Orient），”基伦霍尔夫人立刻答道，“还有他的律师事务所，贝德福德罗（Bedford Bow）的克劳尔、波尔与拉滕伯里（Crawle, Pawle and Rattenbury）。”

“想必那些担保都非常可靠吧，夫人？”德里尔福德问道。

基伦霍尔夫人让目光在屋内的陈设上转了一圈。

===Original===

“Eminently so,” she said dryly. “Mr. Ashton was a very wealthy man.”

Drillford pulled out a pocketbook and entered the names which Mrs. Killenhall had just mentioned.

“The solicitors will be able to tell something,” he murmured as he put the book back. “We’ll communicate with them first thing in the morning. But just two questions before I go. Can you tell me anything about Mr. Ashton’s usual habits? Had he any business? What did he do with his time?”

“He was out a great deal,” said Mrs. Killenhall. “He used to go down to the City. He was often out of an evening. Once, since I came here, he was away for a week in the country﻿—he didn’t say where. He was an active man﻿—always in and out. But he never said much as to where he went.”

“The other question,” said Drillford, “is this: Did he carry much on him in the way of valuables or money? I mean﻿—as a rule?”

===Chinese===

“可靠极了，”她淡淡地说，“阿什顿先生是个非常有钱的人。”

德里尔福德掏出记事本，把基伦霍尔夫人刚才提到的名字一一记了下来。

“那家律师事务所应该能说出些情况，”他把本子收回去时低声说道，“明天一早我们就同他们接洽。不过临走之前，还有两个问题。您能跟我讲讲阿什顿先生的日常习惯吗？他有没有什么营生？他的时间都用来做些什么？”

“他经常出门，”基伦霍尔夫人说，“他常往伦敦城跑。晚上也时常不在家。我来了以后，他有一回到乡下去了整整一个星期——没说去了哪儿。他是个闲不住的人——总是进进出出。可是他去了什么地方，向来不多说。”

“另一个问题，”德里尔福德说，“是这样：他身上平常带的贵重物品或者钱财多不多？我是说——就一般情形而论？”

===Original===

“He wore a very fine gold watch and chain,” answered Mrs. Killenhall; “and as for money﻿—well, he always seemed to have a lot in his purse. And he wore two diamond rings﻿—very fine stones.”

“Just so!” murmured Drillford. “Set upon for the sake of those things, no doubt. Well, ladies, I shall telephone to Crawle’s first thing in the morning, and they’ll send somebody along at once, of course. I’m sorry to have brought you such bad news, but﻿—”

He turned toward the door; Miss Wickham stopped him.

“Will Mr. Ashton’s body be brought here﻿—tonight?” she asked.

“No,” replied Drillford. “It will be taken to the mortuary. If you’ll leave everything to me, I’ll see that you are spared as much as possible. Of course, there’ll have to be an inquest﻿—but you’ll hear all about that tomorrow. Leave things to us and to Mr. Ashton’s solicitors.”

He moved towards the door, and Viner, until then a silent spectator, looked at Miss Wickham, something impelling him to address her instead of Mrs. Killenhall.

===Chinese===

“他戴着一块极好的金怀表，连着金表链，”基伦霍尔夫人答道，“至于钱嘛——他的钱夹里似乎总是装得满满的。他还戴着两枚钻石戒指——都是上好的宝石。”

“这就对了！”德里尔福德喃喃说道，“十有八九，就是冲着这些东西下的手。好了，两位女士，明天一早我就打电话到克劳尔律师事务所，他们自然立刻会派人来。给二位带来这样的噩耗，我很抱歉，不过——”

他转身朝门口走去；威克姆小姐叫住了他。

“阿什顿先生的遗体会送回这里来吗——就在今晚？”她问。

“不会，”德里尔福德答道，“遗体会被送往停尸房。你们若是把一切都交给我来办，我保证尽量让二位少受牵累。当然，验尸官的调查是免不了的——不过相关事宜，明天自会告知你们。把事情交给我们和阿什顿先生的律师吧。”

他朝门口走去。这时，一直沉默旁观的维纳望着威克姆小姐，不知是股什么力量，促使他越过基伦霍尔夫人，径直向她开了口。

===Original===

“I live close by you,” he said. “If there is anything that I can do, or that my aunt Miss Penkridge, who lives with me, can do? Perhaps you will let me call in the morning.”

The girl looked at him steadily and frankly.

“Thank you, Mr. Viner,” she said. “It would be very kind if you would. We’ve no men folk﻿—yes, please do.”

“After breakfast, then,” answered Viner, and went away to join the Inspector, who had walked into the hall.

“What do you think of this matter?” he asked, when they had got outside the house.

“Oh, a very clear and ordinary case enough, Mr. Viner,” replied Drillford. “No mystery about it at all. Here’s this Mr. Ashton been living here some weeks﻿—some fellow, the man, of course, whom you saw running away, has noticed that he was a very rich man and wore expensive jewellery, has watched him, probably knew that he used that passage as a shortcut, and has laid in wait for him and murdered him for what he’d got on him. It wouldn’t take two minutes to do the whole thing. Rings, now! They spoke of diamond rings, in there. Well, I didn’t see any diamond rings on his hands when I looked at his body, and I particularly noticed his hands, to see if there were signs of any struggle. No sir﻿—it’s just a plain case of what used to be called highway robbery and murder. But come round with me to the police-station, Mr. Viner﻿—they’ll have taken him to the mortuary by now, and I should like to hear what our divisional surgeon has to say, and what our people actually found on the body.”

===Chinese===

“我就住在你们近旁，”他说，“若有我能效劳之处，或者与我同住的姨母彭克里奇（Penkridge）小姐能帮上忙的地方——不知可否容我明早过来拜访？”

姑娘坦然而镇定地望着他。

“谢谢您，维纳先生，”她说，“您肯来，真是太好心了。我们家里没有男人——好的，请一定来。”

“那就早饭过后，”维纳答道，随即走开，去同已经踱进门厅的探长会合。

“您怎么看这桩案子？”两人走出宅子后，他问道。

“哦，这案子够清楚、够平常的了，维纳先生，”德里尔福德答道，“毫无神秘可言。这位阿什顿先生在这里住了几个星期——某个家伙，自然就是您看见跑掉的那个，注意到他是个很有钱的人，戴着值钱的首饰，便盯上了他，多半还知道他惯常抄那条小巷当近路，于是埋伏下来，为了他身上的财物把他害了。整件事干下来，用不了两分钟。就说戒指吧！她们方才在屋里提到钻石戒指。可我查验尸体的时候，他手上并没见到什么钻石戒指，我还特意留意了他的双手，看看有没有搏斗的痕迹。没有，先生——这不过是一桩从前所谓拦路抢劫、谋财害命的老套案子。不过请随我到警察局走一趟吧，维纳先生——这会儿他们该把他送到停尸房了，我想听听我们分区法医怎么说，再看看我们的人从尸体上究竟搜出了些什么。”

===Original===

As Viner and the Inspector walked into the police-station, Dr. Cortelyon came out. Drillford stopped him.

“Found out anything more, Doctor?” he asked.

“Nothing beyond what I said at first,” replied Cortelyon. “The man has been stabbed through the heart, from behind, in one particularly well-delivered blow. I should say the murderer had waited for him in that passage, probably knowing his habits. That passage, now﻿—you know it really will have to be seen to! That wretched old lamp in the middle gives no light at all. The wonder is that something of this sort hasn’t occurred before.”

Drillford muttered something about local authorities and property-owners and went forward into an office, motioning Viner to follow. The divisional surgeon was there in conversation with the sergeant whom Drillford had left in charge of the body. “That is something on which I’d stake my professional reputation,” he said. “I’m sure of it.”

“What’s that, Doctor?” asked Drillford. “Something to do with this affair?”

“I was saying that whoever stabbed this unfortunate man had some knowledge of anatomy,” remarked the doctor. “He was killed by one swift blow from a particularly keen-edged, thin-bladed weapon which was driven through his back at the exact spot. You ought to make a minute search behind the walls on either side of that passage﻿—the probability is that the murderer threw his weapon away.”

===Chinese===

维纳和探长走进警察局时，科特利恩（Cortelyon）医生正从里面出来。德里尔福德叫住了他。

“有什么新发现吗，医生？”他问。

“除了我起初说的那些，再没有别的了，”科特利恩答道，“这人是被人从背后一刀刺穿心脏的，那一刀出手又准又狠。依我看，凶手是预先守在那条巷子里等他，多半是摸透了他的习惯。说起那条巷子——您知道，真该好好整治整治了！中间那盏糟糕的老油灯压根儿照不出光来。稀奇的是，这样的事从前竟没有出过。”

德里尔福德咕哝了几句关于地方当局和产业业主的话，径直走进一间办公室，示意维纳跟上。分区法医正在里面，同德里尔福德留下来看守尸体的那个警士说话。“这一点，我敢拿自己的职业声誉担保，”他说，“我确信无疑。”

“指哪一点，医生？”德里尔福德问，“与这案子有关？”

“我是说，刺死这个不幸家伙的人，多少懂些解剖学，”医生说道，“他死于一件刃口极锋利、刀身极薄的凶器——一刀疾刺，从背后分毫不差，正中要害。你们应当把那条巷子两侧墙后的地方细细搜查一遍——凶手多半是把凶器随手抛掉了。”

===Original===

“We’ll do all that, Doctor,” said Drillford. “As to your suggestion﻿—don’t you forget that there are a good many criminals here in London who are regular experts in the use of the knife﻿—I’ve seen plenty of instances of that myself. Now,” he went on, turning to the sergeant, “about that search? What did you find on him?”

The sergeant lifted the lid of a desk and pointed to a sheet of foolscap paper whereon lay certain small articles at which Viner gazed with a sense of strange fascination. A penknife, a small gold matchbox, a gold-mounted pencil-case, some silver coins, a handkerchief, and conspicuous among the rest, a farthing.

“That’s the lot,” said the sergeant, “except another handkerchief, and a pair of gloves in the overcoat, where I’ve left them. Nothing else﻿—no watch, chain, purse or pocketbook. And no rings﻿—but it’s very plain from his fingers that he wore two rings one on each hand, third finger in each case.”

“There you are!” said Drillford with a glance at Viner. “Murdered and robbed﻿—clear case! Now, Mr. Viner, give us as accurate a description as possible of the fellow who ran out of that passage.”

Viner did his best. His recollections were of a young man of about his own age, about his own height and build, somewhat above the medium; it was his impression, he said, that the man was dressed, if not shabbily, at least poorly; he had an impression, too, that the clean-shaven face which he had seen for a brief moment was thin and worn.

“Got any recollection of his exact look?” inquired the Inspector. “That’s a lot to go by.”

===Chinese===

“这些我们都会照办，医生，”德里尔福德说，“至于您的推测——您可别忘了，咱们伦敦这儿有的是罪犯，使起刀来是不折不扣的行家——这样的例子我亲眼见过不少。”他接着转向那警士，“说说搜身的结果吧。你们在他身上找到了什么？”

警士掀开一张书桌的盖板，指着一张大裁纸上摊开的几件小物件——维纳盯着它们，心头涌起一种奇异的痴迷。一把小折刀，一只小小的金质火柴盒，一支镶金铅笔，几枚银币，一方手帕——而在这一堆东西中间，一枚法新小铜币（英国旧币，值四分之一便士）格外扎眼。

“就这些了，”警士说，“另有一方手帕和一副手套，留在他大衣口袋里，我没有动。别的什么都没有——没有怀表，没有表链，没有钱夹，也没有记事本。戒指也没有——不过看他的手指就很清楚，他原先戴着两枚戒指，两只手各一枚，都戴在无名指上。”

“您瞧！”德里尔福德说着，瞥了维纳一眼，“谋财害命——明摆着的！好了，维纳先生，请尽可能准确地描述一下那个从巷子里跑出来的家伙。”

维纳尽了力。据他回忆，那是个年纪与自己相仿的年轻人，身量体格也同自己差不多，属于中等偏上；他说，他的印象是，那人穿得即使算不上褴褛，至少也十分寒酸；他还记得，那张只在刹那间见过的刮得光洁的脸，又瘦又憔悴。

“想得起他的确切长相吗？”探长问道，“凭这些去查，也算有据可循了。”

===Original===

“I’m trying to think,” said Viner. “Yes﻿—I should say he looked to be pretty hard-up. There was a sort of desperate gleam in his eye. And﻿—”

“Take your time,” remarked Drillford. “Anything you can suggest, you know﻿—”

“Well,” replied Viner. “I’d an idea at the moment, and I’ve had it since, that I’d seen this man before. Something in his face was familiar. The only thing I can think of is this: I potter round old bookshops and curiosity-shops a good deal﻿—I may have seen this young fellow on some occasion of that sort.”

“Anyway,” suggested Drillford, glancing over the particulars which he had written down, “you’d know him again if you saw him?”

“Oh, certainly!” asserted Viner. “I should know him anywhere.”

“Then that’s all we need trouble you with now, sir,” said Drillford. “The next business will be﻿—tomorrow.”

===Chinese===

“我正在回想，”维纳说，“是的——我看他那副样子，境况相当窘迫。他眼睛里有一股孤注一掷似的光。还有——”

“慢慢想，”德里尔福德说，“您想起什么，尽管说——”

“是这样，”维纳答道，“当时我就有个念头，事后这念头愈发清楚：我从前见过这个人。他脸上有什么东西让人眼熟。我唯一想得起来的是：我平日爱在旧书铺和古玩铺里闲逛——说不定就是在那种场合见过这个年轻人。”

“不管怎么说，”德里尔福德扫了一眼自己记下的那些细节，问道，“若是再见到他，您认得出来吧？”

“哦，那当然！”维纳斩钉截铁地说，“无论在哪儿，我都能认出他。”

“那么，现在就不必再劳烦您了，先生，”德里尔福德说，“下一步的事——明天再办。”

===Original===

Viner walked slowly out of the police-station and still more slowly homeward. When he reached the first lamp, he drew out his watch. Half-past twelve! Just two hours ago he had been in his own comfortable library, smiling at Miss Penkridge’s ideas about the very matters into one of which he was now plunged. He would not have been surprised if he had suddenly awoke, to find that all this was a bad dream, induced by the evening’s conversation. But just then he came to the passage in which the murder had been committed. A policeman was on guard at the terrace end﻿—and Viner, rather than hear any more of the matter, hastened past him and made a circuitous way to Markendale Square.

He let himself into his house as quietly as possible, and contrary to taste and custom, went into the dining-room, switched on the electric light and helped himself to a stiff glass of brandy and soda at the sideboard. When the mixture was duly prepared, he forgot to drink it. He stood by the sideboard, the glass in his hand, his eyes staring at vacancy. Nor did he move when a very light foot stole down the stairs, and Miss Penkridge, in wraps and curl-papers, looked round the side of the door.

“Heavens above, Richard!” she exclaimed, “What is the matter! I wondered if you were burglars! Half-past twelve!”

Viner suddenly became aware of the glass which he was unconsciously holding. He lifted it to his lips, wondering whatever it was that made his mouth feel so dry. And when he had taken a big gulp, and then spoke, his voice﻿—to himself﻿—sounded just as queer as his tongue had been feeling.

“You were right!” he said suddenly. “There are queerer, stranger affairs in life than one fancies! And I﻿—I’ve been pitchforked﻿—thrown﻿—clean into the middle of things! I!”

Miss Penkridge came closer to him, staring. She looked from him to the glass, from the glass to him.

===Chinese===

维纳缓步走出警察局，回家的一路走得愈发慢。走到第一盏路灯下，他掏出怀表。十二点半！就在两个钟头之前，他还坐在自己那间舒适的书房里，为彭克里奇小姐关于这类事情的念头发笑——而此刻，他自己竟一头栽进了其中一桩里。倘若他猛然惊醒，发现这一切不过是晚间谈话引出的一场噩梦，他也不会有半分诧异。可就在这时，他走到了那条出了凶案的巷子。一名警察守在巷子临街的一头——维纳不愿再听到关于此案的任何动静，便加快脚步从他身旁走过，绕了条远路，回到马肯代尔广场（Markendale Square）。

他尽量悄没声儿地开了门进屋，一反自己的脾性与习惯，走进餐室，拧亮电灯，在餐具柜前给自己兑上浓浓的一大杯白兰地苏打。酒兑好了，他却忘了喝。他站在餐具柜旁，手里擎着酒杯，两眼直愣愣地望着虚空。楼梯上有极轻的脚步悄悄下来，彭克里奇小姐裹着外衣、头上还别着卷发纸，从门边探进头来，他仍旧一动不动。

“老天爷，理查德！”她惊呼道，“出什么事了！我方才还当是进了贼呢！都十二点半了！”

维纳这才猛然醒觉，自己手里还无意识地擎着那只酒杯。他把杯子举到唇边，心里纳闷，不知是什么缘故，嘴里干得这么厉害。待到灌下一大口、再开口说话时——他的嗓音，在他自己听来——同他舌头上一直以来的感觉一样古怪。

“你说对了！”他突然说道，“人生里有些事情，比人所料想的更离奇、更古怪！而我——我竟被人一把——甩了进来——结结实实甩进了万事正中央！我！”

彭克里奇小姐走近他，直盯盯地瞧着。她的目光从他移到酒杯上，又从酒杯移回他身上。

===Original===

“No﻿—I haven’t been drinking,” said Viner with a harsh laugh. “I’m drinking now, and I’m going to have another, too. Listen!”

He pushed her gently into a chair, and seating himself on the edge of the table, told her the adventure. And Miss Penkridge, who was an admirable listener to fictitious tales of horror, proved herself no less admirable in listening to one of plain fact, and made no comment until her nephew had finished.

“That poor man!” she said at last. “Such a fine, strong, healthy-looking man, too! I used to wonder about him, when I saw him in the square, I used to think of him as somebody who’d seen things!”

Viner made a sudden grimace.

“Don’t!” he said. “Ugh! I’ve seen things tonight that I never wished to see! And I wish﻿—”

“What?” demanded Miss Penkridge after a pause, during which Viner had sat staring at the floor.

===Chinese===

“不——我没有喝酒，”维纳干笑一声，说道，“我这会儿才在喝，而且待会儿还要再来一杯。你听着！”

他轻轻把她让进一把椅子，自己坐在桌沿上，把这番遭遇讲给她听。彭克里奇小姐听虚构的恐怖故事时是位顶出色的听众，听一桩确凿的真事时也毫不逊色，直到外甥讲完，始终未置一词。

“那可怜的人！”她终于开口，“那样一个健壮、精神、看着就叫人痛快的人！我从前在广场上见到他，还常常琢磨他的来历，总把他当成一个见过世面的人！”

维纳猛地做了个苦脸。

“别说了！”他说，“呃！今晚我见到的事，是我从来不曾想见的！我只愿——”

“只愿什么？”彭克里奇小姐停了片刻，问道——这当儿，维纳一直坐着，两眼盯着地板。

===Original===

“I wish to God I’d never seen that poor devil who was running away!” exclaimed Viner with sudden passion. “They’ll catch him, and I shall have to give evidence against him, and my evidence’ll hang him, and﻿—”

“There’s a lot to do, and a lot’ll happen before that comes off, Richard,” interrupted Miss Penkridge. “The man may be innocent.”

“He’d have a nice job to prove it!” said Viner with a forced laugh. “No, if the police get him﻿—besides, he was running straight from the place! Isn’t it a queer thing?” he went on, laughing again. “I don’t mind remembering the﻿—the dead man, but I hate the recollection of that chap hurrying away! I wonder what it feels like when you’ve just murdered another fellow, to slink off like﻿—”

“You’ve no business to be wondering any such thing!” said Miss Penkridge sharply. “Here﻿—get yourself another brandy and soda, and let us talk business. These two women﻿—did they feel it much?”

“They puzzled me,” replied Viner. He took his aunt’s advice about the extra glass, and obeyed her, too, when she silently pointed to a box of cigars which lay on the sideboard. “All right,” he said after a minute or two. “I’m not going to have nerves. What was I saying? They puzzled me? Yes, puzzled. Especially the girl; she seemed so collected about everything. And yet, according to her own story, she’s only just out of the schoolroom. You’ll go round there with me?”

“If we can be of any service to them? certainly,” assented Miss Penkridge.

===Chinese===

“上帝作证，我宁愿从没见过那个逃跑的可怜家伙！”维纳突然激动地喊道，“他们会抓住他的，我就得出来作证指控他，而我的证词会把他送上绞架，还有——”

“到那一步之前，还有的是事要办，有的是变故要发生呢，理查德，”彭克里奇小姐打断他，“那人也许是清白的。”

“他想证明自己清白，可有的忙呢！”维纳强笑着说，“不，警察一旦抓到他——况且，他是从那地方一路直跑出来的！这事儿怪不怪？”他又笑着往下说，“想起那位——那位死者，我倒不觉得什么，可一想到那家伙急急逃走的样子，我就打心底里厌恶！不知道一个人刚刚害了另一条性命、像那样偷偷溜走的时候，心里是什么滋味——”

“这种事，轮不着你去瞎琢磨！”彭克里奇小姐厉声说道，“来——再去给自己倒一杯白兰地苏打，咱们说正经的。那两位女士——她们很受震动吗？”

“她们让我摸不着头脑，”维纳答道。关于再添一杯，他听从了姨母的主意；她默默指了指餐具柜上放着的一盒雪茄，他也一并从命。“好吧，”过了一两分钟，他说，“我不会闹神经紧张的。我刚才说到哪儿了？她们让我摸不着头脑？对，摸不着头脑。尤其是那姑娘；她对什么都显得那么镇定自若。可是照她自己讲的，她才刚刚跨出校门。你明天肯同我一起去拜访吗？”

“只要我们能帮上她们的忙？那自然去，”彭克里奇小姐应道。

===Original===

“The girl said they’d no men folk,” remarked Viner.

“In that case I shall certainly go,” said Miss Penkridge. “Now, Richard, smoke your cigar, and think no more about all this till tomorrow.”

Viner flung himself into an easy-chair.

“All right!” he said. “Don’t bother! It’s been a bit of a facer, but﻿—”

He was astonished when he woke the next morning, much later than was his wont, to find that he had not dreamed about the events of the midnight. And he was his usual practical and cool-headed self when, at eleven o’clock, he stood waiting in the hall for Miss Penkridge to go round with him to number seven. But the visit was not to be paid just then﻿—as they were about to leave the house, a police-officer came hurrying up and accosted Viner. Inspector Drillford’s compliments, and would Mr. Viner come round? And then the messenger gave a knowing grin.

“We’ve got the man, sir!” he whispered. “That’s why you’re wanted.”

===Chinese===

“那姑娘说，她们家里没有男人，”维纳说。

“那样的话，我一定去，”彭克里奇小姐说，“好了，理查德，抽你的雪茄吧，明天之前，别再想这些事了。”

维纳一屁股陷进安乐椅里。

“好吧！”他说，“不用操心！这事情是有点叫人发懵，不过——”

第二天早晨他醒来时，比平日晚了许多，却发现自己竟没有梦见昨夜的种种变故，不由得暗自称奇。十一点钟，他站在门厅里，等彭克里奇小姐同他一道去七号宅，那时他又恢复了平日那个讲求实际、头脑冷静的自己。可这一趟拜访偏偏没能立刻成行——他们正要出门，一名警官匆匆赶上前来，向维纳搭话：德里尔福德探长向您致意，可否请维纳先生移步一叙？说罢，那传话的警员露出了心照不宣的一笑。

“我们抓到那个人了，先生！”他压低声音说，“所以才要请您去一趟。”

## IV / 四

===Original===

The Ring and the Knife

Viner was hoping that the police had got hold of the wrong man as he reluctantly walked into Drillford’s office, but one glance at the inspector’s confident face, alert and smiling, showed him that Drillford himself had no doubts on that point.

“Well, Mr. Viner,” he said with a triumphant laugh, “we haven’t been so long about it, you see! Much quicker work than I’d anticipated, too.”

“Are you sure you’ve got the right man?” asked Viner. “I mean﻿—have you got the man I saw running away from the passage?”

“You shall settle that yourself,” answered Drillford. “Come this way.”

===Chinese===

戒指与刀

维纳（Viner）走进德里尔福德（Drillford）的办公室时，心里还怀着一线希望，但愿警方抓错了人；可他一眼瞥见这位督察机警而含笑的自信面孔，便明白德里尔福德本人对此毫无怀疑。

“嘿，维纳先生，”他得意地笑道，“您瞧，我们没费多久工夫吧！比我自己预料的还快得多。”

“你肯定抓对了人？”维纳问，“我是说——你们抓到的，是我看见从过道里跑出去的那个人吗？”

“这个由您自己来认定，”德里尔福德答道，“这边请。”

===Original===

He led Viner down a corridor, through one or two locked doors, and motioning him to tread softly, drew back a sliding panel in the door of a cell and silently pointed. Viner, with a worse sickness than before, stole up and looked through the barred opening. One glance at the man sitting inside the cell, white-faced, staring at the drab, bare wall, was enough; he turned to Drillford and nodded. Drillford nodded too, and led him back to the office.

“That’s the man I saw,” said Viner.

“Of course!” assented Drillford. “I’d no doubt of it. Well, it’s been a far simpler thing than I’d dared to hope. I’ll tell you how we got him. This morning, about ten o’clock, this chap, who won’t give his name, went into the pawnbroker’s shop in Edgware Road, and asked for a loan on a diamond ring which he produced. Now, Pelver, who happened to attend to him himself, is a good deal of an expert in diamonds﻿—he’s a jeweller as well as a pawnbroker, and he saw at once that the diamond in this ring was well worth all of a thousand pounds﻿—a gem of the first water! He was therefore considerably astonished when his customer asked for a loan of ten pounds on it﻿—still more so when the fellow suggested that Pelver should buy it outright for twenty-five. Pelver asked him some questions as to his property in the ring﻿—he made some excuses about its having been in his family for some time, and that he would be glad to realize on it. Under pretence of examining it, Pelver took the ring to another part of his shop and quietly sent for a policeman. And the end was, this officer brought the man here, and Pelver with him, and the ring. Here it is!”

He opened a safe and produced a diamond ring at which Viner stared with feelings for which he could scarcely account.

“How do you know that’s one of Mr. Ashton’s rings?” he asked.

===Chinese===

他领着维纳沿一条走廊走去，穿过一两道上锁的门，示意他放轻脚步，随后拉开牢房门上的一扇活动小窗板，默然往里一指。维纳只觉心头那阵翻搅比先前更甚，蹑手蹑脚地凑上前，从装着栅栏的窗口望进去。只消一眼，便看清那人坐在牢房里，脸色惨白，怔怔地瞪着灰秃秃的光墙——这一眼就够了；他转向德里尔福德，点了点头。德里尔福德也点点头，领他回到办公室。

“我看见的就是这个人。”维纳说。

“那还用说！”德里尔福德深表赞同，“我本就毫不怀疑。嘿，这事办得比我敢指望的还顺利得多。我告诉您我们是怎么抓到他的。今天上午十点钟光景，这个死活不肯报出姓名的家伙走进埃奇韦尔路（Edgware Road）的一家当铺，取出一只钻戒，想抵押一笔钱。接待他的恰巧是店主佩尔弗（Pelver）本人；此人在钻石上头颇有眼力——他既开当铺，又做珠宝生意，一眼便看出这只戒指上的钻石足值一千英镑——是颗头等水色的珍品！因此，听到顾客开口只押借十英镑，他着实吃了一惊；待那家伙又提议，索性请佩尔弗出二十五英镑把戒指整个买断，他就更觉诧异了。佩尔弗盘问了他几句戒指的来历——他支支吾吾，说这物件在他家已有些年头，如今情愿折成现钱。佩尔弗借口验货，把戒指拿到铺子另一头，悄悄叫人去请警察。结果便是，那位警察把人带到了这里，佩尔弗连同戒指也一并带到。喏，就是它！”

他打开保险柜，取出一只钻戒；维纳盯着它瞧，心头涌起一股连自己也说不明白的滋味。

“你怎么知道这只戒指是阿什顿（Ashton）先生的？”他问。

===Original===

“Oh, I soon solved that!” laughed Drillford. “I hurried round to Markendale Square with it at once. Both the ladies recognized it﻿—Mr. Ashton had often shown it to them, and told them its value, and there’s a private mark of his inside it. And so we arrested him, and there he is! Clear case!”

“What did he say?” asked Viner.

“He’s a curious customer,” replied Drillford. “I should say that whatever he is now, he has been a gentleman. He was extremely nervous and so on while we were questioning him about the ring, but when it came to the crucial point, and I charged him and warned him, he turned strangely cool. I’ll tell you what he said, in his exact words. ‘I’m absolutely innocent of that!’ he said. ‘But I can see that I’ve placed myself in a very strange position.’ And after that he would say no more﻿—he hasn’t even asked to see a solicitor.”

“What will be done next?” asked Viner.

“He’ll be brought before the magistrate in an hour or two,” said Drillford. “Formal proceedings﻿—for a remand, you know. I shall want you there, Mr. Viner; it won’t take long. I wish the fellow would tell us who he is.”

===Chinese===

“哦，这个我很快便弄清了！”德里尔福德笑道，“我揣上戒指，立刻赶到马肯代尔广场（Markendale Square）。两位女士都认出了它——阿什顿先生常拿给她们看，还讲过它的价值，戒指内侧有他本人的一处私记。于是我们便逮捕了他，他就在那儿！案子再清楚不过！”

“他说了些什么？”维纳问。

“他是个古怪的主顾，”德里尔福德答道，“依我看，不管他如今沦落到什么田地，从前总归是个体面人。我们盘问戒指的事时，他紧张得不行；可一到紧要关头，我对他提出控告、履行警诫，他反倒出奇地镇定下来。我把他的原话告诉您。‘这件事我全然无辜！’他说，‘不过我看得出，我已经把自己摆进了十分离奇的处境。’此后他便再不肯多说一个字——连要求见律师都没有。”

“接下来怎么处置？”维纳问。

“一两个钟头之内，他就得被带到治安法官跟前，”德里尔福德说，“是例行程序——申请还押，您懂的。届时我要请您到场，维纳先生；费不了多少工夫。只盼这家伙肯说出自己是谁。”

===Original===

“And I wish I could remember where and when I have seen him before!” exclaimed Viner.

“Ah, that’s still your impression?” remarked Drillford. “You’re still convinced of it?”

“More than ever﻿—since seeing him just now,” affirmed Viner. “I know his face, but that’s all I can say. I suppose,” he continued, looking diffidently at the inspector, as if he half-expected to be laughed at for the suggestion he was about to make, “I suppose you don’t believe that this unfortunate fellow may have some explanation of his possession of Mr. Ashton’s ring?”

Drillford, who had been replacing the ring in a safe, locked the door upon it with a snap, and turned on his questioner with a look which became more and more businesslike and official with each succeeding word.

“Now, Mr. Viner,” he said, “you look at it from our point of view. An elderly gentleman is murdered and robbed. A certain man is seen﻿—by you, as it happens﻿—running away as fast as he can from the scene of the murder. Next morning that very man is found trying to get rid of a ring which, without doubt, was taken from the murdered man’s finger. What do you think? Or﻿—another question﻿—what could we, police officials, do?”

===Chinese===

“我倒巴不得能想起来，先前究竟在哪儿、什么时候见过他！”维纳脱口叹道。

“啊，您还抱着那个印象？”德里尔福德道，“您仍然笃信不疑？”

“比先前更笃定了——就因方才看了他一眼，”维纳断言，“我认得那张脸，可也仅此而已。我想，”他接着说，一面迟疑地望着督察，仿佛自觉即将出口的建议多半要招人讪笑，“我想，你不会相信这个倒霉家伙握着阿什顿先生的戒指，也许自有他的一番解释吧？”

德里尔福德正把戒指放回保险柜，咔哒一声上了锁，随即转过脸来对着发问的人，那副神气随着一句接一句说下去，越来越是公事公办、官腔十足。

“这么说吧，维纳先生，”他开腔道，“您得站在我们的立场上看。一位上了年纪的绅士遭人谋杀抢劫。有人看见某个男人——恰好让您撞见——从凶案现场没命奔逃。次日早晨，正是这个男人被人发现正要脱手一只戒指，而它毫无疑问是从死者指头上取下来的。您作何感想？或者——换个问法——我们这些警务人员，还能怎么做？”

===Original===

“Nothing but what you’re doing, I suppose,” said Viner. “Still﻿—there may be a good deal that’s﻿—what shall I say?﻿—behind all this.”

“It’s for him to speak,” observed Drillford, nodding in the direction of the cells. “He’s got a bell within reach of his fingers; he’s only got to ring it and to ask for me or any solicitor he likes to name. But﻿—we shall see!”

Nothing had been seen or heard, in the way hinted at by Drillford, when, an hour later, Viner, waiting in the neighbouring police-court, was aware that the humdrum, sordid routine was about to be interrupted by something unusual. The news of an arrest in connection with the Lonsdale Passage murder had somehow leaked out, and the court was packed to the doors﻿—Viner himself had gradually been forced into a corner near the witness-box in which he was to make an unwilling appearance. And from that corner he looked with renewed interest at the man who was presently placed in the dock, and for the hundredth time asked himself what it was in his face that woke some chord of memory in him.

There was nothing of the criminal in the accused man’s appearance. Apparently about thirty years of age, spare of figure, clean-shaven, of a decidedly intellectual type of countenance, he looked like an actor. His much-worn suit of tweed was well cut and had evidently been carefully kept, in spite of its undoubtedly threadbare condition. It, and the worn and haggard look of the man’s face, denoted poverty, if not recent actual privation, and the thought was present in more than one mind there in possession of certain facts: if this man had really owned the ring which he had offered to the pawnbroker, why had he delayed so long in placing himself in funds through its means? For if his face expressed anything, it was hunger.

Viner, who was now witnessing police-court proceedings for the first time in his life, felt an almost morbid curiosity in hearing the tale unfolded against the prisoner. For some reason, best known to themselves, the police brought forward more evidence than was usual on first proceedings before a magistrate. Viner himself proved the finding of the body; the divisional surgeon spoke as to the cause of death; the dead man’s solicitor testified to his identity and swore positively as to the ring; the pawnbroker gave evidence as to the prisoner’s attempt to pawn or sell the ring that morning. Finally, the police proved that on searching the prisoner after his arrest, a knife was found in his hip-pocket which, in the opinion of the divisional surgeon, would have caused the wound found in the dead man’s body. From a superficial aspect, no case could have seemed clearer.

===Chinese===

“我想，除了照目前的办法，也别无他途，”维纳说，“不过——这背后也许大有——怎么说好呢？——隐情。”

“话得由他自己开口，”德里尔福德说着，朝牢房那边点了点头，“他手指够得着的地方就有铃；只要按铃，指名要见我，或是任一位他愿意指名的律师，都由得他。不过——咱们瞧着吧！”

德里尔福德话里暗示的那种情形，直到一个钟头后也毫无动静——既未见着，也未听着。维纳正在隔壁的警务法庭里等候，觉察出这套枯燥龌龊的例行公事眼看要被一桩不寻常的事打断。朗斯代尔过道（Lonsdale Passage）凶案有人被捕的消息不知怎么泄漏了出去，法庭里挤得水泄不通——维纳自己也渐渐被挤到证人席近旁的一个角落，而他还要老大不情愿地到那席位上露面。就从那个角落，他重新怀着兴味打量那个旋即被押上被告席的男人，第一百次问自己：他脸上究竟是哪一点，拨动了记忆里的某根心弦？

被告的外表没有半分罪犯气象。年纪约莫三十上下，身形清瘦，脸刮得干净，面相分明属于颇有智识的一类，像是个做演员的。他那身磨损得厉害的斜纹呢衣裳剪裁合身，虽然无疑已经磨得露了线，却显然一向经心爱惜。这身衣裳，加上他脸上那种憔悴枯槁的神色，都透着贫穷，近期说不定还真挨过饥；在场知晓若干内情的人心里，不止一个转着这样的念头：这人若真拥有他拿到当铺去的那只戒指，何以迟迟不靠它弄几个钱救急？因为他那张脸若能表明什么，表明的便是饥饿。

维纳平生头一遭见识警务法庭的审理，听着一桩桩不利于被告的证词铺陈开来，心里生出近乎病态的好奇。不知出于什么缘故——只有他们自己心里最有数——警方提出的证据，比治安法官首次庭审的惯例为多。维纳本人出庭证明发现尸体一事；分区法医就死因作了陈述；死者的律师出面证明其身份，并斩钉截铁地宣誓认明那只戒指；当铺老板就被告当天上午企图典当或出售戒指一事作证。最后，警方证明被告被捕后经搜身，在其后裤袋里搜出一柄刀，而按分区法医的看法，这柄刀足以造成死者身上那处创伤。从表面看，再没有比这更明白的案子了。

===Original===

But in Viner’s reckoning of things there was mystery. Two episodes occurred during the comparatively brief proceedings which made him certain that all was not being brought out. The first was when he himself went into the witness-box to prove his discovery of the body and to swear that the prisoner was the man he had seen running away from the passage. The accused glanced at him with evident curiosity as he came forward; on hearing Viner’s name, he looked at him in a strange manner, changed colour and turned his head away. But when a certain question was put to Viner, he looked round again, evidently anxious to hear the answer.

“I believe you thought, on first seeing him, that the prisoner’s face was familiar to you, Mr. Viner?”

“Yes﻿—I certainly think that I have seen him before, somewhere.”

“You can’t recollect more? You don’t know when or where you saw him?”

“I don’t. But that I have seen him, perhaps met him, somewhere, I am certain.”

===Chinese===

但在维纳的盘算里，此事处处透着神秘。这场为时不长的审理中先后出了两段插曲，叫他确信内情并未和盘托出。第一段，是他本人走进证人席，证明自己发现了尸体，并宣誓指认被告正是他看见从过道里跑掉的那个男人。他走上前去时，被告带着显而易见的好奇打量他；一听见维纳的名字，那人便神情古怪地瞅了他一眼，变了脸色，把头掉开。可当某个问题问到维纳头上时，他又回过头来，分明急于听到答复。

“我想，您起初见到他时，是觉得被告这张脸似曾相识吧，维纳先生？”

“是的——我确实认为，从前在什么地方见过他。”

“您再回想不起更多了？您不知道是在何时何地见过他？”

“想不起。但我见过他，说不定还在哪儿同他照过面，这一点我确信不疑。”

===Original===

This induced the magistrate to urge the accused man﻿—who had steadfastly refused to give name or address﻿—to reveal his identity. But the prisoner only shook his head.

“I would rather not give my name at present,” he answered. “I am absolutely innocent of this charge of murder, but I quite realize that the police are fully justified in bringing it against me. I had nothing whatever to do with Mr. Ashton’s death﻿—nothing! Perhaps the police will find out the truth; and meanwhile I had rather not give my name.”

“You will be well advised to reconsider that,” said the magistrate. “If you are innocent, as you say, it will be far better for you to say who you are, and to see a solicitor. As things are, you are in a very dangerous position.”

But the prisoner shook his head.

“Not yet, at any rate,” he answered. “I want to hear more.”

===Chinese===

这一来，治安法官便敦促被告——此人始终执意不肯说出姓名住址——交代自己的身份。可被告只是摇头。

“眼下我宁可不说出姓名，”他答道，“这桩谋杀指控加在我身上，我全然无辜；不过我心里很明白，警方这样指控我，是完全有凭据的。阿什顿先生的死，我毫无干系——毫无干系！真相如何，警方或许自会查个水落石出；在此期间，我不愿报出名字。”

“你还是三思为妙，”治安法官说，“你若果真无辜，如你自己所言，那就远宜说出你是何人，并去见一位律师。照眼下的情形，你的处境危险得很。”

可被告摇了摇头。

“至少眼下还不，”他答道，“我还想再多听一些。”

===Original===

When the proceedings were over and the accused, formally remanded for a week, had been removed to the cells previous to being taken away, Viner went round to Drillford’s office.

“Look here!” he said abruptly, finding the Inspector alone, “I dare say you think I’m very foolish, but I don’t believe that chap murdered Ashton. I don’t believe it for one second!”

Drillford who was filling up some papers, smiled.

“No?” he said. “Now, why, Mr. Viner?”

“You can call it intuition if you like,” answered Viner. “But I don’t! And I shall be surprised if I’m not right. There are certain things that I should think would strike you.”

===Chinese===

审理完毕，被告被正式还押候审一星期，先押回牢房听候解走；维纳便绕到德里尔福德的办公室去。

“听我说！”他见督察独自在屋里，便劈头说道，“你多半觉得我蠢得很，可我不信那家伙杀了阿什顿。一秒钟都不信！”

德里尔福德正填着几份表格，笑了笑。

“不信？”他说，“那么，为什么呢，维纳先生？”

“你乐意的话，尽可管它叫直觉，”维纳答道，“可我不这么叫！要是我料得不准，那才奇怪呢。有几桩事，我原以为总该叫你心里一动。”

===Original===

“What, for instance?” asked Drillford.

“Do you think it likely that a man who must have known that a regular hue and cry would be raised about that murder, would be such a fool as to go and offer one of the murdered man’s rings within a mile of the spot where the murder took place?” asked Viner.

Drillford turned and looked steadily at his questioner.

“Well, but that’s precisely what he did, Mr. Viner!” he exclaimed. “There’s no doubt whatever that the ring in question was Ashton’s; there’s also no doubt that this man did offer it to Pelver this morning. Either the fellow is a fool or singularly ignorant, to do such a mad thing! But﻿—he did it! And I know why.”

“Why, then?” demanded Viner.

===Chinese===

“比如说？”德里尔福德问。

“一个必定料到那桩凶案会闹得满城风雨的人，你说他会蠢到那步田地，竟跑到离凶案现场不出一英里的地方，拿死者的一只戒指去变卖？”维纳问。

德里尔福德转过身，定睛望着发问的人。

“嘿，可他干的恰恰就是这个，维纳先生！”他扬声道，“那只戒指是阿什顿的，这一点毫无疑义；这人今天上午确实把它拿给了佩尔弗，这也毫无疑义。干出这种发疯的事来，这家伙不是个蠢材，便是懵懂得出奇！不过——他反正是干了！而且我知道为什么。”

“那么，为什么？”维纳追问。

===Original===

“Because he was just starving,” answered Drillford. “When he was brought in here, straight from Pelver’s, he hadn’t a halfpenny on him, and in the very thick of my questionings﻿—and just think how important they were!﻿—he stopped me. ‘May I say a word that’s just now much more important to me than all this?’ he said. ‘I’m starving! I haven’t touched food or drink for nearly three days. Give me something, if it’s only a crust of bread!’ That’s fact, Mr. Viner.”

“What did you do?” inquired Viner.

“Got the poor chap some breakfast, at once,” answered Drillford, “and let him alone till he’d finished. Have you ever seen a starved dog eat? No﻿—well, I have, and he ate like that﻿—he was ravenous! And when a man’s at that stage, do you think he’s going to stop at anything? Not he! This fellow, you may be sure, after killing and robbing Ashton, had but one thought﻿—how soon he could convert some of the property into cash, so that he could eat. If Pelver had made him that advance, or bought the ring, he’d have made a beeline for the nearest coffee-shop. I tell you he was mad for food!”

“Another thing,” said Viner. “Where is the rest of Mr. Ashton’s property﻿—his watch, chain, the other ring, his purse, and﻿—wasn’t there a pocketbook? How is it this man wasn’t found in possession of them?”

“Easy enough for him to hide all those things, Mr. Viner,” said Drillford, with an indulgent smile. “What easier? You don’t know as much of these things as I do﻿—he could quite easily plant all those articles safely during the night. He just stuck to the article which he could most easily convert into money.”

===Chinese===

“因为他正是饿极了，”德里尔福德答道，“把他从佩尔弗铺子里径直带进来的时候，他身上连半个便士也没有。我盘问得正紧——您想想那些问话何等要紧！——他却把我拦住了。‘我可以说一句眼下对我比这一切要紧得多的话吗？’他说，‘我快饿死了！快三天了，我水米没沾牙。给我点吃的吧，哪怕一片面包皮也行！’这是实情，维纳先生。”

“那你怎么办？”维纳问。

“立刻给那可怜的家伙弄了份早饭，”德里尔福德答道，“然后由着他，等他吃个干净。您见过饿瘪了的狗吃东西吗？没见过——嘿，我可见过，他那吃相正是一样——简直是狼吞虎咽！一个人到了那步田地，您还指望他有所不为吗？才不呢！这家伙，你只管放心，杀了阿什顿、抢了财物之后，心里只有一个念头——多快能把一部分财物换成现钱，好去吃上一顿。佩尔弗若是那笔钱贷给了他，或是把戒指买了下来，他准会一路直奔最近的一家咖啡馆。我跟您说，他为了吃的都快发疯了！”

“还有一件事，”维纳说，“阿什顿先生其余的财物都在哪儿——他的怀表、表链、另一只戒指、钱夹，还有——不是还有个记事本吗？怎么这人身上一件也没搜出来？”

“要把那些东西藏起来，对他还不容易，维纳先生，”德里尔福德带着纵容的微笑说，“还有比这更容易的吗？这种事您知道的没我多——夜里他尽可以把那些物件稳稳当当安置妥帖。他单留下那件最容易变成钱的东西罢了。”

===Original===

“Well, I don’t believe he’s guilty,” repeated Viner. “And I want to do something for him. You may think me quixotic, but I’d like to help him. Is there anything to prevent you from going to him, telling him that I’m convinced of his innocence and that I should like to get him help﻿—legal help?”

“There’s nothing to prevent it, to be sure,” answered Drillford. “But Mr. Viner, you can’t get over the fact that this fellow had Ashton’s diamond ring in his possession!”

“How do I﻿—how do you﻿—know how he came into possession of it?” demanded Viner.

“And then﻿—that knife!” exclaimed Drillford. “Look here! I’ve got it. What sort of thing is that for an innocent, harmless man to carry about him? It’s an American bowie-knife!”

He opened a drawer and exhibited a weapon which, lying on a pile of paper, looked singularly suggestive and fearsome.

===Chinese===

“反正我不信他有罪，”维纳又说了一遍，“而且我要为他出点力。你尽可以笑我堂吉诃德，可我愿意帮他。有什么能拦住你到他跟前去，告诉他：我确信他清白，我愿意给他寻些帮助——法律上的援手？”

“要说拦，自然没什么可拦的，”德里尔福德答道，“可是维纳先生，这个事实您绕不过去：这家伙手里握着阿什顿的钻戒！”

“我怎么知道——你又怎么知道——那戒指是怎么落到他手里的？”维纳质问。

“还有——那把刀！”德里尔福德嚷道，“瞧这儿！我拿来了。一个清白无害的人，随身带的该是这种物件吗？这可是一把美式鲍伊猎刀！”

他拉开一个抽屉，出示一件凶器；它躺在一叠纸上，瞧着格外意味深长、狰狞可怖。

===Original===

“I don’t care!” said Viner with a certain amount of stubbornness. “I’m convinced that the man didn’t kill Ashton. And I want to help him. I’m a man of considerable means; and in this case﻿—well, that’s how I feel about it.”

Drillford made no answer. But presently he left the room, after pointing Viner to a chair. Viner waited﻿—five, ten minutes. Then the door opened again, and Drillford came back. Behind him walked the accused man, with a couple of policemen in attendance upon him.

“There, Mr. Viner!” said Drillford. “You can speak to him yourself!”

Viner rose from his chair. The prisoner stepped forward, regarding him earnestly.

“Viner!” he said, in a low, concentrated tone, “don’t you know me? I’m Langton Hyde! You and I were at Rugby together. And﻿—we meet again, here!”

===Chinese===

“我不管这些！”维纳带着几分执拗说，“我坚信这人没有杀阿什顿。我要帮他。我是个颇有些家产的人；而这一桩案子——嘿，我的心意就是如此。”

德里尔福德没有答话。不多时，他指给维纳一把椅子，便出了房间。维纳等着——五分钟，十分钟。随后门又开了，德里尔福德走了回来。他身后跟着被告，有两名警察随押。

“喏，维纳先生！”德里尔福德说，“您可以亲自同他讲了！”

维纳从椅子里站起身。犯人跨步上前，恳切地注视着他。

“维纳！”他用低沉而紧绷的声调说，“你认不出我了吗？我是兰顿·海德（Langton Hyde）！你我在拉格比（Rugby）是同窗。而——你我竟在这里重逢！”

## V / 五

===Original===

Look for That Man!

At these words Viner drew back with an exclamation of astonishment, but in the next instant he stepped forward again, holding out his hand.

“Hyde!” he said. “Then﻿—that’s what I remembered! Of course I know you! But good heavens, man, what does all this mean? What’s brought you to this﻿—to be here, in this place?”

The prisoner looked round at his captors, and back at Viner, and smiled as a man smiles who is beginning to realize hopelessness to the full.

“I don’t know if I’m allowed to speak,” he said.

Drillford, who had been watching this episode with keen attention, motioned to the two policemen.

===Chinese===

去找那个人！
