听了这话，维纳惊呼一声，倒退了一步，可转眼间他又抢上前来，朝对方伸出了手。

“海德（Hyde）！”他说，“这么说——我就是想起了这个！我当然认得你！可是天哪，老兄，这到底是怎么回事？你怎么落到这步田地——落到这里，待在这个地方？”

囚犯环顾左右押解他的人，又回头望望维纳，脸上浮起一丝笑意，那是一个开始彻底明白自己再无指望之人才有的笑。

“我不知道他们准不准我开口。”他说。

德里尔福德一直在全神贯注地留意这一幕，这时朝两名警察摆了摆手。

===Original===

“Wait outside,” he said abruptly. “Now, then,” he continued when he, Viner and Hyde were alone, “this man can say anything he likes to you, Mr. Viner, so long as you’ve asked to see him. This is all irregular, but I’ve no wish to stop him from telling you whatever he pleases. But remember,” he went on, glancing at the prisoner, “you’re saying it before me﻿—and in my opinion, you’d a deal better have said something when you were in court just now.”

“I didn’t know what to say,” replied Hyde doubtfully. “I’m pretty much on the rocks, as you can guess; but﻿—I have relatives! And if it’s possible, I don’t want them to know about this.”

Drillford looked at Viner and shook his head, as if to signify his contempt of Hyde’s attitude.

“Considering the position you’re in,” he said, turning again to Hyde, “you must see that it’s impossible that your relations should be kept from knowing. You’ll have to give particulars about yourself, sooner or later. And charges of murder, like this, can’t be kept out of the newspapers.”

“Tell me, Hyde!” exclaimed Viner. “Look here, now, to begin with﻿—you didn’t kill this man?”

Hyde shook his head in a puzzled fashion﻿—something was evidently causing him surprise.

===Chinese===

“到外面等着。”他简短生硬地说。等屋里只剩他、维纳和海德三人，他又接着说：“那么——维纳先生，既然是您要求见他，他想对您说什么都可以。这事情样样不合规矩，可我也不想拦着，随他对您讲什么。不过记住，”他瞥了一眼囚犯，继续说道，“他是当着我的面说的——依我看，方才在法庭上，你还不如多少交代几句呢。”

“我当时不知道该说什么才好，”海德迟疑地答道，“诸位可以想见，我算是彻底破了产；可是——我还有亲戚！只要办得到，我不想让他们知道这件事。”

德里尔福德看了看维纳，摇了摇头，仿佛在表示他对海德这副态度的鄙夷。

“想想你眼下的处境，”他重新转向海德说道，“你该明白，想让你的亲戚一直蒙在鼓里是办不到的。迟早你得交代自己的详细来历。再说，这样的谋杀罪名，报上是瞒不住的。”

“跟我说，海德！”维纳急切地喊道，“听好，眼下先问一句——你没有杀这个人吧？”

海德困惑地摇了摇头——显然有什么事让他暗自诧异。

===Original===

“I didn’t know the man was killed, or dead, until they brought me here, from that pawnbroker’s this morning!” he said. Then he laughed almost contemptuously, and with some slight show of spirit. “Do you think I’d have been such a fool as to try to pawn or sell a ring that belonged to a man who’d just been murdered?” he demanded. “I’m not quite such an ass as that!”

Viner looked round at Drillford.

“There!” he said quietly. “What did I tell you? Isn’t that what I said? You’re on the wrong track, Inspector!”

But Drillford, sternly official in manner, shook his head.

“How did he come by the ring, then?” he asked, pointing at his prisoner. “Let him say!”

===Chinese===

“直到今天上午他们把我从那家当铺带到这里，我才知道那人遇了害，才知道他死了！”他说。接着他近乎轻蔑地笑了，多少显出几分精气神。“你们以为我会蠢到那步田地，去典当或变卖一个刚刚遭人谋害的戒指主人的东西吗？”他质问道，“我还不至于蠢成那副样子！”

维纳转头看了看德里尔福德。

“怎么样！”他不动声色地说，“我方才怎么说的？是不是正如我所言？探长，你们追错方向了！”

可德里尔福德一副冷峻的官家做派，摇了摇头。

“那么，这戒指他是怎么到手的？”他指着囚犯问道，“让他说！”

===Original===

“Hyde!” said Viner. “Tell! I’ve been certain for an hour that you didn’t kill this man, and I want to help you. But﻿—tell us the truth! What do you know about it? How did you get that ring?”

“I shall make use of anything he tells,” remarked Drillford warningly.

“He’s going to tell﻿—everything,” said Viner. “Come now, Hyde, the truth!”

Hyde suddenly dropped into a chair by which he was standing, and pressed his hand over his face with a gesture which seemed to indicate a certain amount of bewilderment.

“Let me sit down,” he said. “I’m weak, tired, too. Until this morning I hadn’t had a mouthful of food for a long time, and I’d﻿—well, I’d been walking about, night as well as day. I was walking about all yesterday, and a lot of last night. I’m pretty nearly done, if you want to know!”

“Take your time,” said Drillford. “Here, wait a bit,” he went on after a sudden glance at his prisoner. “Keep quiet a minute.” He turned to a cupboard in the corner of the room and presently came back with something in a glass. “Drink that,” he said not unkindly. “Drop of weak brandy and water,” he muttered to Viner. “Do him no harm﻿—I see how it is with him﻿—he’s been starving.”

===Chinese===

“海德！”维纳说，“说出来！一个钟头以来我一直确信你没有杀这个人，我是想帮你的。可是——你得对我们说实话！这件事你知道些什么？那枚戒指你是怎么弄到手的？”

“他说的每一句话，我都可能派上用场。”德里尔福德告诫道。

“他会说的——把一切都告诉我们。”维纳说，“来吧，海德，说实话！”

海德忽然一屁股坐进身旁的椅子里，抬手捂住脸，那姿态似乎透着几分茫然失措。

“让我坐一坐，”他说，“我浑身没力气，也乏透了。直到今天早上，我已经很久没进过一口吃食，而且我——唉，我一直在外头逛荡，白天逛，夜里也逛。昨天整整走了一天，昨夜又走了大半宿。不瞒你们说，我快撑不住了！”

“不用急，慢慢说。”德里尔福德说。忽然朝囚犯瞥了一眼，他又接着说：“慢着，先别作声。”他转身走到屋角的食橱前，不多时端着一杯东西回来。“把这个喝了。”他的语气并不算不客气。“兑了水的淡白兰地。”他低声向维纳咕哝道，“喝了对他没有坏处——我瞧得出他是怎么回事——他一直在挨饿。”

===Original===

Hyde caught the last word and laughed feebly as he handed the glass back.

“Starving!” he said. “Yes﻿—that’s it! I hope neither of you’ll know what it means! Three days without﻿—”

“Now, Hyde!” interrupted Viner. “Never mind that﻿—you won’t starve again. Come﻿—tell us all about this﻿—tell everything.”

Hyde bent forward in his chair, but after a look at the two men, his eyes sought the floor and moved from one plank to another as if he found it difficult to find a fixed point.

“I don’t know where to begin, Viner,” he said at last. “You see, you’ve never met me since we left school. I went in for medicine﻿—I was at Bart’s for a time, but﻿—well, I was no good, somehow. And then I went in for the stage﻿—I’ve had some fairly decent engagements, both here and in the States, now and then. But you know what a precarious business that is. And some time ago I struck a real bad patch, and I’ve been out of a job for months. And lately it’s gone from bad to worse﻿—you know, or rather I suppose you don’t know, because you’ve never been in that fix﻿—pawning everything, and so on, until﻿—well, I haven’t had a penny in my pockets for days now!”

“Your relations?” questioned Viner.

===Chinese===

海德听见了最后那个词，把杯子递还回去时，无力地笑了笑。

“挨饿！”他说，“对——就是这个！但愿你们二位永远不知道那是什么滋味！三天没吃——”

“好了，海德！”维纳打断了他，“这个不消说——你再也不会挨饿了。来——把这件事原原本本讲给我们听——什么都讲出来。”

海德在椅子里俯身向前，可朝那两人望了一眼之后，他的目光便落到了地板上，从一块地板挪到另一块地板，仿佛难以寻到一个可以停驻的地方。

“我不知道从哪儿说起，维纳。”他终于开口道，“你要知道，打从我们离开学校，你就再没见过我。我先是学医——在圣巴塞洛缪医院（Bart’s）待过一阵子，可是——唉，不知怎么的，我不成器。后来我又去登台演戏——在国内和美国，时不时也接过几份还算体面的戏约。可那营生朝不保夕，你是知道的。前些时候我倒了霉运，失业好几个月了。近来更是一天不如一天——你是知道的，不，恐怕你并不知道，因为你从没落到过那步田地——什么都拿去当了，如此这般，直到——唉，我口袋里已经好几天连一个便士都没有了！”

“你的亲戚呢？”维纳问。

===Original===

“Didn’t want them to know,” answered Hyde. “The fact is, I haven’t been on good terms with them for a long time, and I’ve got some pride left﻿—or I had, until yesterday. But here’s the truth: I had to clear out of my lodgings﻿—which was nothing but an attic, three days since, and I’ve been wandering about, literally hungry and homeless, since that. If it hadn’t been for that, I should never have been in this hole! And that’s due to circumstances that beat me, for I tell you again, I don’t know anything about this man’s murder﻿—at least, not about it actually.”

“What do you know?” asked Viner. “Tell us plainly.”

“I’m going to,” responded Hyde. “I was hanging about the Park and around Kensington Gardens most of yesterday. Then, at night, I got wandering about this part﻿—didn’t seem to matter much where I went. You don’t know, either of you, what it means to wander round, starving. You get into a sort of comatose state﻿—you just go on and on. Well, last night I was walking, in that way, in and out about these Bayswater squares. I got into Markendale Square. As I was going along the top side of it, I noticed a passage and turned into it﻿—as I’ve said, when a man’s in the state I was in, it doesn’t matter where he slouches﻿—anywhere! I turned into that passage, I tell you, just aimlessly, as a man came walking out. Viner, look for that man! Find him! He’s the fellow these police want! If there’s been murder﻿—”

“Keep calm, Hyde!” said Viner. “Go on, quietly.”

“This man passed me and went on into the square,” continued Hyde. “I went up the passage. It was very dark, except in the middle, where there’s an old-fashioned lamp. And then I saw another man, who was lying across the flags. I don’t know that I’d any impression about him﻿—I was too sick and weary. I believe I thought he was drunk, or ill or something. But you see, at the same instant that I saw him, I saw something else which drove him clean out of my mind. In fact, as soon as I’d seen it, I never thought about him any more, nor looked at him again.”

“What was it?” demanded Viner, certain of what the answer would be.

===Chinese===

“不想让他们知道。”海德答道，“实情是，我和他们早已不睦，而我身上还剩下几分傲气——或者说，直到昨天还有。可实情是这样：三天前，我不得不从住处搬出来——那不过是间阁楼——打那以后，我就一直在外头流浪，真正是饥肠辘辘、无家可归。若不是为了这个，我绝不至于陷进这个坑里！这都得怪那些我应付不来的境遇，因为我再对你们说一遍，这个人的谋杀案，我什么都不知道——至少，不知道实际的情形。”

“那你知道些什么？”维纳问，“明白地告诉我们。”

“我正要说。”海德应道，“昨天大半天，我都在公园和肯辛顿花园（Kensington Gardens）那一带打转。到了夜里，我又逛荡到这一带来——走去哪儿都无所谓了。你们两位都不知道，一个人饿着肚子四处游荡是什么滋味。人会陷入一种半昏迷的状态——只管一味往前走。唉，昨夜我就是那样恍恍惚惚地走着，在贝斯沃特（Bayswater）这些广场之间穿进穿出。我走进了马肯代尔广场。顺着广场靠外的一边走时，我瞥见一条过道，便拐了进去——我方才说过，人到了我那步田地，拖沓着走到哪儿都无所谓——哪儿都一样！我告诉你，我拐进那条过道，纯粹是无心无意，恰在那时，有个人从里头走了出来。维纳，去找那个人！找到他！警察要捉的正是那家伙！倘若真出了谋杀案——”

“镇定些，海德！”维纳说，“接着讲，慢慢讲。”

“那人从我身边走过，往广场里头去了。”海德继续说道，“我顺着过道往里走。过道里黑得很，只有正中一段亮着，那儿有一盏老式路灯。接着我看见了另一个人，横躺在石板地上。我说不清自己当时对他有什么印象——我实在太难受、太疲乏了。我想我八成把他当作了醉汉，或是犯了病什么的。可你们知道，就在我看见他的同一瞬间，我还看见了另一样东西，把这个人从我脑子里赶了个一干二净。实话说，我一见那样东西，就再没想过他，也没再看他一眼。”

“是什么？”维纳追问，其实他已料到了答案。

===Original===

“A diamond ring,” replied Hyde. “It was lying on the flags close by the man. The light from the lamp fell full on it. And I snatched it up, thrust it into my pocket and ran up the passage. I ran into somebody at the far end﻿—it turns out to have been you. Well, you saw me hurry off﻿—I got as far away as I could, lest you or somebody else should follow. I wandered round Westbourne Grove, and then up into the Harrow Road, and in a sort of back street there I sneaked into a shanty in a yard, and stopped in it the rest of the night. And this morning I tried to pawn the ring.”

“Having no idea of its value,” suggested Viner, with a glance at Drillford, who was listening to everything with an immovable countenance.

“I thought it might be worth thirty or forty pounds,” answered Hyde. “Of course, I’d no idea that it was worth what’s been said. You see, I’m fairly presentable, and I thought I could tell a satisfactory story if I was asked anything at the pawnshop. I didn’t anticipate any difficulty about pawning the ring﻿—I don’t think there’d have been any if it hadn’t been for its value. A thousand pounds! of course, I’d no idea of that!”

“And that’s the whole truth?” asked Viner.

“It’s the whole truth as far as I’m concerned,” answered Hyde. “I certainly picked up that ring in that passage, close by this man who was lying there. But I didn’t know he was dead; I didn’t know he’d been murdered. All I know is that I was absolutely famishing, desperate, in no condition to think clearly about anything. I guess I should do the same thing again, under the circumstances. I only wish﻿—”

===Chinese===

“一枚钻石戒指。”海德答道，“它就躺在那人近旁的石板地上，路灯的光正正照在上面。我一把抓起它，塞进口袋，顺着过道跑了出去。在过道外头那一端，我撞上了一个人——原来那就是你。唉，你也看见我匆匆跑开了——我拼命往远处跑，就怕你或是别人追上来。我在韦斯特本格罗夫一带转了转，又转上哈罗路（Harrow Road），在那一带一条背街陋巷里，我溜进一个院子里的破棚子，在里面躲了后半夜。今天早上，我就去当那枚戒指。”

“完全不知道它值多少钱吧？”维纳提示道，一面瞥了德里尔福德一眼——后者正绷着一动不动的脸听着这一切。

“我估摸它顶多值三四十英镑。”海德答道，“我当然想不到它竟值外头说的那个数。你们知道，我外表还算体面，心想就算当铺里的人问起来，我也能编出一套说得过去的说辞。我本以为当这戒指不会遇到任何麻烦——要不是它那么值钱，我看也确实不会有。一千英镑！我当然做梦也想不到！”

“这就是全部实情？”维纳问。

“就我而言，这就是全部实情。”海德答道，“那枚戒指确实是我在那条过道里捡的，就在躺着的那人近旁。可我不知道他已经死了；我不知道他遭了谋害。我只知道自己实在饿疯了，走投无路，根本没有心思把任何事想清楚。换作那样的情形，我看我还是会那么干的。我只希望——”

===Original===

He paused and began muttering to himself, and the two listeners glanced at each other. “You only wish what, Hyde?” asked Viner.

“I wish it had been a half-crown instead of that ring!” said Hyde with a queer flashing glance at his audience. “I could have got a bed for fourpence, and have lived for three days on the rest. And now﻿—”

Viner made no remark; and Drillford, who was leaning against his desk, watching his prisoner closely, tapped Hyde on the shoulder.

“Can you describe the man who came out of the passage as you entered it?” he asked. “Be accurate, now!”

Hyde’s face brightened a little, and his eyes became more intelligent.

“Yes!” he answered. “You know﻿—or you don’t know﻿—how your mental faculties get sharpened by hunger. I was dull enough, in one way, but alert enough in another. I can describe the man﻿—as much as I saw of him. A tall man﻿—neither broad nor slender﻿—half-and-half. Dressed in black from top to toe. A silk hat﻿—patent leather boots﻿—and muffled to the eyes in a white silk handkerchief.”

===Chinese===

他顿住话头，自顾自喃喃起来，两位听者对视了一眼。“你只希望什么，海德？”维纳问。

“我倒希望捡到的是一枚半克朗（half-crown），而不是那枚戒指！”海德说着，异样地朝听他的人瞥了一眼，目光一亮。“花四便士就能弄个铺位，剩下的钱还能对付三天。可如今——”

维纳没有作声；德里尔福德倚着办公桌，正仔细打量他的囚犯，这时伸手拍了拍海德的肩膀。

“你能描述一下你进过道时从里面走出来的那个人吗？”他问道，“要说得准些！”

海德的脸色稍稍一亮，眼神也机敏了些。

“能！”他答道，“你们知道——或者并不知道——饥饿会把人的心思磨得多么锋利。我一方面够迟钝的，另一方面却又够警醒。我能描述那个人——就我所见的那部分。高个子——不胖也不瘦——两者居中。从头到脚一身黑衣。头戴大礼帽——脚穿漆皮靴——脸上蒙着一条白绸手帕，一直蒙到眼睛。”

===Original===

“Could you see his face?” asked Drillford. “Was he clean-shaved, or bearded, or what?”

“I tell you he was muffled to the very eyes,” answered Hyde. “One of those big silk handkerchiefs, you know﻿—he had it drawn up over his chin and nose﻿—right up.”

“Then you’d have difficulty in knowing him again,” observed Drillford. “There are a few thousand men in the West End of London who’d answer the description you’ve given.”

“All right!” muttered Hyde doggedly. “But﻿—I know what I saw. And if you want to help me, Viner, find that man﻿—because he must have come straight away from the body!”

Drillford turned to Viner, glancing at the same time at the clock.

“Do you want to ask him any more questions?” he inquired. “No? Well, there’s just one I want to ask. What were you doing with that knife in your possession?” he went on, turning to Hyde. “Be careful, now; you heard what the doctor said about it, in court?”

===Chinese===

“你看清他的脸了吗？”德里尔福德问，“他是刮净了胡子，还是蓄着胡须，还是别的样子？”

“我告诉你们，他蒙得连眼睛都快遮住了。”海德答道，“就是那种大块绸手帕，你们知道——他把它一直拉到下巴和鼻子上面——拉得严严实实。”

“那么你再要认出他来，可就难了。”德里尔福德说道，“伦敦西区（the West End）里，符合你这番描述的男人，少说也有几千个。”

“随你们怎么说！”海德执拗地咕哝道，“可是——我看见的就是我看见的。维纳，你要是想帮我，就去找到那个人——因为他准是从尸体跟前直接走出来的！”

德里尔福德转向维纳，同时瞥了一眼时钟。

“你还想再问他什么吗？”他问道，“不问了？好，那我只有一个问题要问。”他转向海德，接着说，“你身上带着那把刀，是想做什么用的？仔细着点再答；你在法庭上也听到医生对它的说法了，不是吗？”

===Original===

“I’ve nothing to conceal,” replied Hyde. “You heard me say just now that I’d had engagements in the States. I bought that knife when I was out West﻿—more as a curiosity than anything﻿—and I’ve carried it in my pocket ever since.”

Drillford looked again at Viner.

“He’ll have to go, now,” he said. “If you’re going to employ legal help for him, the solicitor will know where and when he can see him.” He paused on his way to the door and looked a little doubtfully at his prisoner. “I’ll give you a bit of advice,” he said, “not as an official, but as an individual. If you want to clear yourself, you’d better give all the information you can.”

“I’ll send my own solicitor to you, Hyde, at once,” said Viner. “Be absolutely frank with him about everything.”

When Viner was once more alone with Drillford, the two men looked at each other.

===Chinese===

“我没有什么要隐瞒的。”海德答道，“你们方才听我说过，我在美国接过戏约。那把刀是我当年在西部时买的——多半是当个稀罕玩意儿——打那以后就一直揣在口袋里。”

德里尔福德又看了看维纳。

“现在得把他带走了。”他说，“如果你打算为他聘请法律上的帮手，事务律师自会知道在何时何地见他。”他往门口走的路上停了停，略带疑虑地看了看囚犯。“我给你一句忠告，”他说，“这不是以官家的身份，而是以私人身份说的。你想洗清自己，最好把所知道的一切都交代出来。”

“我马上把我自己的事务律师给你派来，海德。”维纳说，“对他什么事情都要开诚布公。”

等维纳再次和德里尔福德单独相对时，两人互相看了看。

===Original===

“My own impression,” said Viner, after a significant silence, “is that we have just heard the plain truth! I’m going to work on it, anyway.”

“In that case, Mr. Viner, there’s no need for me to say anything,” remarked Drillford. “It may be the plain truth. But as I am what I am, all I know is the firsthand evidence against this young fellow. So he really was a schoolmate of yours?”

“Certainly!” said Viner. “His people live, or did live, in the north. I shall have to get into communication with them. But now﻿—what about the information he gave you? This man he saw?”

Drillford shook his head.

“Mr. Viner,” he answered, “you don’t understand police methods. We’ve got very strong evidence against Hyde. We know nothing about a tall man in a white muffler. If you want to clear Hyde, you’d better do what he suggested﻿—find that man! I wish you may﻿—if he ever existed!”

===Chinese===

在一阵意味深长的沉默之后，维纳开口道：“我个人的感觉是，我们方才听到的正是明明白白的真话！不管怎样，我要循着它去查。”

“那样的话，维纳先生，我用不着多说什么了。”德里尔福德说道，“那也许正是明明白白的真话。可我这身份摆在这儿，我所知道的，只有对这个年轻人不利的第一手证据。这么说，他真是你的同学？”

“当然！”维纳说，“他家里人住在——或者说从前住在——北方。我得同他们取得联系。可是现在——他提供的那条线索，你们打算怎么办？他看见的那个男人呢？”

德里尔福德摇了摇头。

“维纳先生，”他答道，“您不了解警方的路数。我们手里握着对海德非常不利的证据。至于一个围白围巾的高个子男人，我们一无所知。您若想洗清海德，最好照他说的办——去找那个人！但愿您找得到——倘若他真存在的话！”

===Original===

“You don’t believe Hyde?” asked Viner.

“I’m not required to believe anything, sir, unless I’ve good proof of it,” said Drillford with a significant smile. “If there is any mystery in this murder, well﻿—let’s hope something will clear it up.”

Viner went away troubled and thoughtful. He remembered Hyde well enough now, though so many years had elapsed since their last meeting. And he was genuinely convinced of his innocence: there had been a ring of truth in all that he had said. Who, then, was the guilty man? And had robbery been the real motive of the murder? Might it not have been that Ashton had been murdered for some quite different motive, and that the murderer had hastily removed the watch, chain, purse, and rings from the body with the idea of diverting suspicion, and in his haste had dropped one of the rings?

“If only one knew more about Ashton and his affairs!” mused Viner. “Even his own people don’t seem to know much.”

This reminded him of his promise to call on Miss Wickham. He glanced at his watch: it was not yet one o’clock: the proceedings before the magistrate and the subsequent talk with Hyde had occupied comparatively little time. So Viner walked rapidly to number seven in the square, intent on doing something toward clearing Hyde of the charge brought against him. The parlourmaid whom he had seen the night before admitted him at once; it seemed to Viner that he was expected. She led him straight to a room in which Mrs. Killenhall and Miss Wickham were in conversation with an elderly man, who looked at Viner with considerable curiosity when his name was mentioned, and who was presently introduced to him as Mr. Ashton’s solicitor, Mr. Pawle, of Crawle, Pawle and Rattenbury.

===Chinese===

“你不相信海德？”维纳问。

“我的职责并不要求我相信任何事，先生，除非我有确凿的证据。”德里尔福德带着意味深长的微笑说，“这桩谋杀案里若真有什么隐情，那么——但愿总会有什么东西让它水落石出。”

维纳心事重重、思前想后地走了出去。虽然自上次见面已隔了这么多年，他此刻把海德清清楚楚想起来了。他也真心相信海德是无辜的：他说的每一句话，都带着实话的口吻。那么，有罪的是谁呢？抢劫当真是这件谋杀案的动机吗？会不会是阿什顿因某种全然不同的动机被杀，凶手为了转移视线，才慌忙从尸体上取下怀表、表链、钱袋和戒指，而仓促之间掉落了其中一枚？

“要是能多知道些阿什顿的来历和他手头的事就好了！”维纳暗自思忖，“连他自家人似乎都所知无几。”

这让他想起了要去拜访威克姆小姐的许诺。他看了看表：还不到一点钟：治安法庭上的程序和随后与海德的谈话，占去的时间并不算多。于是维纳快步走向广场上的七号宅，一心想着为洗清加在海德身上的指控出点力。昨夜见过的那位客厅侍女立刻放他进门；维纳觉得自己是被盼着来的。她径直把他领进一个房间，里面基伦霍尔夫人正和威克姆小姐同一位上了年纪的男子交谈；通报维纳姓名时，那男子颇为好奇地打量着他，随即经人介绍，原来他是阿什顿先生的事务律师——克劳尔、波尔与拉滕伯里事务所的波尔先生（Mr. Pawle）。

## VI / 六

===Original===

Speculations

Mr. Pawle, an alert-looking, sharp-eyed little man, whom Viner at once recognized as having been present in the magistrate’s court when Hyde was brought up, smiled as he shook hands with the new visitor.

“You don’t know me, Mr. Viner,” he said. “But I knew your father very well﻿—he and I did a lot of business together in our time. You haven’t followed his profession, I gather?”

“I’m afraid I haven’t any profession, Mr. Pawle,” answered Viner. “I’m a student﻿—and a bit, a very little bit, of a writer.”

“Aye, well, your father was a bit in that way too,” remarked Mr. Pawle. “I remember that he was a great collector of books﻿—you have his library, no doubt?”

===Chinese===

种种推测

波尔先生是个模样机灵、目光锐利的小个子，海德（Hyde）被押上治安法庭那天他也在场，维纳一见便认了出来；此刻他同新来的客人握着手，脸上堆起笑意。

“您不认得我，维纳先生，”他说，“可我跟令尊相熟得很——他和我当年一块儿做过不少生意。据我猜想，您没有承继他的行当？”

“恐怕我压根儿没有什么行当，波尔先生，”维纳答道，“我是个读书人——多少也算写点东西，不过是小小不言的一点儿。”

“哦，是嘛，令尊当年也沾过这个边儿，”波尔先生说，“我记得他还是位了不得的藏书家——他的藏书，如今自然都在您手里？”

===Original===

“Yes, and I’m always adding to it,” said Viner. “I shall be glad to show you my additions, any time.”

Mr. Pawle turned to the two ladies, waving his hand at Viner.

“Knew his father most intimately,” he said, as if he were guaranteeing the younger man’s status. “Fine fellow, was Stephen Viner. Well,” he continued, dropping into a chair, and pointing Viner to another, “this is a sad business that we’ve got concerned in, young man! Now, what do you think of the proceedings we’ve just heard? Your opinion, Mr. Viner, is probably better worth having than anybody’s, for you saw this fellow running away from the scene, and you found my unfortunate client lying dead. What, frankly, *is* your opinion?”

“I had better tell you something that’s just happened,” replied Viner. He went on to repeat the statements which Hyde had just made to Drillford and himself. “My opinion,” he concluded, “is that Hyde is speaking the plain truth﻿—that all he really did was, as he affirms, to pick up that ring and run away. I don’t believe he murdered Mr. Ashton, and I’m going to do my best to clear him.”

He looked round from one listener to another, seeking opinion from each. Mr. Pawle maintained a professional imperturbability; Mrs. Killenhall looked mildly excited on hearing this new theory. But from Miss Wickham, Viner got a flash of intelligent comprehension.

===Chinese===

“都在，而且我还不断添置，”维纳说，“我新添的书，什么时候都乐意请您过目。”

波尔先生转向两位女士，朝维纳那边摆了摆手。

“跟他父亲熟得不能再熟，”他说，那口气像是在给这个年轻人作保。“斯蒂芬·维纳（Stephen Viner），好样儿的一个人。好吧，”他接着往下说，一面坐进一张椅子，一面指了指另一张让维纳坐，“咱们搅和进去的这档子事，可是件惨事啊，年轻人！那么，方才那堂官司，您怎么看？您的见解，维纳先生，多半比谁的都值钱，因为您亲眼瞧见那家伙从现场跑掉，又是您发现我不幸的委托人躺在那里断了气。坦白说一句，您*究竟*怎么看？”

“我最好把方才发生的一件事告诉您，”维纳答道。接着他把海德刚刚对德里尔福德和他本人供述的那番话，原原本本学说了一遍。“依我之见，”他末了说道，“海德讲的是不折不扣的实话——正如他自己声明的，他真正干的事，不过是捡起那枚戒指，撒腿跑了而已。我不信是他谋害了阿什顿先生，我要尽力给他洗刷冤屈。”

他环顾左右，一个个探询每位听众的意见。波尔先生端着职业性的不动声色；基伦霍尔夫人听了这个新说法，脸上泛起些微的兴奋。倒是威克姆小姐那里，维纳得着了一瞥心领神会的眼色。

===Original===

“The real thing is this,” she said, “none of us know anything about Mr. Ashton, really. He may have had enemies.”

Pawle rubbed his chin; the action suggested perplexity.

“Miss Wickham is quite right,” he said. “Mr. Ashton is more or less a man of mystery. He had been here in England two months. His ward knows next to nothing about him, except that she was left in his guardianship many a year ago, that he sent her to England, to school, and that he recently joined her here. Mrs. Killenhall knows no more than that he engaged her as chaperon to his ward, and that they exchanged references. His references were to his bankers and to me. But neither his bankers nor I know anything of him, except that he was a very well-to-do man. I can tell precisely what his bankers know. It is merely this: he transferred his banking-account from an Australian bank to them on coming to London. I saw them this morning on first getting the news. They have about two hundred thousand pounds lying to his credit. That’s absolutely all they know about him﻿—all!”

“The Australian bankers would know more,” suggested Viner.

“Precisely!” agreed Mr. Pawle. “We can get news from them, in time. But now, what do I know? No more than this﻿—Mr. Ashton called on me about six or seven weeks ago, told me that he was an Australian who had come to settle in London, that he was pretty well off, and that he wanted to make a will. We drafted a will on his instructions, and he duly executed it. Here it is! Miss Wickham has just seen it. Mr. Ashton has left every penny he had to Miss Wickham. He told me she was the only child of an old friend of his, who had given her into his care on his death out in Australia, some years ago, and that as he, Ashton, had no near relations, he had always intended to leave her all he had. And so he has, without condition, or reservation, or anything﻿—all is yours, Miss Wickham, and I’m your executor. But now,” continued Mr. Pawle, “how far does this take us toward solving the mystery of my client’s death? So far as I can see, next to nowhere! And I am certain of this, Mr. Viner: if we are going to solve it, and if this old school friend of yours is being unjustly accused, and is to be cleared, we must find out more about Ashton’s doings since he came to London. The secret lies﻿—there!”

===Chinese===

“要紧的是这一层，”她说，“说实在的，我们谁也不真正了解阿什顿先生。他兴许结过仇家。”

波尔摸着下巴；这动作透着一脸的踌躇。

“威克姆小姐说得一点不错，”他说，“阿什顿先生多少算得上一个来历不明的人。他来英国才两个月光景。他的被监护人对他几乎一无所知，只知道多年前她便被托付给他监护，只知道他送她来英国上学，前些日子才来同她团聚。基伦霍尔夫人所知道的，也不外乎他聘她做被监护人的年长女伴，两人彼此查验过荐保。他出具的保证人，是他的往来银行和在下。可是不论银行还是在下，对他的底细都无从知晓，只晓得他是个家道极为殷实的人。他的银行方面知道些什么，我能一五一十地说出来。无非如此：他来伦敦时，把往来账户从一家澳大利亚银行转到了他们那里。今天一早得到凶讯，我头一件事便是去见他们。他名下存着大约二十万英镑。他们对他的了解，到此为止——仅此而已！”

“澳大利亚那边的银行想必知道得多些，”维纳提了一句。

“正是！”波尔先生附和道，“早晚能从他们那里问出消息来。可是话说回来，我又知道些什么呢？不过如此——大约六七个星期之前，阿什顿先生到事务所来看我，说他是个来伦敦定居的澳大利亚人，说自家境相当丰裕，说想立一份遗嘱。我们依他的意思拟好遗嘱，他也正式签立了。喏，就是这个！威克姆小姐方才已经看过。阿什顿先生把名下的每一文钱都留给了威克姆小姐。他告诉我，她是他一位老友的独生女；那位老友几年前在澳大利亚撒手人寰时，把她托付给了他；又说阿什顿自己没有至亲，一向存心把全部家产都留给她。如今他果然这么办了，不带条件，没有保留，什么都没有——一切都是您的了，威克姆小姐，而我，就是您的遗嘱执行人。可是，”波尔先生接着说，“这些对于我们解开我委托人遇害之谜，又有多大进益呢？依我看，几乎是寸步未进！而这一层我敢断定，维纳先生：我们若要解开此案，您这位老同学若果真蒙着不白之冤、要得昭雪，就必须查清阿什顿来伦敦以后的种种行迹。秘密就藏在那里！”

===Original===

“I quite agree,” answered Viner. “But﻿—who knows anything?”

Mr. Pawle looked at the two ladies.

“That’s a stiff question!” he said. “The bankers tell me that Ashton only called on them two or three times; he called on me not oftener; neither they nor I ever had much conversation with him. These two ladies should know more about him than anybody﻿—but they seem to know little.”

Viner, who was sitting opposite to her, looked at Miss Wickham.

“You must know something about his daily life?” he said. “What did he do with himself?”

===Chinese===

“我完全同意，”维纳答道，“可是——谁又知道些什么内情呢？”

波尔先生望了望两位女士。

“这可是个难人的问题！”他说，“银行告诉我，阿什顿统共只到他们那里去过两三回；来找我也不比这更勤；他们也好，我也好，都难得同他深谈。按理说，这两位女士该比谁都了解他——可她们似乎也知道得有限。”

维纳正坐在她对面，便望着威克姆小姐。

“他平日的起居行止，您总该知道一些吧？”他说，“他是怎么打发自个儿的？”

===Original===

“We told you and the police-inspector pretty nearly all we know, last night,” replied Miss Wickham. “As a rule, he used to go out of a morning﻿—I think, from his conversation, he used to go down to the City. I don’t think it was on business: I think, he liked to look about him. Sometimes he came home to lunch; sometimes he didn’t. Very often in the afternoon he took us for motor-rides into the country﻿—sometimes he took us to the theatres. He used to go out a good deal, alone at night﻿—we don’t know where.”

“Did he ever mention any club?” asked Mr. Pawle.

“No, never!” replied Miss Wickham. “He was reticent about himself﻿—always very kind and thoughtful and considerate for Mrs. Killenhall and myself, but he was a reserved man.”

“Did he ever have anyone to see him?” inquired the solicitor. “Any men to dine, or anything of that sort?”

“No﻿—not once. No one has ever even called on him,” said Miss Wickham. “We have had two or three dinner-parties, but the people who came were friends of mine﻿—two or three girls whom I knew at school, who are now married and live in London.”

===Chinese===

“昨天晚上，我们已把所知道的对您和那位督察说得八九不离十了，”威克姆小姐答道，“他照例上午出门——听他言谈之间，我料想他是往伦敦城去。我看不是为了正事；我想，他不过是喜欢四处走走看看。有时他回家吃午饭，有时不回。下午他常带我们坐汽车到乡下去兜风——有时也带我们去看戏。夜里他常常独自外出——去了哪儿，我们就不得而知了。”

“他提没提起过什么俱乐部？”波尔先生问。

“没有，从来没有！”威克姆小姐答道，“他对自己的事守口如瓶——对基伦霍尔夫人同我，向来和气、周到、体贴，可他是个深沉寡言的人。”

“可曾有人来会过他？”律师问道，“有什么男客来吃顿饭，或是诸如此类的应酬？”

“没有——一回也没有。连登门访他的人都不曾有过，”威克姆小姐说，“我们家倒办过两三回晚宴，可来的都是我的朋友——两三个我在学校时的旧识，如今都嫁了人，住在伦敦。”

===Original===

“A lonely sort of man!” commented Mr. Pawle. “Yet﻿—he must have known people. Where did he go when he went into the City? Where did he go at night? There must be somebody somewhere who can tell more about him. I think it will be well if I ask for information through the newspapers.”

“There is one matter we haven’t mentioned,” said Mrs. Killenhall. “Just after we got settled down here, Mr. Ashton went away for some days﻿—three or four days. That, of course, may be quite insignificant.”

“Do you know where he went?” asked Mr. Pawle.

“No, we don’t know,” answered Mrs. Killenhall. “He went away one Monday morning, saying that now everything was in order we could spare him for a few days. He returned on the following Thursday or Friday﻿—I forget which﻿—but he didn’t tell us where he had been.”

“You don’t think any of the servants would know?” asked Mr. Pawle.

===Chinese===

“真是个落落寡合的人！”波尔先生议论道，“然而——他总不至于一个相识也没有。他进伦敦城时往哪里去？夜里又往哪里去？总有什么人、在什么地方，能多讲出他的一些情形来。我看，不妨由我出面在报上登文，广求知情者的音信。”

“有一件事我们还没有提起，”基伦霍尔夫人说，“我们刚在这儿安顿下来不久，阿什顿先生便出门去了几天——三四天的样子。这件事，当然，也许全然无关紧要。”

“可知道他去了哪里？”波尔先生问。

“不，我们不知道，”基伦霍尔夫人答道，“一个星期一的早上他出了门，说如今诸事都已安顿停当，我们尽可以让他离开几天。到下一个星期四还是星期五他才回来——我记不清是哪一天了——可他没告诉我们他到过什么地方。”

“您想，仆人里头就没有一个知道的么？”波尔先生问。

===Original===

“Oh, dear me, no!” replied Mrs. Killenhall. “He was the sort of man who rarely speaks to his servants﻿—except when he wanted something.”

Mr. Pawle looked at his watch and rose.

“Well!” he said. “We shall have to find out more about my late client’s habits and whom he knew in London. There may have been a motive for this murder of which we know nothing. Are you coming, Mr. Viner? I should like a word with you!”

Viner, too, had risen; he looked at Miss Wickham.

“I hope my aunt called on you this morning?” he asked. “I was coming with her, but I had to go round to the police-station.”

===Chinese===

“哎呀，天哪，没有！”基伦霍尔夫人答道，“他是那种人，轻易不跟仆人搭话——除非要支使他们拿什么东西。”

波尔先生看了看表，站起身来。

“好吧！”他说，“关于我先委托人的起居习惯，关于他在伦敦结识过些什么人，我们非得再打探出些眉目不可。这桩凶案，说不定另有一个我们全然不知的动机。您同我一道走么，维纳先生？我有句话想跟您说说！”

维纳也站了起来；他望着威克姆小姐。

“我姨妈今天上午来探望过您吧？”他问，“本要陪她同来的，只是我不得不先绕到警察分局去了一趟。”

===Original===

“She did call, and she was very kind indeed, thank you,” said Miss Wickham. “I hope she’ll come again.”

“We shall both be glad to do anything,” said Viner. “Please don’t hesitate about sending round for me if there’s anything at all I can do.” He followed Mr. Pawle into the square, and turned him towards his own house. “Come and lunch with me,” he said. “We can talk over this at our leisure.”

“Thank you﻿—I will,” answered Mr. Pawle. “Very pleased. Between you and me, Mr. Viner, this is a very queer business. I’m quite prepared to believe the story that young fellow Hyde tells. I wish he’d told it straight out in court. But you must see that he’s in a very dangerous position﻿—very dangerous indeed! The police, of course, won’t credit a word of his tale﻿—not they! They’ve got a strong prima facie case against him, and they’ll follow it up for all they’re worth. The real thing to do, if you’re to save him, is to find the real murderer. And to do that, you’ll need all your wits! If one only had some theory!”

Viner introduced Mr. Pawle to Miss Penkridge with the remark that she was something of an authority in mysteries, and as soon as they had sat down to lunch, told her of Langton Hyde and his statement.

“Just so!” said Miss Penkridge dryly. “That’s much more likely to be the real truth than that this lad killed Ashton. There’s a great deal more in this murder than is on the surface, and I dare say Mr. Pawle agrees with me.”

===Chinese===

“她来过，而且实在和气得很，谢谢您，”威克姆小姐说，“我盼着她再来。”

“我们两个都乐意效力，”维纳说，“但凡有我帮得上一分力的事，请千万别客气，尽管打发人来叫我。”他随着波尔先生走到广场上，引着他朝自家宅子那头去。“来同我一起吃顿午饭吧，”他说，“咱们可以从从容容地细谈这件事。”

“多谢——我就叨扰了，”波尔先生答道，“甚为荣幸。咱们私下里说句体己话，维纳先生，这是桩顶顶蹊跷的案子。海德那年轻人讲的那番话，我完全愿意相信。只可惜他没在堂上和盘托出。可您也得看清，他眼下的处境凶险得很——凶险得很呐！警方么，对他的说法自然一个字也不会信——才不会呢！他们手里攥着一桩表面证据相当扎实的案子，是要使出浑身解数穷追到底的。要救他，正经的路只有一条：找出真凶。而要做到这一步，您得拿出全副的心机！唉，但凡能有个什么推想也好！”

维纳把波尔先生引见给彭克里奇小姐，说她于疑难案情一道，颇算得一位行家；三人刚刚坐上午餐桌，他便把兰顿·海德（Langton Hyde）和他那番供述讲给她听。

“可不是嘛！”彭克里奇小姐干巴巴地说，“比起说这小子杀了阿什顿，那番话倒更像是实情。这桩凶案，水面底下藏着的，比面上露出的多得多，我料想波尔先生是赞同我的。”

===Original===

“I dare say I do,” assented Mr. Pawle. “The difficulty is﻿—how to penetrate into the thick cloak of mystery.”

“When I was round there, at Number Seven, this morning,” observed Miss Penkridge, “those two talked very freely to me about Mr. Ashton. Now, there’s one thing struck me at once﻿—there must be men in London who knew him. He couldn’t go out and about, as he evidently did, without meeting men. Even if it wasn’t in business, he’d meet men somewhere. And if I were you, I should invite men who knew him to come forward and tell what they know.”

“It shall be done﻿—very good advice, ma’am,” said Mr. Pawle.

“And there’s another thing,” said Miss Penkridge. “I should find out what can be told about Mr. Ashton where he came from. I believe you can get telegraphic information from Australia within a few hours. Why not go to the expense﻿—when there’s so much at stake? Depend upon it, the real secret of this murder lies back in the past﻿—perhaps the far past.”

“That too shall be done,” agreed Mr. Pawle. “I shouldn’t be surprised if you’re right.”

===Chinese===

“我料想我是赞同的，”波尔先生应道，“难就难在——怎样戳破这层又厚又密的疑幕。”

“今天上午我在那边七号的时候，”彭克里奇小姐说道，“那两位同我谈起阿什顿先生，倒是知无不言。喏，有一件事我当场便觉察了——伦敦城里必定有认识他的男人。他既然分明时常出门走动，就不可能碰不上几个男人。就算不是为了生意，他总会在什么地方结识些什么人。我要是您，就去敦请那些认识他的人站出来，把知道的一五一十说出来。”

“这就去办——高见，夫人，”波尔先生说。

“还有一桩事，”彭克里奇小姐说，“他打哪儿来，就该往哪儿查访，看看那边能讲出他些什么来。我相信，往澳大利亚拍个电报，不出几个钟头便有回音。既然干系如此重大，这笔电报费怎么倒省不得？你只管信我的话，这桩凶案真正的根由，就埋在往昔——没准儿埋在久远的往昔。”

“这也照办，”波尔先生赞同道，“夫人若说得不错，我一点也不会诧异。”

===Original===

“In my opinion,” remarked Miss Penkridge, dryly, “the robbing of this dead man was all a blind. Robbery wasn’t the motive. Murder was the thing in view! And why? It may have been revenge. It may be that Ashton had to be got out of the way. And I shouldn’t wonder a bit if that isn’t at the bottom of it, which is at the top and bottom of pretty nearly everything!”

“And that, ma’am?” asked Mr. Pawle, who evidently admired Miss Penkridge’s shrewd observations, “that is what, now?”

“Money!” said Miss Penkridge. “Money!”

The old solicitor went away, promising to get to work on the lines suggested by Miss Penkridge, and next day he telephoned to Viner asking him to go down to his offices in Bedford Row. Viner hurried off, and on arriving found Mr. Pawle with a cablegram before him.

“I sent a pretty long message to Melbourne, to Ashton’s old bankers, as soon as I left you yesterday,” he said. “I gave them the news of his murder, and asked for certain information. Here’s their answer. I rang you up as soon as I got it.”

===Chinese===

“依我看，”彭克里奇小姐干巴巴地议论道，“劫掠这个死人，全是障眼的幌子。图财并非动机。谋命才是正题！为什么呢？也许是报仇雪恨。也许是阿什顿挡了谁的路，非除掉不可。要说根子不在它上头，我才不信呢——天底下的事，从头到尾，差不多件件都少不了它！”

“那么那个，夫人？”波尔先生问道，他显然十分钦佩彭克里奇小姐的敏锐见地，“那究竟是什么呢？”

“钱！”彭克里奇小姐说，“钱！”

老律师告辞而去，允诺照彭克里奇小姐指点的那几条路数去办。第二天，他打电话请维纳到贝德福德罗他的事务所去一趟。维纳匆匆赶去，一到那里，只见波尔先生面前摊着一份越洋电报。

“昨天一离开你们，我便给墨尔本（Melbourne）阿什顿的旧日银行拍了一份挺长的电报，”他说，“我把凶讯告诉了他们，又托他们查询几件事。这便是他们的回电。一到手，我便打电话请您来了。”

===Original===

Viner read the cablegram carefully:

> Deeply regret news. Ashton well known here thirty years dealer in real estate. Respected, wealthy. Quiet man, bachelor. Have made inquiries in quarters likely to know. Cannot trace anything about friend named Wickham. Ashton was away from Melbourne, up country, four years, some years ago. May have known Wickham then. Ashton left here end July, by *Maraquibo*, for London. Was accompanied by two friends Fosdick and Stephens. Please inform if can do more.

“What do you think of that?” asked Mr. Pawle. “Not much in it, is there?”

“There’s the mention of two men who might know something of Ashton’s habits,” said Viner. “If Fosdick and Stephens are still in England and were Ashton’s friends, one would naturally conclude that he’d seen them sometimes. Yet we haven’t heard of their ever going to his house.”

“We can be quite certain that they never did﻿—from what the two ladies say,” remarked Mr. Pawle. “Perhaps they don’t live in London. I’ll advertise for both. But now, here’s another matter. I asked these people if they could tell me anything about Wickham, the father of this girl to whom Ashton’s left his very considerable fortune. Well, you see, they can’t. Now, it’s a very curious thing, but Miss Wickham has no papers, has, in fact, nothing whatever to prove her identity. Nor have I. Ashton left nothing of that sort. I know no more, and she knows no more, than what he told both of us﻿—that her father died when she was a mere child, her mother already being dead, that the father left her in Ashton’s guardianship, and that Ashton, after sending her here to school, eventually came and took her to live with him. There isn’t a single document really to show who she is, who her father was, or anything about her family.”

===Chinese===

维纳细细读着这份电报：

> 惊闻凶讯，至为哀痛。阿什顿在此间三十年，营地产之业，素孚众望，家道殷实。其人恬静，终身未娶。已向可能知情的各方面查询。遍查名为威克姆之友人，未见端倪。数年前，阿什顿曾离墨尔本赴内陆，历时四年。或于彼时结识威克姆。阿什顿于七月底乘“马拉开波”号（Maraquibo）离此赴伦敦。同船有友人二，福斯迪克（Fosdick）与斯蒂芬斯（Stephens）。如可再为效力，乞示知。

“您看这份电报如何？”波尔先生问，“里头的名堂不多，是吧？”

“总归提到了两位男士，或许知道些阿什顿的习性，”维纳说，“福斯迪克同斯蒂芬斯倘若还在英国，倘若真是阿什顿的朋友，照理自然会推断他时常见得着他们。可我们从没听说过这两人登过他的门。”

“照两位女士的说法，他们压根儿没去过，这一点尽可放心，”波尔先生说，“兴许他们不住在伦敦。我两人都登报寻访。不过，这里还有另一桩事。我问过那边，关于威克姆——阿什顿把偌大一份家私遗赠给了他这个女儿——他们可说得出些什么。喏，您瞧，他们说不出。再有，说来也怪得很，威克姆小姐手里没有任何文书，实际上，拿不出一样东西来证明自己的身份。我这里也没有。阿什顿没有留下任何这类东西。他所告诉咱们两人的，便是全部所知了——说是她父亲去世时她还年幼，她母亲那时已经亡故，父亲把她留在阿什顿的监护之下；说是阿什顿先送她来此地求学，末了才来接她同住。真正能表明她是谁、她父亲是谁、她家世如何的文书，竟一份也没有。”

===Original===

“Is that very important?” asked Viner.

“It’s decidedly odd!” said Mr. Pawle. “This affair seems to be getting more mysterious than ever.”

“What’s to be done next?” inquired Viner.

“Well, the newspapers are always very good about that,” answered the solicitor. “I’m getting them to insert paragraphs asking the two men, Fosdick and Stephens, to come forward and tell us if they’ve seen anything of Ashton since he came to England; I’m also asking if anybody can tell us where Ashton was when he went away from home on that visit that Mrs. Killenhall spoke of. If﻿—”

Just then a clerk came into Mr. Pawle’s room, and bending down to him, whispered a few words which evidently occasioned him great surprise.

“At once!” he said. “Bring them straight in, Parkinson. God bless me!” he exclaimed, turning to Viner. “Here are the two men in question﻿—Fosdick and Stephens! Saw our name in the paper as Ashton’s solicitors and want to see me urgently.”

===Chinese===

“这个很要紧么？”维纳问。

“这分明是桩怪事！”波尔先生说，“这件事，看样子越发神秘莫测了。”

“下一步该怎么办？”维纳问。

“这个嘛，报馆在这类事上向来是肯帮忙的，”律师答道，“我正请他们刊出启事，一是寻访福斯迪克同斯蒂芬斯两位，请他们站出来，告知阿什顿来英国之后可曾同他见过面；二是问问众人，阿什顿那回离家外出——就是基伦霍尔夫人提起的那趟——身在何处，可有人说得上来。倘若——”

正在这时，一个书记员走进波尔先生的房间，俯身对他低声说了几句，显然使他大为诧异。

“马上！”他说，“直接把他们领进来，帕金森（Parkinson）。我的天哪！”他转过身来，朝维纳失声道，“来的正是咱们说的那两位——福斯迪克同斯蒂芬斯！他们在报上看见我们是阿什顿的律师，急着要见我。”

## VII / 七

===Original===

What Was the Secret?

The two men who were presently ushered in were typical Colonials﻿—big, hefty fellows as yet in early middle age, alert, evidently prosperous, if their attire and appointments were anything to go by, and each was obviously deeply interested in the occasion of his visit to Mr. Pawle. Two pairs of quick eyes took in the old solicitor and his companion, and the elder of the men came forward in a businesslike manner.

“Mr. Pawle, I understand?” he said. “I’m Mr. Fosdick, of Melbourne, Victoria; this is my friend Mr. Stephens, same place.”

“Take a seat, Mr. Fosdick﻿—have this chair, Mr. Stephens,” responded Mr. Pawle. “You wish to see me﻿—on business?”

“That’s so,” answered Fosdick as the two men seated themselves by the solicitor’s desk. “We saw your name in the newspapers this morning in connection with the murder of John Ashton. Now, we knew John Ashton﻿—he was a Melbourne man, too﻿—and we can tell something about him. So we came to you instead of the police. Because, Mr. Pawle, what we can tell is maybe more a matter for a lawyer than for a policeman. It’s mysterious.”

“Gentlemen,” said Mr. Pawle, “I’ll be frank with you. I recognized your names as soon as my clerk announced them. Here’s a cablegram which I have just received from Melbourne﻿—you’ll see your names mentioned in it.”

===Chinese===

秘密究竟是什么？

随即被引进屋来的两个人，是典型的殖民地来客——年纪刚届中年，高大健壮，精干利落；单看衣着做派，便知家道殷实，而且两人显然都为自己此番造访波尔先生的缘由深感关切。两双敏捷的眼睛把老律师和他的同伴打量了一遍，年长的那位便一副办正事的气派，径直走上前来。

“想必您就是波尔先生？”他说，“我叫福斯迪克，维多利亚州（Victoria）墨尔本人；这位是我的朋友斯蒂芬斯先生，同乡。”

“请坐，福斯迪克先生——斯蒂芬斯先生，坐这把椅子，”波尔先生应道，“二位要见我——是为正事吧？”

“正是，”福斯迪克一面在律师办公桌旁落座，一面答道，“今天早晨，我们在报上看见您的大名，同约翰·阿什顿被害一案牵连在一起。这个嘛，我们认识约翰·阿什顿——他也是墨尔本人——关于他，我们能说出些事情来。所以我们没去找警察，先来找您。因为，波尔先生，我们知道的这些事，说不定是律师的事务，而非警察的事务。这里头透着蹊跷。”

“两位先生，”波尔先生说，“我同你们坦诚相见。我的书记员一通报两位的大名，我就知道是谁了。喏，这是我刚从墨尔本收到的一份电报——二位的大名都在上头。”

===Original===

The two callers bent over the cablegram, and Fosdick looked up and nodded.

“Yes, that’s right,” he said. “We came over with John Ashton in the *Maraquibo*. We knew him pretty well before that﻿—most folk in Melbourne did. But of course, we were thrown into his company on board ship rather more than we’d ever been before. And we very much regret to hear of what’s happened to him.”

“You say there is something you can tell?” observed Mr. Pawle. “If it’s anything that will help to solve the mystery of this murder﻿—for there is a mystery﻿—I shall be very glad to hear it.”

Fosdick and Stephens glanced at each other and then at Viner, who sat a little in Mr. Pawle’s rear.

“Partner of yours?” asked Fosdick.

“Not at all! This gentleman,” replied Mr. Pawle, “is Mr. Viner. It was he who found Ashton’s dead body. They were neighbours.”

===Chinese===

两位来客俯身看了电报，福斯迪克抬起头来点了点头。

“不错，正是这么回事，”他说，“我们是同约翰·阿什顿一道乘‘马拉开波’号过来的。在那之前我们就同他很熟——墨尔本多数人都同他熟。不过上了船，自然朝夕相处，来往比从前任何时候都多。听到他的遭遇，我们心里非常难过。”

“二位说有事情可以相告？”波尔先生说，“若是能有助于解开这桩谋杀的谜团——这案子确有蹊跷——我乐意之至。”

福斯迪克和斯蒂芬斯彼此对视一眼，又望了望坐在波尔先生侧后方的维纳。

“这位是您的合伙人？”福斯迪克问。

“不是不是！这位先生，”波尔先生答道，“是维纳先生。发现阿什顿尸身的正是他。两位是邻居。”

===Original===

“Well, you found the body of a very worthy man, sir,” remarked Fosdick gravely. “And we’d like to do something toward finding the man who killed him. For we don’t think it was this young fellow who’s charged with it, nor that robbery was the motive. We think John Ashton was﻿—removed. Put out of the way!”

“Why, now?” asked Mr. Pawle.

“I’ll tell you,” replied Fosdick. “My friend Stephens, here, is a man of few words; he credits me with more talkativeness than he’ll lay claim to. So I’m to tell the tale. There mayn’t be much in it, and there may be a lot. We think there’s a big lot! But this is what it comes to: Ashton was a close man, a reserved man. However, one night, when the three of us were having a quiet cigar in a corner of the smoking saloon in the *Maraquibo*, he opened out to us a bit. We’d been talking about getting over to England﻿—we’d all three emigrated, you’ll understand, when we were very young﻿—and the talk ran on what we’d do. Fosdick and Stephens, d’ye see, were only on a visit﻿—which is just coming to an end, Mr. Pawle; we sail home in a day or two﻿—but Ashton was turning home for good. And he said to us, in a sort of burst of confidence, that he’d have plenty to do when he landed. He said that he was in possession﻿—sole possession﻿—of a most extraordinary secret, the revelation of which would affect one of the first families in England, and he was going to bring it out as soon as he’d got settled down in London. Well﻿—you may be surprised, but﻿—that’s all.”

“All you can tell?” exclaimed Mr. Pawle.

“All! But we can see plenty in it,” said Fosdick. “Our notion is that Ashton was murdered by somebody who didn’t want that secret to come out. Now, you see if events don’t prove we’re right.”

“Gentlemen,” said Mr. Pawle, “allow me to ask you a few questions.”

===Chinese===

“这么说，先生，您发现的可是一位非常正派的人的尸身，”福斯迪克正色道，“我们也想出一把力，把杀害他的凶手找出来。因为我们不认为是被指控的这位年轻人干的，也不认为图财是这案子的动机。我们认为，约翰·阿什顿是被人——除掉的。铲除掉了！”

“何以见得呢？”波尔先生问。

“我这就告诉您，”福斯迪克答道，“我这位朋友斯蒂芬斯，是个没多少言语的人；他认定我比他能说会道，讲这故事的差事便落到我头上。这故事里头也许没有多少东西，也许有很多。我们认为有很不少！归结起来是这样的：阿什顿是个嘴巴紧的人，一个不露心迹的人。可是有一天晚上，我们三个在‘马拉开波’号的吸烟室角落里安安静静抽雪茄，他向我们略略吐露了一点心曲。我们先是聊起到英国去的事——您知道，我们三个都是很小就移居过去的——接着话头便转到了英国之后要做些什么。您瞧，福斯迪克和斯蒂芬斯不过是一趟短访——这趟访问就要收尾了，波尔先生；过一两天我们就坐船回家——可阿什顿却是回英国长住，落叶归根。他一时兴起，像是推心置腹似的对我们说，他一上岸就有的是事要办。他说，他手中掌握着——独家掌握着——一个非同寻常的秘密，一旦揭出来，会牵动英国数一数二的豪门世家，等他在伦敦安顿下来，就要把它公之于世。这个——您也许会觉得意外，可是——就这么多。”

“能说的就这些？”波尔先生失声道。

“全部！不过这里头大有文章，”福斯迪克说，“我们的看法是：阿什顿是被人谋杀的，凶手不想让那个秘密见天日。往后您瞧着吧，事情必定证明我们说得不错。”

“两位先生，”波尔先生说，“容我问你们几个问题。”

===Original===

“Many as you please, sir,” assented Fosdick. “We’ll answer anything.”

“He didn’t tell you what the secret was?” asked Mr. Pawle.

“No. He said we’d know more about it in time,” replied Fosdick. “It would possibly lead to legal proceedings, he said﻿—in that case, it would be one of the most celebrated cases ever known.”

“And romantic,” added Stephens, speaking for the first time. “Romantic! That was the term he used.”

“And romantic﻿—quite so,” assented Fosdick. “Celebrated and romantic﻿—those were the words. But in any case, he said, whether it got to law matters or not, it couldn’t fail to be in the papers, and we should read all about it in due time.”

“And you know no more than that?” inquired Mr. Pawle.

===Chinese===

“您尽管问，先生，”福斯迪克一口应承，“问什么都照实回答。”

“他没告诉你们那秘密是什么？”波尔先生问。

“没有。他说时机一到，我们自然会知道得更多，”福斯迪克答道，“他说那件事说不定要闹到打官司——真到那一步，就会成为旷古未有的一桩名案。”

“还有传奇色彩，”斯蒂芬斯头一回开口，补了一句，“传奇！他用的就是这个词。”

“还有传奇色彩——正是如此，”福斯迪克表示同意，“轰动一时，又富传奇——他用的是这两个词。不过他还说，无论如何，就算闹不到公堂上去，报上也必定登载，到时候我们自会读到全部详情。”

“二位知道的就止于此？”波尔先生追问。

===Original===

“Nothing!” said Fosdick with decision.

Mr. Pawle looked at Viner as if to seek some inspiration. And Viner took up the work of examination.

“Do you know anything of Mr. Ashton’s movements since he came to London?” he asked.

“Next to nothing,” replied Fosdick. “Ashton left the *Maraquibo* at Naples, and came overland﻿—he wanted to put in a day or two in Rome and a day or two in Paris. We came round by sea to Tilbury. Then Stephens and I separated﻿—he went to see his people in Scotland, and I went to mine in Lancashire. We met﻿—Stephens and I﻿—in London here last week. And we saw Ashton for just a few minutes, down in the City.”

“Ah!” exclaimed Mr. Pawle. “You have seen him, then! Did anything happen?”

“You mean relating to what he’d told *us*?” said Fosdick. “Well, no more than I asked him sort of jokingly, how the secret was. And he said it was just about to come out, and we must watch the papers.”

===Chinese===

“再没有别的了！”福斯迪克说得斩钉截铁。

波尔先生望了望维纳，仿佛想从他那里讨一点灵感。于是盘问的差事便由维纳接了过去。

“阿什顿先生到伦敦之后的行踪，二位知道些什么？”他问。

“几乎一无所知，”福斯迪克答道，“阿什顿在那不勒斯（Naples）下的船，取道陆路过来——他想到罗马（Rome）盘桓一两天，再到巴黎（Paris）待一两天。我们则坐船绕海路，一直到蒂尔伯里（Tilbury）上岸。随后我和斯蒂芬斯便分了手——他去苏格兰（Scotland）探望亲人，我回兰开夏（Lancashire）探望我的。到上礼拜，我们俩——我和斯蒂芬斯——在伦敦这儿碰了头。还在伦敦城里同阿什顿见了一面，前后不过几分钟。”

“啊！”波尔先生失声道，“这么说二位见过他！可曾有什么事情？”

“您是问，同他跟咱们讲过的那件事有没有牵连？”福斯迪克说，“唔，也不过是我半开玩笑地问他，那秘密进行得怎么样了。他说眼看就要揭出来了，叫我们留意报纸。”

===Original===

“There was a remark he made,” observed Stephens. “He said it would be of just as much interest, perhaps of far more, to our Colonial papers as to the English.”

“Yes﻿—he said that,” agreed Fosdick. “He knew, you see, that we were just about setting off home.”

“He didn’t ask you to his house?” inquired Mr. Pawle.

“That was mentioned, but we couldn’t fix dates,” replied Fosdick. “However, we told him we were both coming over again on business, next year, and we’d come and see him then.”

Mr. Pawle spread out his hands with a gesture of helplessness.

“We’re as wise as ever,” he exclaimed.

===Chinese===

“他倒是说过一句，”斯蒂芬斯说，“他说这件事，咱们殖民地的报纸读起来会同样有趣，说不定还远甚于此，比英国报纸有过之而无不及。”

“是——他说过这话，”福斯迪克表示同意，“您想，他知道我们正要动身回家。”

“他没请二位上他家去？”波尔先生问。

“提过这事，只是日子定不下来，”福斯迪克答道，“不过我们告诉他，明年我们俩都要为生意再过来一趟，到时再去看他。”

波尔先生摊开双手，做出一副无可奈何的姿势。

“咱们还是同先前一样不明就里，”他失声道。

===Original===

“No,” said Fosdick emphatically, “wiser! The man had a secret, affecting powerful interests. Many a man’s been put away for having a secret.”

Mr. Pawle put his fingertips together and looked thoughtfully at his elder visitor.

“Well, there’s a good deal in that,” he said at last. “Now, while you’re here, perhaps you can tell me something else about Ashton. How long have you known him?”

“Ever since we were lads,” answered Fosdick readily. “He was a grown man, then, though. Stephens and I are about forty﻿—Ashton was sixty.”

“You’ve always known of him as a townsman of Melbourne?”

“That’s so. We were taken out there when we were about ten or twelve﻿—Ashton lived near where we settled down. He was a speculator in property﻿—made his money in buying and selling lots.”

===Chinese===

“不，”福斯迪克加重语气说，“比先前明白了！这人怀着一个秘密，牵动着有权有势者的利害。为了一个秘密送命的人，自古多着呢。”

波尔先生把指尖对拢，若有所思地望着年长的这位来客。

“唔，这话不无道理，”他终于说道，“正好，趁二位在这儿，也许还能给我讲讲阿什顿别的情形。你们认识他多久了？”

“从我们还是半大孩子的时候起，”福斯迪克爽快地答道，“不过那时候他已是成年人了。我和斯蒂芬斯四十上下——阿什顿六十岁。”

“在二位那里，他一直是墨尔本本地人？”

“正是。我们十岁到十二岁之间被带到那边——阿什顿就住在我们落脚之处的附近。他是做地产投机的——靠买进卖出地皮赚的钱。”

===Original===

“Was he well known?”

“Everybody knew Ashton.”

“Did you ever know of his having a friend named Wickham?” inquired Mr. Pawle with a side-glance at Viner. “Think carefully, now!”

But Fosdick shook his head, and Stephens shook his.

“Never heard the name,” said Fosdick.

“Did you ever hear Ashton mention the name?” asked Mr. Pawle.

===Chinese===

“他名气大吗？”

“人人都认识阿什顿。”

“可曾听说他有一位名叫威克姆的朋友？”波尔先生问着，朝维纳斜瞟了一眼，“现在可得仔细想想！”

但福斯迪克摇了摇头，斯蒂芬斯也摇了头。

“从没听过这名字，”福斯迪克说。

“可曾听阿什顿提起过这个名字？”波尔先生问。

===Original===

“Never!”

“Never heard him mention it on board ship﻿—when he was coming home?”

“No﻿—never!”

“Well,” said Mr. Pawle, “I happen to know that Ashton, some years ago, had a very particular friend named Wickham, out in Australia.”

A sudden light came into Fosdick’s keen grey-blue eyes.

“Ah,” he said. “I can tell how that may be. A good many years ago, when we were just familiar enough with Melbourne to know certain people in it, I remember that Ashton was away up country for some time﻿—as that cablegram says. Most likely he knew this Wickham then. Is that the Wickham mentioned there?”

===Chinese===

“从未！”

“坐船回国的时候，也没听他提起过？”

“没有——从来没有！”

“这个嘛，”波尔先生说，“我恰巧知道，阿什顿几年前在澳大利亚有过一位十分要好的朋友，名叫威克姆。”

福斯迪克敏锐的灰蓝色眼睛里倏地闪过一丝亮光。

“啊，”他说，“这我倒能说出个所以然来。好些年前，那时我们对墨尔本刚熟到能认得其中某些人的地步，我记得阿什顿曾到内陆去了些时日——正像那份电报上说的。多半他就是在那时候认识这位威克姆的。电报里提的就是那个威克姆吗？”

===Original===

“It is,” assented Mr. Pawle, “and I want to know who he was.”

“Glad to set any inquiries going for you when we get back,” said Fosdick. “We sail in two days.”

“Gentlemen,” answered Mr. Pawle gravely, “it takes, I believe, five or six weeks to reach Australia. By the time you get there, this unfortunate fellow Hyde, who’s charged with the murder of Ashton, on evidence that is quite sufficient to satisfy an average British jury, will probably have been tried, convicted and hanged. No! I’m afraid we must act at once if we’re to help him, as Mr. Viner here is very anxious to do. And there’s something you can do. The coroner’s inquest is to be held tomorrow. Go there and volunteer the evidence you’ve just told us! It mayn’t do a scrap of good﻿—but it will introduce an element of doubt into the case against Hyde, and that will benefit him.”

“Tomorrow?” said Fosdick. “We’ll do it. Give us the time and place. We’ll be there, Mr. Pawle. I see your point, sir﻿—to introduce the idea that there’s more to this than the police think.”

When the two callers had gone, Mr. Pawle turned to Viner.

“Now, my friend,” he said, “you’ve already sent your own solicitor to Hyde, haven’t you? Who is he, by the by?”

===Chinese===

“正是，”波尔先生点头称是，“我正想知道他是何等人物。”

“等我们回去，乐意替您把查访张罗起来，”福斯迪克说，“我们两天后就上船。”

“两位先生，”波尔先生神色郑重地答道，“据我所知，到澳大利亚得走五六个星期的水路。等二位赶到那边，这个不幸的海德——他被控谋杀了阿什顿，而单凭现有证据，已足以让英国寻常陪审团信服定罪——多半早已受审、定罪、绞决了。不成！要救他，恐怕就得立刻动手，而这位维纳先生正急着想救他。有一件事二位可以效力。验尸官（coroner）的死因研讯明天就要举行。二位到庭去，把方才告诉我们的话主动呈出来！这也许起不了一丝一毫的作用——却能给强加于海德的罪名掺进一点疑窦，对他总是有益的。”

“明天？”福斯迪克说，“成，我们去。把时间和地点告诉我们。我们准到，波尔先生。我明白您的用意，先生——要让大伙儿明白，这案子比警方所想的更有文章。”

两位来客走后，波尔先生转向维纳。

“我说，我的朋友，”他说，“你请的自己那位律师，已经去过海德那儿了吧？他是哪一位，顺便问一句？”

===Original===

“Felpham, of Chancery Lane,” replied Viner.

“Excellent man! Now,” said Mr. Pawle, “you go to Felpham and tell him what these two Australians have just told us, and say that in my opinion it will be well worth while, in his client’s interest, to develop their evidence for all it’s worth. That theory of Fosdick’s may have a great deal in it. And another thing﻿—Felpham must insist on Hyde being present at the inquest tomorrow and giving evidence. That, I say, must be done! Hyde must make his story public as soon as possible. He must be brought to the inquest. He’ll be warned by the coroner, of course, that he’s not bound to give any evidence at all, but he must go into the box and tell, on oath, all that he told you and Drillford. Now be off to Felpham and insist on all this being done.”

Viner went away to Chancery Lane more puzzled than ever. What was this secret affecting one of the first families in England, of which Ashton had told his two Melbourne friends? How was it, if legal proceedings were likely to arise out of it, that Ashton had not told Pawle about it? Was it possible that he had gone to some other solicitor? If so, why didn’t he come forward? And what, too, was this mystery about Miss Wickham and her father? Why, as Pawle had remarked, were there no papers or documents, concerning her to be found anywhere? Had she anything to do with the secret? It seemed to him that the confusion was becoming more confounded. But the first thing to do was to save Hyde. And he was relieved to see that Felpham jumped at Pawle’s suggestion.

“Good!” said Felpham. “Of course, I’ll have Hyde brought up at the inquest, and he shall tell his story. And we’ll save these Australian chaps until Hyde’s been in the box. I do wish Hyde himself could tell us more about that man whom he saw leaving the passage. Of course, that man is the actual murderer.”

“You think that?” asked Viner.

“Don’t doubt it for one moment﻿—and a cool, calculating hand, too!” declared Felpham. “A man who knew what he was doing. How long do you suppose it would take to strike the life out of a man and to snatch a few valuables from his clothing? Pooh! to a hand such as this evidently was, a minute. Then, he walks calmly away. And﻿—who is he? But﻿—we’re not doing badly.”

===Chinese===

“钱塞里巷（Chancery Lane）的佩尔弗，”维纳答道。

“是位好手！听我说，”波尔先生道，“你这就去找佩尔弗，把这两位澳大利亚人方才讲的话告诉他，并且说，依我看，为了他当事人的利益，把他们的证词充分发掘出来，大有必要。福斯迪克那套看法，兴许大有文章。还有一件事——佩尔弗必须坚持让海德明天出席研讯并出庭作证。这件事，我说，非办不可！海德必须尽快把他的经历公之于众。必须把他带到研讯上去。验尸官当然会提醒他，他完全有权不作任何证供，但他必须上证人席，把对你和德里尔福德讲过的那些话，一一宣誓讲出来。好了，快去找佩尔弗，务必把这些都办到。”

维纳往钱塞里巷去，心里比先前更加困惑。阿什顿向他的两位墨尔本朋友透露的，究竟是个什么秘密，竟能牵动英国数一数二的豪门？倘若那件事当真可能闹出官司来，阿什顿为什么不告诉波尔？会不会他去找了别的律师？若真如此，那人又为何不挺身而出？还有，威克姆小姐和她父亲这桩谜，又是怎么回事？为什么正如波尔所言，哪儿也找不到关于她的一纸文书、一份档件？她与那秘密有没有干系？在他看来，这团乱麻是越理越乱了。但眼下头一件要紧事，是救海德。见佩尔弗对波尔的建议一口应承、欣然照办，他松了一口气。

“好！”佩尔弗说，“我自然会提请让海德到研讯上露面，由他把事情原原本本讲出来。这两位澳大利亚朋友，我们留到海德作证之后再传。我倒真盼望，海德本人能对他看见离开那条过道的家伙，多讲出些名堂来。不用说，那家伙才是真凶。”

“您这样看？”维纳问。

“这一点我丝毫不疑心——而且是个沉着冷静、算计精到的角色！”佩尔弗断言道，“是个清楚自己在干什么的人。你说，把一条性命结果掉，再从死者衣裳里抓走几件值钱物件，要得了多大工夫？呸！对这样一只手来说，分明一分钟尽够了。然后，他便从容不迫地扬长而去。可是——他是谁？不过——咱们眼下干得不坏。”

===Original===

That, too, was Viner’s impression when he walked out of the coroner’s court next day. After having endured its close and sordid atmosphere for four long hours, he felt, more from intuition than from anything tangible, that things had gone well for Hyde. One fact was plain﻿—nothing more could be brought out against Hyde, either there, when the inquest was resumed a week later, or before the magistrate, or before a judge and jury. Every scrap of evidence against him was produced before the coroner: it was obvious that the police could rake up no more, unless indeed they could prove him to have hidden Ashton’s remaining valuables somewhere which was ostensibly an impossibility. And the evidence of Hyde himself had impressed the court. Two days’ rest and refreshment, even in a prison and on prison fare, had pulled him together, and he had given his evidence clearly and confidently. Viner had seen that people were impressed by it: they had been impressed, too, by the evidence volunteered by the two Australians. And when the coroner announced that he should adjourn the inquiry for a week, the folk who had crowded the court went away asking each other not if Hyde was guilty, but what was this secret of which Ashton had boasted the possession?

Drillford caught Viner up as he walked down the street and smiled grimly at him.

“Well, you’re doing your best for him, and no mistake, Mr. Viner,” he said. “He’s a lucky chap to have found such a friend!”

“He’s as innocent as I am,” answered Viner. “Look here; if you police want to do justice, why don’t you try to track the man whom Hyde has told of?”

“What clue have we?” exclaimed Drillford almost contemptuously. “A tall man in black clothes, muffled to his eyes! But I’ll tell you what, Mr. Viner,” he added with a grin: “as you’re so confident, why don’t you find him?”

===Chinese===

第二天走出验尸法庭时，维纳心里也是这般印象。在那窒闷寒酸的法庭气氛里熬了整整四个钟头之后，他感到——与其说出于什么实在的凭据，不如说出于直觉——事态对海德有利。有一点很清楚：对海德再也抖搂不出更多的东西了，无论是一周后研讯续开的时候，还是到了治安法官面前，还是到了法官和陪审团面前。不利于他的每一丝证据都已呈到验尸官跟前：警方显然再无新证可挖，除非他们能证明他把阿什顿余下的财物藏到了什么地方——而这一点，明摆着绝无可能。海德本人的证供，也给法庭留下了深刻印象。两天的休憩和调养，纵然身在狱中、吃着囚饭，也使他振作了起来，他作证时口齿清楚，神态从容。维纳看得出，众人对此深有触动：两位澳大利亚人主动提供的证词，同样打动了他们。等到验尸官宣布研讯延期一周，挤满法庭的人们散去时，彼此打听已不是海德究竟有罪无罪，而是阿什顿生前夸口掌握的那个秘密，究竟是什么？

维纳沿街走着，德里尔福德从后面赶了上来，冲他冷冷地一笑。

“嗬，你为他可真是尽心竭力，一点儿不假，维纳先生，”他说，“他能交上你这样的朋友，真是运气！”

“他同我一样清白，”维纳答道，“我说，你们警方若是真想主持公道，为什么不去追查海德供出的那个人？”

“我们有什么线索？”德里尔福德几乎是轻蔑地失声道，“一个穿黑衣裳的高个子，围巾一直裹到眼睛！不过我倒要请问，维纳先生，”他咧嘴一笑，又添了一句，“您既然这么有把握，干吗不亲手把他找出来？”

===Original===

“Perhaps I shall,” said Viner, quietly.

He meant what he said, and he was thinking deeply what might be done towards accomplishing his desires, when, later in the afternoon, Mr. Pawle rang him up on the telephone.

“Run down!” said Mr. Pawle cheerily. “There’s a new development!”

===Chinese===

“说不定我真会去找，”维纳不动声色地说。

他说这话是当真的。他正深深思量着怎样才能实现心愿，当天下午晚些时候，波尔先生打来了电话。

“快过来一趟！”波尔先生兴冲冲地说，“有新进展！”

## VIII / 八

===Original===

News from Arcadia

When Viner, half an hour later, walked into the waiting-room at Crawle, Pawle and Rattenbury’s, he was aware of a modestly attired young woman, evidently, from her dress and appearance, a country girl, who sat shyly turning over the pages of an illustrated paper. And as soon as he got into Pawle’s private room, the old solicitor jerked his thumb at the door by which Viner had entered, and smiled significantly.

“See that girl outside?” he asked. “She’s the reason of my ringing you up.”

“Yes?” said Viner. “But what﻿—why? More mystery?”

“Don’t know,” said Mr. Pawle. “I’ve kept her story till you came. She turned up here about three-quarters of an hour ago, and said that her grandmother, who keeps an inn at Marketstoke, in Buckinghamshire, had seen the paragraph in the papers this morning in which I asked if anybody could give any information about Mr. John Ashton’s movements, and had immediately sent her off to me with the message that a gentleman of that name stayed at their house for a few days some weeks since, and that if I would send somebody over there, she, the grandmother, could give some particulars about him. So that solves the question we were talking of at Markendale Square, as to where Ashton went during the absence Mrs. Killenhall told us of.”

“If this is the same Ashton,” suggested Viner.

===Chinese===

阿卡迪亚（Arcadia）来的消息

半个钟头之后，维纳走进克劳尔、波尔与拉滕伯里（Crawle, Pawle and Rattenbury）事务所的会客室，一眼便注意到一个衣着素净的年轻姑娘——瞧那穿着打扮，分明是个乡下姑娘，正怯生生地坐着，翻一本画报。他刚进波尔的私人办公室，老律师便朝他进来的那扇门翘起大拇指，意味深长地一笑。

“瞧见外头那个姑娘了吧？”他问。“我打电话请您来，就是为着她。”

“哦？”维纳说。“可是——这是为什么？又出了什么谜团？”

“不知道，”波尔先生说。“她的话我特意存着，专等您来再听。大约三刻钟之前，她上这儿来了，说她祖母在白金汉郡（Buckinghamshire）的马克特斯托克（Marketstoke）开着一爿客栈；今儿早上，祖母在报上看到我登的那段启事——问有谁能提供约翰·阿什顿先生行踪的线索——当即打发她赶来给我传话：几个星期之前，有位这个姓名的先生在她们店里住过几天，倘使我肯派人过去，她——做祖母的——可以讲出有关他的一些详情。这么一来，咱们在马肯代尔广场议论的那个问题——阿什顿在基伦霍尔夫人所说的那次外出期间去了哪里——也就有了着落。”

“前提是，这位阿什顿得就是同一位阿什顿，”维纳提了一句。

===Original===

“We’ll soon decide that,” answered Mr. Pawle as he touched the bell on his desk. “I purposely awaited your coming before hearing what this young woman had to tell. Now, my dear,” he continued as a clerk brought the girl into the room, “take a chair and tell me what your message is, more particularly. You’re from Marketstoke eh? Just so﻿—and your grandmother, who sent you here, keeps an inn there?”

“Yes, sir, the Ellingham Arms,” replied the girl as she sat down and glanced a little nervously at her two interviewers.

“To be sure. And your grandmother’s name is﻿—what?”

“Hannah Summers, sir.”

“Mrs. Hannah Summers. Grandfather living?”

“No, sir.”

===Chinese===

“这个很快便见分晓，”波尔先生答道，一面按了按桌上的铃。“我特意等您来了，再听这位年轻姑娘要说的话。好了，姑娘，”书记员把姑娘领进屋来，他接着往下说，“坐下吧，把你要带的口信再仔细说一遍。你是从马克特斯托克来的，嗯？这就对了——打发你来的那位祖母，在那儿开着一爿客栈？”

“是的，先生，埃灵厄姆纹章客栈（the Ellingham Arms），”姑娘答道，一面坐下，一面略带紧张地朝问话的两位望了望。

“这就对了。你祖母的名字是——怎么称呼？”

“汉娜·萨默斯（Hannah Summers），先生。”

“汉娜·萨默斯太太。祖父还在世么？”

“不在了，先生。”

===Original===

“Very well﻿—Mrs. Hannah Summers, landlady at the Ellingham Arms, Marketstoke, in Buckinghamshire. Now then﻿—but what’s your name, my dear?”

“Lucy Summers, sir.”

“Very pretty name, I’m sure! Well, and what’s the message your grandmother sent me? I want this gentleman to hear it.”

“Grandmother wished me to say, sir, that we read the piece in the paper this morning asking if anybody could give you any news about a Mr. John Ashton, and that as we had a gentleman of that name staying with us for three or four days some weeks since, she sent me to tell you, and to say that if you would send somebody down to see her, she could give some information about him.”

“Very clearly put, my dear﻿—much obliged to you,” said Mr. Pawle. “Now, I suppose you were at the Ellingham Arms when this Mr. Ashton came there?”

“Oh, yes, sir; I live there!”

===Chinese===

“很好——白金汉郡马克特斯托克，埃灵厄姆纹章客栈店主汉娜·萨默斯太太。那么——姑娘，你叫什么名字？”

“露西·萨默斯（Lucy Summers），先生。”

“好一个好听的名字，错不了！那么，你祖母叫我带的口信是什么来着？我要让这位先生也听一听。”

“祖母要我说的是，先生：今儿早上，我们在报上看到一段文字，问有谁能把一位约翰·阿什顿先生的消息告诉您；因为几个星期之前，曾有位这个姓名的先生在我们店里住了三四天，她就打发我来告诉您，还说，倘若您肯派人下去见她，她可以把他的情形讲出一些来。”

“说得清清楚楚，姑娘——多谢你了，”波尔先生说。“那么，这位阿什顿先生到店里来的时候，你想必正在埃灵厄姆纹章客栈吧？”

“哦，在的，先生；我就住在那儿！”

===Original===

“To be sure! Now, what sort of man was he﻿—in appearance?”

“A tall, big gentleman, sir, with a beard, going a little grey. He was wearing a blue serge suit.”

Mr. Pawle nodded at Viner.

“Seems like our man,” he remarked. “Now,” he went on, turning again to Lucy Summers, “you say he stayed there three or four days. What did he do with himself while he was there?”

“He spent a good deal of time about the church, sir,” answered the girl, “and he was at Ellingham Park a good deal﻿—”

“Whose place is that?” interrupted Mr. Pawle.

===Chinese===

“可不是嘛！那么，他是个什么样的人——说说模样？”

“一位高高大大、身量魁伟的先生，先生，蓄着胡须，颜色有点花白了。他当时穿着一身蓝色哗叽衣服。”

波尔先生朝维纳点了点头。

“看样子就是咱们说的那位，”他说。“那么，”他接着说，又转向露西·萨默斯，“你说他在那儿住了三四天。那几天里，他都是怎么打发的？”

“他把好些工夫都花在教堂那一带，先生，”姑娘答道，“埃灵厄姆庄园（Ellingham Park）他也常去——”

“那是谁家的宅子？”波尔先生插嘴问。

===Original===

“Lord Ellingham’s, sir.”

“Do you mean that Mr. Ashton called on Lord Ellingham, or what?”

“No, sir, because Lord Ellingham wasn’t there﻿—he scarcely ever is there,” replied Lucy Summers. “I mean that Mr. Ashton went into the park a good deal and looked over the house﻿—a good many people come to see Ellingham Park, sir.”

“Well, and what else?” asked Mr. Pawle. “Did he go to see people in the town at all?”

“I don’t know, sir﻿—but he was out most of the day. And at night he talked a great deal with my grandmother, in her sitting-room, I think,” added the girl with a glance which took in both listeners. “I think that’s what she wants to tell about. She would have come here herself, but she’s over seventy and doesn’t like travelling.”

Mr. Pawle turned to Viner.

===Chinese===

“埃灵厄姆勋爵（Lord Ellingham）的，先生。”

“你是说，阿什顿先生登门拜会了埃灵厄姆勋爵，还是怎么的？”

“不是，先生，因为埃灵厄姆勋爵不在家——他难得在家，”露西·萨默斯答道。“我是说，阿什顿先生常到园子里去，把那宅子瞧了个仔细——好些人专程来看埃灵厄姆庄园呢，先生。”

“唔，还有呢？”波尔先生问。“镇上的人家，他可曾去拜望过？”

“这我说不上，先生——只是他白天多半在外头。到了夜里，他同我祖母谈得很多，我想是在她的起居室里，”姑娘又添了一句，眼风把两位听客都扫了一遍。“我想，她要讲的正是这个。她本要亲自来的，只是她年过七旬，不爱出远门。”

波尔先生转向维纳。

===Original===

“Now we know where we are,” he said. “There’s no doubt that this is our Ashton, and that Mrs. Summers has something she can tell about him. Viner, I suggest that you and I go down to Marketstoke this afternoon. You’ve accommodations for a couple of gentlemen, I suppose, my dear?” he added, turning to the girl. “Couple of nice bedrooms and a bit of dinner, eh?”

“Oh, yes sir!” replied Lucy Summers. “We constantly have gentlemen there, sir.”

“Very well,” said Mr. Pawle. “Now, then, you run away home to Marketstoke, my dear, and tell your grandmother that I’m very much obliged to her, and that I am coming down this evening, with this gentleman, Mr. Viner, and that we shall be obliged if she’ll have a nice, plain, well-cooked dinner ready for us at half-past seven. We shall come in my motorcar﻿—you can put that up for the night, and my driver too? Very well﻿—that’s settled. Now, come along, and one of my clerks shall get you a cab to your station. Great Central, isn’t it? All right﻿—mind you get yourself a cup of tea before going home.”

“Viner,” Pawle continued when he had taken the girl into the outer office, “we can easily run down to Marketstoke in under two hours. I’ll call for you at your house at half-past five. That’ll give us time to wash away the dirt before our dinner. And then﻿—we’ll hear what this old lady has to tell.”

Viner, who was musing somewhat vaguely over these curious developments, looked at Mr. Pawle as if in speculation about his evident optimism.

“You think we shall hear something worth hearing?” he asked.

===Chinese===

“这下咱们心里有数了，”他说。“毫无疑问，这就是咱们说的阿什顿，萨默斯太太也确有关于他的话可讲。维纳，我提议，今天下午你我两人就下去一趟，到马克特斯托克去。你们店里接待得了两位先生吧，姑娘？”他转身向姑娘添了一句。“两间像样的卧室，再备一顿晚饭，行么？”

“哦，行的，先生！”露西·萨默斯答道。“我们店里是常接待先生们的，先生。”

“很好，”波尔先生说。“那么，姑娘，你就快回马克特斯托克去吧，告诉你祖母，我十分感激她；就说今天傍晚，我同这位先生——维纳先生——一道下去，倘若她肯在七点半给我们备下一顿干净可口、烧得地道的家常晚饭，我们就领她的情了。我们坐我的汽车去——车子连同我的司机，能在你们店里过一夜吧？很好——就这么定了。来吧，我叫一个书记员给你雇车上车站去。是大中央车站（Great Central）吧？好——回去之前，可别忘了给自己买杯茶喝。”

“维纳，”把姑娘送进外间办公室之后，波尔接着说，“到马克特斯托克去，用不了两个钟头，宽宽裕裕。五点半我到您府上接您。这样，吃晚饭之前还来得及洗去一路风尘。然后嘛——就听这位老太太有什么话要说了。”

维纳正为这些离奇的进展想得有些出神，他望着波尔先生，仿佛在掂量他这股子显而易见的乐观劲儿。

“您觉得咱们会听到些值得一听的东西？”他问。

===Original===

“I should say we probably shall,” replied Mr. Pawle. “Put things together. Ashton goes away﻿—as soon as he’s got settled down in Markendale Square﻿—on a somewhat mysterious journey. Now we hear that he had a secret. Perhaps something relating to that secret is mixed up with his visit to Marketstoke. Depend upon it, an old woman of over seventy﻿—especially a landlady of a country-town inn, whose wits are presumably pretty sharp﻿—wouldn’t send for me unless she’d something to tell. Before midnight, my dear sir, we may have learnt a good deal.”

Viner picked up his hat.

“I’ll be ready for you at half-past five,” he said. Then, halfway to the door, he turned with a question: “By the by,” he added, “you wouldn’t like me to tell the two ladies that we’ve found out where Ashton went when he was away?”

“I think not until we’ve found out why he went away,” answered the old lawyer with a significant smile. “We may draw the covert blank, you know, after all. When we’ve some definite news﻿—”

Viner nodded, went out, into the afternoon calm of Bedford Row. As he walked up it, staring mechanically at the old-fashioned red brick fronts, he wondered how many curious secrets had been talked over and perhaps unravelled in the numerous legal sanctuaries approached through those open doorways. Were there often as strange ones as that upon which he had so unexpectedly stumbled? And when they first came into the arena of thought and speculation did they arouse as much perplexity and mental exercise as was now being set up in him? Did every secret, too, possibly endanger a man’s life as his old schoolfellow’s was being endangered? He had no particular affection or friendship for Langton Hyde, of whom, indeed, he had known very little at school, but he had an absolute conviction that he was innocent of murder, and that conviction had already aroused in him a passionate determination to outwit the police. He had been quick to see through Drillford’s plans. There was a case, a strong prima facie case against Hyde, and the police would work it up for all they were worth. Failing proofs in other directions, failing the discovery of the real murderer, how was that case going to be upset? And was it likely that he and Pawle were going to find any really important evidence in an obscure Buckinghamshire market-town?

He jumped into a cab at the top of Bedford Row and hastened back to Markendale Square to pack a bag and prepare for his journey. Miss Penkridge called to him from the drawing-room as he was running upstairs; he turned into the room to find her in company with two ladies﻿—dismal, pathetic figures in very plain and obviously countrified garments, both in tears and evident great distress, who, as Viner walked in, rose from their chairs and gazed at him sadly and wistfully. They reminded him at once of the type of spinster found in quiet, unpretentious cottages in out-of-the-way villages﻿—the neither young nor old women, who live on circumscribed means and are painfully shy of the rude world outside. And before either he or Miss Penkridge could speak, the elder of the two broke into an eager exclamation.

===Chinese===

“我看多半会的，”波尔先生答道。“把各桩事归拢到一块儿想想。阿什顿刚在马肯代尔广场安顿下来，便出了趟多少带点神秘色彩的远门。如今我们又听说，他藏着一桩秘密。没准儿，同那秘密有关的什么，正缠在他这趟马克特斯托克之行里。你只管信我的话：一位年过七旬的老太太——尤其是乡镇客栈的老板娘，那副心思想必磨得相当锐利——若不是真有什么要讲的，断不会打发人来叫我。不到午夜，亲爱的先生，咱们兴许已经打探出不少东西了。”

维纳拿起帽子。

“五点半我恭候您，”他说。随后，走到离门口一半的地方，他又回身，带着一个问题：“对了，”他补上一句，“咱们已经查明阿什顿出门期间去了哪儿——这话，您是不乐意叫我去告诉那两位女士吧？”

“我想，还是等咱们查明了他为什么出门，再说不迟，”老律师答道，意味深长地一笑。“您知道，说不定到头来，咱们扑了个空呢。等有了确切的消息——”

维纳点点头，出了门，走进午后天光安静的贝德福德罗。他沿街信步而上，两眼机械地盯着那些老式的红砖门面，心里不禁琢磨：从这些敞开的门洞走进去的这一座座法律殿堂里，曾经有多少离奇的秘密被商议过，说不定还被解开过？像他这般不期而然撞上的稀罕案子，从前可也常有么？这些案子当初刚一进入思量与揣测的场子时，可也曾像此刻搅扰着他这样，激起那么多的惶惑与苦思？再者，是不是每一桩秘密，都可能叫人性命涉险，就像他这位老同学眼下的处境？对兰顿·海德，他并没有什么特别的情分，也谈不上交情——念书的时候，他对海德原本就知道得很少——但他抱定一个不容动摇的信念：海德决非杀人凶手；这信念已在他胸中燃起一股炽烈的决心，定要斗过警方。德里尔福德的盘算，他早一眼看穿。对付海德，警方手里有一桩案子，一桩表面证据相当硬扎的案子，他们是必要竭尽所能把它经营到底的。旁的方向若拿不出凭证，真凶若无从查获，那桩案子又从何推翻？而他同波尔，当真能指望在一座偏僻的白金汉郡集镇里，寻出什么真正要紧的证据么？

他在贝德福德罗顶头跳上一辆马车，赶回马肯代尔广场去收拾行囊、预备旅程。他正往楼上跑，彭克里奇小姐从客厅里唤他；他转身进屋，只见她正陪着两位女士——一对愁苦可怜的人物，衣裳素得很，一望便知是乡下装束，两人都泪痕未干，分明正陷于极大的哀伤。维纳一进门，她们便从椅子里站起身，凄凄切切、巴巴望望地瞧着他。她们立时叫他想起僻远村坊里那些安静而不起眼的小屋中的一类老小姐——那些不老也不小的妇人，靠着一星半点的进项过日子，对外面那个粗野世界怕得要命。他和彭克里奇小姐谁都还没来得及开口，年长的那位已经急切地喊出声来：

===Original===

“Oh, Mr. Viner, we are Langton’s sisters! And we are so grateful to you﻿—and oh, do you think you can save him?”

Viner was quick to seize the situation. He said a soothing word or two, begged his visitors to sit down again, and whispered to Miss Penkridge to ring for tea.

“You have come to town today?” he asked.

“We left home very, very early this morning,” replied the elder sister. “We learned this dreadful news last night in the evening paper. We came away at four o’clock this morning﻿—we live in Durham, Mr. Viner﻿—and we have been to Mr. Felpham’s office this afternoon. He told us how kind you had been in engaging his services for our unfortunate brother, and we came to thank you. But oh, do you think there is any chance for him?”

“Every chance!” declared Viner, pretending more conviction than he felt. “Don’t let yourselves be cast down. We’ll move heaven and earth to prove that he’s wrongly accused. I gather﻿—if you don’t mind my asking﻿—that your brother has been out of touch with you for some time?”

===Chinese===

“哦，维纳先生，我们是兰顿的姐姐呀！我们心里多么感激您——哦，您说，您救得了他么？”

维纳立即会意。他说了一两句宽慰的话，请两位来客重新坐下，又低声央彭克里奇小姐打铃叫茶。

“你们是今天进的城？”他问。

“今天清早，我们动身动得极早极早，”姐姐答道。“昨晚的晚报把这条可怕的消息带给了我们。今儿凌晨四点我们就出了门——我们家在达勒姆（Durham），维纳先生——今天下午，我们又去了费尔法姆（Felpham）先生的事务所。他告诉我们，承您好意，为我们那不幸的兄弟延请了他；我们是特意来道谢的。可是哦，您说，他还有指望么？”

“有的是指望！”维纳一口断言，那口气比心里的把握还足上几分。“千万别自伤自损。我们上天入地，也要证明他是冤枉的。我猜——恕我冒昧动问——你们这位兄弟，同你们断绝音信，有些日子了吧？”

===Original===

The two sisters exchanged mournful glances.

“We had not heard anything of Langton for some years,” replied the elder. “He is much﻿—much younger than ourselves, and perhaps we are too staid and old-fashioned for him. But if we had known that he was in want! Oh, dear me, we are not at all well-to-do, Mr. Viner, but we would have sacrificed anything. Mr. Felpham says that we shall be allowed to visit him﻿—he is going to arrange for us to do so. And of course we must remain in London until this terrible business is over﻿—we came prepared for that.”

“Prepared for that!” repeated the other sister, who seemed to be a fainter replica of the elder. “Yes, prepared, of course, Mr. Viner.”

“Now that we have found Langton, though in such painful circumstances,” said the first speaker, “we must stand by him. We must find some quiet lodging, and settle down to help. We cannot let all the burden fall on you, Mr. Viner.”

Viner glanced at Miss Penkridge. They were quick to understand each other, these two, and he knew at once that Miss Penkridge saw what was in his mind.

===Chinese===

姊妹俩交换了一个哀伤的眼色。

“我们有好些年没有兰顿的音信了，”姐姐答道。“他比我们年轻得多——年轻得多；也许是我们太古板、太守旧，配不上他。可倘若我们早知道他日子过得窘迫！哦，天哪，维纳先生，我们实在算不得宽裕，可我们什么都舍得豁出去。费尔法姆先生说，会准我们探望他的——他正要去替我们安排。当然，这桩可怕的事情不了结，我们就得留在伦敦——我们出门时，就是有备而来的。”

“是‘有备而来’！”另一位应声道——她像是姐姐的一个淡些的影子。“是的，有备而来，自然，维纳先生。”

“如今我们总算找着了兰顿——虽是在这样的惨境里，”先开口的那位说。“我们总得守着他。得寻一处安静的寓所住下，安顿下来帮着出力。这副担子，总不能全压在您一个人身上，维纳先生。”

维纳朝彭克里奇小姐望了一眼。这两个人彼此一点就透，他立刻明白，彭克里奇小姐已看出他心里的打算。

===Original===

“You must stay with us,” he said, turning to the two mournful figures. “We have any amount of room in this house, and we shall be only too glad﻿—”

“Oh, but that is too﻿—” began both ladies.

“I insist,” said Viner, with a smile.

“We both insist!” echoed Miss Penkridge. “We are both given to having our own way, too; so say no more about it. We are all in the same boat just now, and its name is *Mystery*, and we must pull together until we’re in harbour.”

“Listen!” said Viner. “I have to go away tonight, on a matter closely connected with this affair. Let me leave you in my aunt’s charge, and tomorrow I may be able to give you some cheering news. You’ll be much more comfortable here than in any lodgings or hotel and﻿—and I should like to do something for Hyde; we’re old schoolfellows, you know.”

===Chinese===

“你们务必住在我们这儿，”他转身向那两位哀伤的人说。“这宅子里的屋子多的是，我们是求之不得——”

“哦，那怎么使得——”两位女士一齐开口。

“我可是不由分说的，”维纳笑着说。

“我们两个都是不由分说的！”彭克里奇小姐跟着说。“我们俩的脾气，也都惯于自行其是，所以这话休再提起。如今咱们全坐在同一条船上，船名就叫*“谜”*，不进港湾，就得一齐划桨。”

“听我说！”维纳道。“今晚我得出趟门，为的是同这案子关系极紧的一件事。你们就托给我姨妈照应；明天，我兴许就能带给你们些叫人宽慰的消息。住在这儿，比住什么旅舍客店都舒坦，再说——再说，我也乐意为海德尽一份力；要知道，我们是老同学。”

===Original===

Then he escaped from the room and made ready for his journey; and at half-past five came Mr. Pawle in his private car and carried him off into the dark. And hour and a half later the car rolled smoothly into the main street of a quiet, wholly Arcadian little town, and pulled up before an old-fashioned many-gabled house over the door of which was set up one of those ancient signs which, in such places, display the coat of arms of the lord of the manor. Viner had just time to glance around him, and in a clear, starlit evening, to see the high tower of a church, the timbered fronts of old houses, and many a tall, venerable tree, before following Mr. Pawle into a stone hall filled with dark oak cabinets and bright with old brass and pewter, on the open hearth of which burnt a fine and cheery fire of logs.

“Excellent!” muttered the old lawyer as he began to take off his multitudinous wraps. “A real bit of the real old England! Viner, if the dinner is as good as this promises, I shall be glad we’ve come, whatever the occasion.”

“Here’s the landlady, I suppose,” said Viner as a door opened.

A tall, silver-haired old woman, surprisingly active and vivacious in spite of her evident age, came forward with a polite, old-fashioned bow. She wore a silk gown and a silk apron and a smart cap, and her still bright eyes took in the two visitors at a glance.

“Your servant, gentlemen,” she said. “Your rooms are ready, and dinner will be ready, too, when you are. This way, if you please.”

===Chinese===

随后他便从屋里脱身出来，收拾行装，预备上路；五点半，波尔先生坐着自己的汽车来了，载着他驶入夜色。一个半钟头之后，汽车平稳地驶进一座小镇的主街——那镇子安安静静，一派十足的阿卡迪亚风味——停在一所老式的、山墙层叠的宅子前；门楣上悬着那一类古旧的招牌，在这种地方，招牌上画的是庄园领主的纹章。维纳只来得及朝四下里望一眼：晴明的星夜之下，望见教堂的高塔、老屋的木骨墙面，还有一株株高大苍然的古树，便跟着波尔先生走进一座石砌大厅；厅里陈着深色的橡木柜橱，古铜和锡器擦得锃亮，敞开的壁炉里，一炉柴火烧得又旺又欢。

“好极了！”老律师一面动手解下他层层裹缠的围巾大衣，一面喃喃赞道。“这才是真正的老英格兰的一角真味！维纳，倘若晚饭也如这屋里气象一般地道，那么不管为着什么事来的，我都要庆幸此行。”

“这位想必就是老板娘了，”一扇门开处，维纳说道。

一位身量挺高、银发皓首的老妇人走上前来，行了一个客气而老派的礼；虽则高龄显然，却出人意料地健朗而有神采。她身穿绸衫，系一条绸围裙，戴一顶俏净的便帽，一双依然明亮的眼睛一瞥之间，便把两位来客瞧了个全。

“给两位请安了，先生们，”她说。“房间已经备妥；晚饭呢，只等二位一进餐厅就得。这边请。”

===Original===

“A very fine old house this, ma’am,” observed Mr. Pawle as they followed her up a curious staircase, all nooks and corners. “And you have, no doubt, been long in it?”

“Born in it, sir,” said the landlady, with a laugh. “Our family﻿—on one side﻿—has been here two hundred years. This is your room, sir﻿—this is your friend’s.” She paused, and with a significant look, pointed to another door. “That,” she said, “is the room which Mr. Ashton had when he was here.”

“Ah! We are very anxious to know what you can tell us about him, ma’am,” said Mr. Pawle.

Mrs. Summers paused, and again glanced significantly at her visitors.

“I wish I knew the meaning of what I shall tell you,” she answered.

===Chinese===

“好一所漂亮的老宅子，夫人，”波尔先生一面随她登上一道别致的楼梯——到处是犄角旮旯——一面说道。“您在这宅子里，想必住得久了吧？”

“我就生在这宅子里，先生，”老板娘笑着说。“我们这一家族——其中一支——在这儿住了两百年了。这间是您的房，先生——这间是您朋友的。”她顿了顿，意味深长地指了指另一扇门。“那间，”她说，“就是阿什顿先生来时住过的屋子。”

“啊！您能讲些什么他的事，我们正急着想听呢，夫人，”波尔先生说。

萨默斯太太顿了顿，又意味深长地瞧了瞧两位来客。

“但愿我自己能明白，我要讲的这一切究竟是什么意思，”她答道。

## IX / 九

===Original===

Looking Backward

On the principle that business should never be discussed when one is dining, Mr. Pawle made no reference during dinner to the matter which had brought Viner and himself to the Ellingham Arms. He devoted all his attention and energies to the pleasures of the table; he praised the grilled soles and roast mutton and grew enthusiastic over some old Burgundy which Mrs. Summers strongly recommended. But when dinner was over and he had drunk a glass or two of old port, his eyes began to turn toward the door of the quaint little parlour in which he and Viner had been installed, and to which the landlady had promised to come.

“I confess I’m unusually curious about what we’re going to hear, Viner,” he said, as he drew out a well-filled cigar-case. “There’s an atmosphere of mystery about our presence and our surroundings that’s like an apéritif to an already hungry man. Ashton, poor fellow, comes over to this quiet, out-of-the-way place; why, we don’t know; what he does here we don’t know, yet﻿—but all the circumstances, up to now, seem to point to secrecy, if not to absolute romance and adventure.”

“Is it going, after all, to clear up the mystery of his death?” asked Viner. “That’s what concerns me﻿—I’m afraid I’m a bit indifferent to the rest of it. What particular romance, do you think, could be attached to the mere fact that Ashton paid a three days’ visit to Marketstoke?”

Mr. Pawle drew out a well-filled cigar-case.

“In my profession,” he answered, “we hear a great deal more of romance than most folk could imagine. Now, here’s a man who returns to this country from a long residence in Australia. The first thing he does, after getting settled down in London, is to visit Marketstoke. Why Marketstoke? Marketstoke is an obscure place﻿—there are at least five or six towns in this very county that are better known. Again, I say﻿—why Marketstoke? And why this, the very first place in England? For what reason? Now, as a lawyer, a reason does suggest itself to me; I’ve been thinking about it ever since that rosy-cheeked lass called at my office this afternoon. What does the man who’s been away from his native land for the best part of his life do, as a rule, when at last he sets foot on it again﻿—eh?”

===Chinese===

回望往昔

本着进餐时绝不谈正事的原则，波尔先生在饭桌上对促使他和维纳跑到埃灵厄姆纹章客栈（Ellingham Arms）来的那桩事只字不提。他把全副心思和精力都放在了餐桌上的享受：他夸赞烤龙利鱼和烤羊肉，对萨默斯太太（Mrs. Summers）极力推荐的陈年勃艮第更是满口称赏。可等晚餐用毕，又喝下一两杯陈年波特酒，他的目光便开始频频转向那间古雅小客厅的房门——他和维纳就被安置在这间客厅里，女店主也答应过要到这里来。

“我承认，对咱们将要听到的事，我好奇得有些异乎寻常，维纳，”他一边掏出一只装得满满的雪茄烟盒，一边说道，“我们的到来和我们身处的环境，四处弥漫着一股神秘气氛，对一个早已饥肠辘辘的人来说，这就好比一杯开胃酒。阿什顿，可怜的家伙，跑到这么个安静偏僻的地方来；为什么来，我们不知道；他在这儿做什么，我们也不知道——可迄今为止，种种情形似乎都指向隐秘，甚至指向不折不扣的罗曼史和奇遇。”

“这究竟能不能解开他的死亡之谜呢？”维纳问，“我关心的只有这个——恐怕对其余种种，我都有些漠不关心了。阿什顿到马克特斯托克（Marketstoke）来做客三天，就这么一件事，你说能附着什么样的罗曼史？”

波尔先生掏出一只装得满满的雪茄烟盒。

“干我们这一行的，”他答道，“听说的罗曼史，可比常人想象的多得多。你瞧，有这么一个人，在澳大利亚长年侨居之后回到英国。他在伦敦安顿下来，头一件事就是跑一趟马克特斯托克。为什么是马克特斯托克？马克特斯托克是个无名之地——光是这个郡里，就至少有五六个比它出名的小镇。我再问一遍——为什么是马克特斯托克？而且为什么这竟是他回到英国后的头一站？图的是什么？喏，以一个律师的眼光，我倒真想到一个缘由；打今天下午那个红脸蛋的姑娘到我事务所来过之后，我就一直在琢磨这件事。一个人背井离乡大半辈子，等终于再度踏上故土的时候，照例会做什么——嗯？”

===Original===

“I’m not greatly experienced,” replied Viner, smiling at the old solicitor’s professional enthusiasm. “What does he do﻿—usually?”

“Makes his way as soon as possible to his native place!” exclaimed Mr. Pawle, with an expressive flourish of his cigar. “That, usually, is the first thing he thinks of. You’re not old enough to remember the circumstances, my boy, but I have, of course, a very distinct recollection of the Tichborne affair in the early seventies. Now, if you ever read the evidence in that cause célèbre, you’ll remember that the claimant, Orton, on arriving in England, posing as the missing heir, Sir Roger Tichborne, did a certain thing, the evidence of which, I can assure you, was not lost on the jury before whom he eventually came.
